Relative 𝔸 1 \mathbb{A}^{1} -Contractibility of Smooth Schemes And Exotic Motivic Spheres


Abstract




See pages 1,2 of
coverpage.pdf

Abstract

One of the emerging problems in algebraic geometry is to characterize the affine
n
-space
\mathbb{A}^{n}
among smooth affine schemes up to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility: a property that makes motivic spaces be homotopy equivalent to a field
\operatorname{Spec}k
in the motivic homotopy theory. Recent efforts show that this characterization holds in dimensions
n<3
over certain fields. In our work, we extend this observation to "reasonable" arbitrary base schemes in relative dimensions
n<3
, exploiting the Zariski local triviality and the triviality of the relative canonical sheaf. From dimensions
n\geq 3
, the existence of smooth “exotic” affine schemes, those that are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible but not isomorphic to the affine
n
-space, has already been established. A well-studied family constitutes the Koras-Russell threefolds
\mathcal{K}
and their generalized prototypes
\mathcal{X}_{n}
in higher dimensions, whose
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility has been so far proven over fields of characteristic zero. In this direction, we extend the relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}
over a Noetherian base scheme in arbitrary dimensions.

Furthermore, using these prototypes, we study the existence of “exotic spheres” -
n
-dimensional smooth schemes that are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic, but not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
, in motivic homotopy theory. This result can be seen as the "compact" analog of the study of exotic affine schemes. Our main result shows that in all dimensions
n\geq 4
, the quasi-affine varieties
\mathcal{X}_{n}\backslash\{\bullet\}
give a model for the exotic motivic spheres over infinite perfect fields. The novelty here is that these constitute the first family of examples of smooth motivic spheres of dimension
n
, which are not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
.

Resumé

L’un des problèmes émergents en géométrie algébrique consiste à caractériser l’esp-ace affine
\mathbb{A}^{n}
parmi les schémas affines lisses à contractibilité
\mathbb{A}^{1}
près : une propriété qui rend les espaces motiviques homotopiquement équivalents à un corps
\operatorname{Spec}k
en homotopie motivique. Des travaux récents montrent que cette caractérisation est valable en dimension
n<3
, sur certains corps. Dans nos travaux, nous étendons cette observation à une large famille de schémas de base « raisonnables » en dimension relatives
n<3
, en exploitant la trivialité locale de Zariski et la trivialité du faisceau canonique relatif. À partir de la dimensions
n\geq 3
, l’existence d’espaces affines « exotiques », c’est-à-dire des schémas affines lisses qui sont
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibles mais qui ne sont pas isomorphes à l’espace affine
\mathbb{A}^{n}
, a déjà été établie. Une famille bien étudiée est celle des 3-variétés de Koras-Russell
\mathcal{K}
et leurs prototypes généralisés
\mathcal{X}_{n}
en dimensions supérieures, dont la
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibilité a été prouvée jusqu’à présent sur des corps de caractéristique nulle. Dans cette direction, nous étendons ces résultats en établissant la
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibilité relative de
\mathcal{K}
et
\mathcal{X}_{n}
sur un schéma de base noethérien en dimension arbitraire.

De plus, en utilisant ces prototypes, nous étudions l’existence de « sphères exotiques » en homotopie motivique - des schémas lisses de dimension
n
qui sont
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopes mais non isomorphes à
\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
-. Ce résultat peut être considéré comme l’analogue « compact » de l’étude des espaces affines exotiques. Notre résultat principal montre qu’en dimension
n\geq 4
, les variétés quasi-affines
\mathcal{X}_{n}\backslash\{\bullet\}
fournissent un modèle pour les sphères motiviques exotiques sur des corps parfaits infinis. La nouveauté ici est que celles-ci constituent la première famille d’exemples de sphères motiviques lisses de dimension
n
qui ne sont pas isomorphes à
\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
.

Abstract

Uno dei problemi emergenti nella geometria algebrica è quello di caratterizzare lo spazio affine
n
-dimensionale
\mathbb{A}^{n}
tra schemi affini regolari a meno di
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contrazione: una proprietà che rende gli spazi motivici omotopicamente equivalenti a un campo
\operatorname{Spec}k
nella teoria dell’omotopia motivica. Recenti studi dimostrano che questa caratterizzazione vale in dimensioni
n<3
su determinati campi. Nel nostro lavoro, estendiamo questa osservazione a schemi “ragionevoli” di base arbitraria, in dimensioni relative
n<3
, sfruttando la trivialità locale di Zariski e la trivialità del fascio canonico relativo. A partire dalle dimensioni
n\geq 3
, è già stata dimostrata l’esistenza di schemi affini regolari “esotici”, in altre parole, schemi che sono
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contraibili ma non isomorfi allo spazio affine
n
-dimensionale. Una famiglia ben studiata è costituita dalle tre-varietà
\mathcal{K}
di Koras-Russell e dai loro prototipi generalizzati
\mathcal{X}_{n}
in dimensioni superiori, la cui
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contraibilità è stata finora dimostrata su campi di caratteristica zero. In questa direzione, estendiamo la
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contraibilità relativa di
\mathcal{K}
e
\mathcal{X}_{n}
su uno schema di base Noetheriano in dimensioni arbitrarie.

Inoltre, utilizzando questi prototipi, studiamo l’esistenza di “sfere esotiche” - schemi lisci
n
-dimensionali che sono
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-omotopici, ma non isomorfi ad
\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
nella teoria dell’omotopia motivica. Questo risultato può essere visto come l’analogo “compatto” dello studio degli schemi affini esotici. Il nostro risultato principale mostra che in tutte le dimensioni
n\geq 4
, le varietà quasi affini
\mathcal{X}_{n}\backslash\{\bullet\}
forniscono un modello per le sfere motiviche esotiche su campi perfetti infiniti. Queste costituiscono la prima famiglia nota di esempi di sfere motiviche lisce di dimensione
n
, che non sono isomorfe a
\mathbb{A}^{n}\lx@nobreakspace\backslash\lx@nobreakspace\{0\}
.

Preface

Our script resides in the realm of algebraic geometry: a unique blend of various fundamental fields of mathematics. In the following paragraphs, we will paint a picture that portrays the position of this script within the mathematical arena.

Topology
is the study of geometry up to continuous deformations. It is unconcerned with the space being dense or sparse, curved or stretched (but it is sensitive to perforations). While geometry takes a quantitative view on spaces, topology takes a non-quantitative, continuous viewpoint: topology equips a space with a notion of
nearness
without quantitatively spelling out how near they actually are. In this way, topology generalizes the notion of a metric space, whence generalizing the notion of distance between its elements. One of the most pivotal challenges in mathematical theory is to classify spaces up to a given
type
. The classification of spaces up to diff(hom)-eomorphisms often pose limitations and topology offers a relaxed treatment through its notion of
homotopy
, which is an invariant that segregates spaces into families by their reachability via continuous maps: two continuous maps
f,g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
between topological spaces are
homotopic
if there is a collection of continuous maps
H(x,i)
(the homotopy function)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912ptH(x,i)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\times[0,1]\to Y\qquad\text{such that}

\displaystyle H(x,0)=f(x),\quad H(x,1)=g(x),\quad\forall\ x\in X

Algebra
is the art of discretizing these continuous data.
Algebraic topology
, thereby, develops frameworks to systematically transport topological challenges into the language of algebra - be it commutative, homological, homotopical, differential, or categorical.

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt\text{Geometry}\xrightarrow{\makebox[71.13188pt]{\text{deformation}}}\text{Topology}\xrightarrow{\makebox[71.13188pt]{\text{discretize}}}\text{Algebra}

\displaystyle\frac{\text{Quantitative data}}{\text{bend, squeeze, stretch}}\xmapsto{\makebox[28.45274pt]{}}\text{Continuous data}\xmapsto{\makebox[28.45274pt]{}}\text{Discrete data}

Algebraic topology provides a home for tackling problems from geometry and topology facilitated by its numerous
algebraic invariants
. This translation is enhanced by the notion of
categories
and functors between categories.
Functors
are certain inherent maps that preserve the invariant nature between categories; an
algebro-topological functor
associates a space to an algebraic invariant (groups, rings, modules,…). The
building blocks
of algebraic topology can be broadly divided as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt\text{Spheres}\quad S^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}||\vec{x}||=1\}\hskip 45.5244pt\text{(Geometrical object)}

\displaystyle\text{Simplices}\quad\Delta^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Sigma x_{i}=1,x_{i}\geq 0\}\quad\text{(Combinatorial object).}

Every "decent" topological space is fundamentally glued out of these blocks. Analyzing maps ’out of spheres’ gives rise to the
homotopy
theory and analyzing maps ’out of simplices’ gives rise to
(co-)homology
theory: two of the most fundamental algebro-topological invariants. The
n
-simplex (pl. simplices) is homeomorphic to another important object called the
n
-ball
given by
D^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}||\vec{x}||\leq 1\}
. In the view of homotopy theory, the following fact exemplifies the usefulness of such balls: every
n
-ball is homotopy equivalent to a point
\{\bullet\}
(though the former is
n
-dimensional and the latter is 0-dimensional!). This crucial property means that almost all of the known algebraic invariants, when taken up to homotopy, become trivial on balls. This property makes it an ideal object for the choice of parameterizing homotopies in any category, as they themselves do not add any extra data! Spaces that are homotopy equivalent to a ball are called
contractible
spaces. In the view of algebro-topological theories, balls can be seen as
contrasting objects
to spheres (e.g., the (co-)homology theory of the former is always trivial and the latter is never trivial). Therefore, within the framework of any homotopy theory, a solid comprehension of these objects is fundamental. Algebraic topology has a wealth of tools to study a given space:

Cellular approximation, Siefert-Van Kampen & Mayer-Vietoris type gadgets, Excision, Covering spaces, Hopf-Brouwer degree map, Obstruction theory & Postnikov towers, Operads, Localization exact sequences, Universal coefficients,

Künneth formula, Poincaré duality, Spectral sequences, Characteristic classes, Hurewicz theorem,
\dots

Such a vast collection of machinery is transported to nearby areas of mathematics by invoking the categorical framework. Several of these tools behave well under ’decent’ functorial transformations, and so this enables one to export tools from algebraic topology to the realm of
algebraic geometry
- a novel area where algebraic topology meets geometry in profound ways. It is where polynomial equations define the geometric objects. The building blocks of algebraic geometry are the
affine varieties
upon which bigger blocks of
quasi-projective varieties
and schemes are built. Every scheme is locally glued out of affine varieties (in the so-called
Zariski topology
). At the heart of this conception is the
Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz
(German for "theorem of zeroes") that embellishes a dictionary between algebra and geometry

\displaystyle\hskip 105.27519pt\text{Algebra}\longleftrightarrow\text{Geometry}

\displaystyle\{\text{Radical\ ideals\ of}\ \mathbb{C}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]\}\longleftrightarrow\{\text{Zero \ sets\ in}\ \mathbb{C}^{n}\}

\displaystyle\hskip 137.99586ptJ\longmapsto\mathcal{Z}(J),

\displaystyle\hskip 109.5431pt\mathrm{\sqrt{\mathcal{I}(W)}\longmapsfrom W}

Roughly, it says that for an ideal
J\subseteq\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
, we have
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{Z}(J))=\sqrt{J}
, where
\sqrt{J}
is the radical ideal of
J
. This result extends to all algebraically closed fields
k
as well!

Affine Algebraic Geometry

Commutative algebra
is a classical subdomain of algebra concerned with algebraic objects (like groups, rings, modules) that obey the law of commutativity
a*b=b*a
(in a precise mathematical sense). It provides the local algebraic tools required to understand the global geometric objects. One of the most useful tools from commutative algebra is that of
localization
of objects, which, in fact, can be generalized to maps in a given category. This allows one to "formally" invert a (class of) maps in any category to create a new category with extra maps. For example, homotopy theories are constructed in this way. The combined power of the Nullstellensatz and the localization allows one to transport almost all of the machinery from commutative algebra, locally, into the realm of algebraic geometry. Such systematic processes form the base of an important subfield of algebraic geometry called the
affine algebraic geometry
. Some of the open problems in affine algebraic geometry involve a deep understanding of the ring with the simplest possible description: the polynomial ring in
n
-variables
k[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
.

What is Motivic Homotopy Theory?

Motivic homotopy theory
is a sophisticated algebro-geometric machinery that utilizes tools from all of the above-mentioned areas. The fundamental idea is to develop a
homotopy theory for algebraic geometry
. In algebraic topology, one studies continuous maps between topological spaces and their homotopy classes. Motivic homotopy theory creates an analogous framework for algebraic varieties and schemes. Unlike algebraic topology, the framework of algebraic geometry is
relative
- rather than studying a scheme by itself, one studies a
scheme over a scheme
, that is, a morphism of schemes.

The Flashback

The plot begins with Grothendieck’s revolutionary ideas about
motives
(from the French usage:
motifs
) in the 1960s through the observation that various cohomology theories (de Rham, étale, crystalline,
\dots
) for algebraic varieties seemed to capture overlapping but distinct aspects of the same underlying geometric information. He envisioned a universal theory of motives - abstract objects that would encode the essential cohomological data of varieties, with each specific cohomology theory arising as a
realization
of these motives. He further conjectured that there exists a suitable cohomology theory (now called the
motivic cohomology
) through which all other known theories should factor. So, philosophically speaking, motives are meant to capture the
cohomological essence
of an algebraic variety. His original program was extraordinarily ambitious but proved technically challenging (
[
89
]
). He envisioned that the category of motives would be Abelian and would provide a universal cohomology theory, but constructing this category rigorously remained elusive for decades. Motivic cohomology was first conjectured to exist by Beilinson and Lichtenbaum in the mid-1980s via certain "motivic complexes" (see
[
29
,
30
]
and references therein). Up till this date, several mathematicians continue investing huge efforts in this direction, and a lot of progress has been made so far in assembling pieces of this motivic puzzle.

Higher Chow groups:
Bloch
[
37
]
introduced the theory of
higher Chow groups
, a generalization of the classical Chow groups, which was the first integral (as opposed to rational) definition of Borel-Moore motivic homology for quasi-projective varieties over a field (see also
[
132
]
). The higher Chow groups later turned out to be the motivic cohomology
H^{p,q}
in disguise due to the work of Voevodsky (
[
205
]
): for a smooth scheme
X
over any field and integers
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a natural isomorphism of groups

H^{p,q}(X,\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{CH}^{q}(X,2q-p).

The Homotopical Revolution:
A crucial parallel development was Quillen’s introduction of
model categories

[
170
]
, which provided an axiomatic framework for doing homotopy theory in contexts far beyond topology. This would later prove crucial for the foundations of motivic homotopy theory. On the other hand, between 1970-80s,
algebraic
K
-theory
developed rapidly under Atiyah, Bass, Milnor, Serre, Quillen et al and it became clear that it had deep connections to the motivic phenomena, but the precise relationship remained mysterious (see also the beautiful explanation of how Serre’s conjecture naturally lead to the birth of
K
-theory
[
130
]
).

The Birth of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Homotopy Theory
: The breakthrough finally came when Morel and Voevodsky realized they could bypass some of the difficulties in motivic theory by working in a homotopical rather than homological context. Their key insight was to construct a homotopy theory where the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
plays the role that the unit
[0,1]
interval plays in classical homotopy theory. They defined the motivic or the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
of schemes
[
158
]
and established that it has the structure of a closed model category. They also showed how to construct motivic cohomology as a
representable
functor in this setting. Their approach involved several sophisticated techniques involving the choice of a Grothendieck topology (Nisnevich), imbibing the theory of simplicial sheaves (thereby encoding homotopical data), and exploiting localization techniques (Bousfield) to systematically invert
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalences.

Motivic Spaces and Motivic Spectra:

Similar to the classical homotopy theory, the motivic homotopy theory has an unstable and stable version. For a field
k
, the unstable motivic theory
\mathcal{H}(k)
has several realization functors that allow one to transport information between the world of algebraic topology and motivic geometry. Passing onto the
S^{1}
-spectra and inverting the Tate circle
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, one obtains the
motivic spectra

\operatorname{Spt}(k)
and the associated stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory
\mathrm{SH}(k)
. The inversion of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
signifies that spaces become
weakly equivalent
upon smashing with
\mathbb{P}^{1}
s. Moreover, inverting
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is essential to have non-trivial transfers in
\mathrm{SH}(k)
(see
[
72
, Part III, §2, Remark 2.8]
). The luxury of the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category is that several of the algebraic theories become representable here:

1.

The algebraic
K
-theory is representable (see
[
82
,
188
]
):
If
k
is a Noetherian ring with finite Krull dimension, then there is a natural isomorphism in
\mathrm{SH}(k)

\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}_{+}[\beta^{-1}]\xrightarrow{\simeq}\textbf{KGL},

where
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
is the infinite projective space,
\beta
is a certain Bott element and
KGL
is the algebraic
K
-theory of Voevodsky
[
202
]
.

2.

Chow groups are representable: assume
k
is a perfect field. For every
n\geq 0
, and every smooth
k
-scheme
X
, there is a canonical bijection

\operatorname{CH}^{n}(X)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{SH}(k)}(X_{+},\textbf{K}(\mathbb{Z}(n),2n).

Here,
\textbf{K}(\mathbb{Z}(n),2n)
is the
motivic Eilenberg–Mac Lane space

3.

Motivic cohomology is representable (
[
147
, Chapter 14]
): For every
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
and integers
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is an isomorphism

H^{p,q}(X,\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{SH}(k)}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\Sigma^{p,q}\textbf{HZ})

where
HZ
is the
motivic Eilenberg-Maclane spectrum
.

4.

Vector bundles are representable on smooth affine schemes (
[
160
,
179
,
17
]
): If
k
is smooth over a Dedekind ring with perfect residue fields, then for any smooth
affine

k
-scheme
X
, there is a pointed bijection

\mathcal{V}_{r}(X)\xrightarrow{\simeq}[X,\operatorname{Gr}_{r}]_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}.

Here,
\mathcal{V}_{r}
denotes the set of algebraic vector bundles of rank
r
and
\operatorname{Gr}_{r}
is the Grassmannian of rank
r
.

Some Phenomenal Applications of Motivic Homotopy Theory

The Milnor Conjecture:

The Milnor Conjecture
[
151
, Question 4.3]
is a statement that asserts a relationship between the Milnor
K
-theory and the fundamental ideal of the Grothendieck-Witt ring of a field
k
(see
Section

B.0.2
). The full solution is due to Orlov-Vishik-Voevodsky
[
167
,
203
,
192
]
using motivic homotopy theory:

\mu\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{M}}_{*}(F)/2\xrightarrow{\simeq}\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{I}^{n}(F)/\mathcal{I}^{n+1}(F)

The Bloch-Kato Conjecture
:
The Bloch-Kato conjectures were solved due to the cumulative efforts of several mathematicians between 1980-2000. On one side of the plot, we have a family of related conjectures stated independently by Beilinson and Lichtenbaum, which were later unified into what’s now called the
Beilinson-Lichtenbaum Conjecture
. It predicts that the motivic cohomology and étale cohomology agree in certain ranges for a smooth scheme
X
over arithmetic fields:

\displaystyle H^{p,q}(X,\mathbb{Z}/n)\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{p}_{\acute{e}t}(X,\mu_{n}^{\otimes q})\quad\text{for}\quad p\leq q.

Around the 1990s, Voevodsky, with several others, recognized that proving the Beilinson Lichtenbaum conjecture required developing motivic cohomology systematically by homotopical methods. On the other side, Bloch and Kato
[
36
]
formulated their conjecture connecting Milnor
K
-theory to Galois cohomology via the Bloch-Kato map (a.k.a. the norm residue homomorphism). Again, these were a collection of statements rather than a single statement, proven incrementally, with different cases requiring different levels of the motivic machinery. These conjectures were then further generalized and unified into what’s called the
Bloch-Kato Conjecture
. It became reformulated in terms of motivic cohomology as it became representable by motivic Eilenberg-MacLane spaces. Building on the proof of the Milnor conjecture, the Bloch-Kato conjecture was eventually proven in full generality through the combined work of several mathematicians, with the final breakthrough coming from Voevodsky
[
210
]
: it describes the relationship between Milnor
K
-theory and Galois cohomology: for any field
k
and integers
n
and
l\neq
char
k
, we have an isomorphism

\displaystyle K^{\operatorname{M}}_{r}(F)/n\xrightarrow{\simeq}H_{\acute{e}t}^{r}(F,\mu_{n}^{\otimes r})\quad\text{for all}\quad n,r\geq 0

The case
n=2
is the Milnor conjecture, and
r=2
is the Merkurjev–Suslin theorem. The proof of the Bloch-Kato conjecture heavily relies on the motivic homotopy theory infrastructure developed between the 1990s-2000s, particularly: The motivic Adams spectral sequence, motivic Steenrod operations (
[
209
]
), and so on. A complete proof of the Bloch-Kato conjecture automatically implies the original Beilinson and Lichtenbaum conjecture
[
207
]
and a related Quillen-Lichtenbaum conjecture. The Bloch-Kato isomorphism is also called the
norm residue isomorphism
. In retrospect, rather than being applications of motivic homotopy theory, these conjectures were the driving factors for the emergence of motivic homotopy theory. These demonstrate that motivic homotopy theory is not just abstract machinery but could solve concrete, longstanding problems in arithmetic and algebraic geometry. The following are some more recent applications.

•

Voevodsky’s slice tower construction in
\mathrm{SH}(k)
(see
[
206
]
and
[
204
]
) and the verifications of several related conjectures (see e.g.,
[
208
,
134
,
189
,
135
,
24
]
and references therein)

•

The construction of Grothendieck’s Six Functor Formalism in
\mathrm{SH}(k)

[
21
]
,

•

Just like the complex cobordism spectrum
MU
is universal among complex-oriented cohomology theories, the existence of
\mathrm{SH}(k)
gives rise to algebraic cobordism spectrum
\Omega^{n}
represented by
MGL
, which is the universal oriented cohomology theory for a smooth scheme
X
of codimension
n
(
[
131
]
):

\displaystyle\Omega^{n}(X)\xrightarrow{\simeq}[\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}X_{+},\Sigma^{2n,n}\textbf{MGL}]_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}

•

Morel’s computation of motivic stable
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
of the sphere spectrum
[
160
]
, followed by first computations of motivic unstable
\pi_{1}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
by Asok-Morel (
[
18
]
) and higher motivic homotopy groups, with applications to vector bundles of Asok-Fasel (
[
15
,
16
]
).

Vista:
This script emerged as a cross-fertilization of ideas collectively drawn from the traditional (commutative algebra, algebraic topology) to the modern (affine algebraic geometry, motivic homotopy theory) vogue machinery. Several mathematical inceptions are borne out of the culmination of these realms, rendering the reciprocal movement fruitful in the last century. The author’s ambitious goal is to convince the reader of one such beautiful truth in the next ten-squared pages or so.

Blueprint of the script

This script is primarily structured into five chapters that we shall now outline.

In
Chapter

1
, we begin with a gentle introduction to the topology at infinity, a machinery that is exclusively designed to study open manifolds (non-compact, without boundary) and review the general knowledge and motivation for such a machinery. The ideal overriding goal would be to develop such machinery for smooth affine schemes (cf.
Section

C.2
). We will touch upon the theory of exotic affine varieties in low dimensions in
Section

1.2
. Following this,
Chapter

2
introduces us to the beautiful world of motivic homotopy theory. This chapter provides the foundational scaffolding upon which the remainder of the script is assembled. In particular, we set up the basic language for the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory via simplicial Nisnevich sheaves and construct the unstable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category in
Section

2.1.1
, where algebro-topological analogs such as (path) connectedness and contractibility are furnished. Among important formulations are that of functoriality (
Section

2.1.8
) and the notion of relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility (
Section

2.1.10
). Affine algebro-geometric counterpart is discussed in
Chapter

3
, where we study in detail the characterization of the affine
n
-space from various sources of related formulations, giving us a multi-faceted vantage point. The foundations developed in these chapters will allow us to develop the theory of relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of smooth schemes in low dimensions in
Chapter

4
. We proceed dimension-by-dimension. The theory of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility for étale (i.e., zero-dimensional) schemes over fields and its extension over arbitrary base schemes are discussed in
Section

4.1
. Here we obtain the following (
Theorem

4.1.2
):

Theorem
.

Let
X
be an étale scheme of finite presentation over an arbitrary base scheme
S
. Then the canonical morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
if and only if
f
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
.

We also show that any separated étale scheme is necessarily
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid (
4.1.3
). In
Section

4.2
, we move on to dimension 1 where the uniqueness of affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
is established over arbitrary fields (
Theorem

4.2.2
) which gives us the leverage to extend this characterization to the relative setting over (normal) base schemes in
Section

4.2.2
. The main theorem here is the following (
Theorem

4.2.5
)

Theorem
.

For a smooth separated morphism of finite presentation
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
and relative dimension
1
over a Noetherian normal scheme
S
, the following are equivalent:

1.

f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\mathrm{Spc}_{S}
,

2.

f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber space,

3.

f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle.

This has the following major applications:

a) The characterization of the affine line over (normal) base schemes (
Corollary

4.2.6
):

Corollary
.

A smooth separated scheme
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
of finite presentation and relative dimension
1
over a Noetherian normal scheme
S
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
if and only if all of the following holds:

1.

X
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible curve in
\mathcal{H}(S)
,

2.

the relative canonical sheaf of differentials
\Omega_{X/S}
of
f
is trivial, and

3.

f
has a section.

b) The relative variant of Zariski Cancellations (
Corollary

4.2.9
):

Corollary
.

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be a smooth separated scheme of finite presentation and relative dimension
1
over a normal scheme
S
such that
X\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\cong\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
for some
n\geq 1
. Then
X
is
S
-isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
.

Here, the normality of the base scheme is essential as we provide plenty of counter-examples in
Section

4.2.3
. Systematically, we proceed to dimension 2, where we discuss the uniqueness of an affine plane over a field of characteristic zero, as was recently proven by Choudhury-Roy (
Theorem

4.3.1
)
and extend it over perfect fields in
Theorem

4.3.4
. This characterization being open for imperfect fields, we provide some speculations involving non-trivial forms of
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
in
Section

4.3.3
. All this will enable us to prove the following main result (
Theorem

4.3.11
):

Theorem
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme of relative dimension
2
over a Dedekind scheme
D
with characteristic zero residue fields. Furthermore, let the canonical morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be affine. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
,

2.

f
is a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-bundle.

This has the following major applications:

a) The characterization of the affine plane in relative dimension 2 (
Corollary

4.3.13
):

Corollary
.

A smooth affine scheme
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to D
over an
affine
Dedekind scheme
D
with characteristic zero residue fields is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{D}
if and only if both of the following holds:

1.

X
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible surface in
\mathcal{H}(D)
, and

2.

the relative canonical sheaf of differentials
\omega_{f}
is trivial.

b) The relative variant of Zariski Cancellations (
Corollary

4.3.14
):

Corollary
.

Let
D
be an affine Dedekind scheme with characteristic zero residue fields. Then for a smooth affine scheme
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to D
of relative dimension 2 over a Dedekind scheme
D
, the isomorphism
X\times_{D}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{D}\cong\mathbb{A}^{3}_{D}
implies that
X\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}_{D}
.

This gives us the complete picture in relative dimension 2 as there are well-established counter-examples over a field of positive characteristics (
Example

4.3.15
and other examples therein). Following these characterizations, we jump on to higher dimensions (
\geq 4
) in
Section

4.4
where our main motivation is to expand on techniques to produce abundant relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible smooth affine schemes. The relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of higher-dimensional smooth (affine) schemes has been studied extensively in
Section

4.4.1
where our motivation is to exemplify that a certain family of smooth affine quadrics, which has been well-studied in the literature, gives ample instances of relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible smooth schemes. This fact is spelled-out in
Theorem

4.4.3
:

Theorem
.

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be a separated
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space over a regular scheme
S
, for all
n\geq 0
. Then
X
is relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over
S
.

In
Chapter

5
, we study the relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of threefolds, which completes the picture of our quest for relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility. We study the family of Koras-Russell threefolds
\mathcal{K}
over a perfect field in
Section

5.1.1
and establish its unstable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility over perfect fields (
5.1.7
):

Proposition
.

For any perfect field
k
, the canonical morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\to\operatorname{Spec}k
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
.

The above proposition, when coupled with Morel-Voevodsky’s gluing technique (
Theorem

2.1.47
), will allow us to derive the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of Koras-Russell threefolds over relative base schemes as well:

Theorem
.

The following are
Theorem

5.1.8
and
Theorem

5.1.9
:

1.

The Koras-Russell three fold of first kind
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}
is relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. In particular,
\mathcal{K}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\mathbb{Z}}
that is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

2.

Let
S
be any Noetherian scheme with perfect residue fields. The Koras-Russell threefold of first kind
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\to S
is relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
.

By observing that all these techniques solely involve fibrewise analysis, we proceed to invoke all these techniques to their higher-dimensional analogs in
Section

5.2
. Here, we study the
generalized Koras-Russell varieties
and enhance its unstable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility over perfect fields (
Theorem

5.2.1
)

Theorem
.

Let
k
be any perfect field. Then the canonical map of the generalized Koras-Russell varieties
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)\to\operatorname{Spec}k
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
.

This will then allow us to extend this to general base schemes (
Corollary

5.2.2
):

Corollary
.

Let
S
be any Noetherian scheme with perfect residue fields. Then the canonical morphism of the generalized Koras-Russell varieties
\varphi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
.

All these results will fruitage to a major application: existence of exotic motivic spheres in higher dimensions. We proceed with a survey of exotic motivic spheres in dimensions
\leq 3
in
Section

5.3.2
and establish the existence of exotic spheres in dimensions
\geq 4
in
Section

5.3.3
. In particular, we show that the complement of Koras-Russell prototypes is sufficiently connected (
Lemma

5.3.7
):

Lemma
.

Let
k
be any infinite perfect field. Then the strictly quasi-affine variety
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{\bullet\}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected.

Primarily using a motivic excision style result, we then show that these varieties in fact provide examples of exotic motivic spheres (
Theorem

5.3.8
):

Theorem
.

Let
k
be an infinite perfect field. Then for every
m\geq 0
, the strictly quasi-affine variety
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{\bullet\}
of dimension
m+3
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
, but is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
as a
k
-scheme.

This concludes the bulk of the script. The author has taken substantial efforts to make this script as self-contained as possible. For this
motive
, a good collection of background and supporting ideas is provided in
Appendix

A
,
Appendix

B
, and
Appendix

D
.

The script in a concise non-expert viewpoint

Spaces similar to "points" in algebraic geometry are called
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibles
, and our script revolves around these objects. The affine spaces
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
are primordial examples of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes, and so one ponders whether a smooth affine scheme
X
being
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
in the motivic homotopy category automatically implies that they are isomorphic as schemes as well?! We will review this situation over base fields and relatively extend it to general base schemes. We will witness that there are instances where this does hold and instances otherwise; spaces of the latter nature will be called
exotic
. We shall cover this aspect in the first part of our script in
Chapter

4
. In contrast to this aspect, the material further studies the "compact" analog of exoticness. The smooth schemes
\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
are examples of spheres in motivic geometry. This makes one ponder
if these are the only spheres up to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy, i.e., whether a smooth scheme
X
being
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
in the motivic homotopy category automatically implies that they are isomorphic as schemes?! This forms the sole essence of
Chapter

5
, where we discover the anti-verity of this expectation.

To the challenges that made me stronger…

Chapter 1
Overview

1.1
Introduction

The Euclidean spaces
\mathbb{R}^{n}
are one of the most basic objects of study in manifold theory. They are open (non-compact, without boundary) smooth
n
-manifolds that are contractible, i.e., homotopy equivalent to a point, and so they have trivial homology and homotopy theories. It is therefore natural to characterize this object with respect to this very property, namely, the following:

Question 1.1.1
.

Is the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
the unique open contractible smooth manifold in dimension
n
?

Phrased differently, the question claims that "every open smooth
n
-manifold that is homotopic to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
". This statement should ring a bell to its closely resembling contrast in topology: the celebrated
Poincaré Conjecture
, whose generalized version asserts that "every closed smooth
n
-manifold homotopic to
\mathbb{S}^{n}
in a specified category
\mathcal{C}
is also homeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{n}
in
\mathcal{C}
". The topological situation
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Top}
is proven to be true in all dimensions: Smale (
n\geq 5
), Freedman (
n=4
), and Perelman (
n=3
), and other cases were well-known. On the flip side, the smooth situation
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Diff}
is not fully solved: well-known for
n\leq 2
, the case
n=3
is peculiar as the topological and the smooth version coincide, the case
n=4
seems to be notoriously difficult and has been open for decades now (refer to
[
180
]
). The smooth version fails in certain higher dimensions due to the presence of Milnor’s
exotic spheres
(
[
150
]
) - a sphere that is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{n}
but not diffeomorphic to the
\mathbb{S}^{n}
with the standard differential structure.

In the light of this breathtaking program,
1.1.1
is famously known as the
Open Poincaré Conjecture
. This question is now completely understood using the machinery of
topology at infinity
, as we shall review in the upcoming sections. Our goal in this chapter is to give the background and solution to this question in the topological setting and motivate its algebro-geometric parallel. In
Section

1.1.1
, we briefly review the formulation in topology describing homology and homotopy at infinity. We then introduce the algebro-geometric analog of
1.1.1
in
Section

1.2
(
1.2.1
). This program is also one of the key motivating factors for the author to delve deeply into algebraic geometry, and this script could be seen as one of its by-products.

1.1.1
Topology at Infinity

Topology at infinity is the study of how spaces behave "at their boundary" or in regions that are very far from any fixed point. It’s a way of studying the global structure of non-compact spaces by examining what happens as one moves arbitrarily far out. This is systematically studied by developing the so-called "end" of a space. An
end
of a (simplicial or chain) complex is a sub-complex with a particular type of infinite behavior capturing non-compactness in topology and infinite generation in algebra. The theory of ends dates back as far as the 1930s to Freudenthal
[
79
]
, who initiated this in connection with topological groups.

The theory of end spaces was initially applied to that of
\sigma
-compact spaces - a space
X
with an increasing sequence
\{K_{n}\}
of compact subspaces with each
K_{n}
in the interior of
K_{n+1}
such that

X=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}K_{n}

and the compact subsets
K_{n}
of
X
are partially ordered by inclusion. The idea was to study the homotopy theory of such spaces by considering its "ends" pointing outward of
X
. The primary example to think of is Euclidean spaces. For instance,
\mathbb{R}
has two ends pointing outside of it, the
+\infty
and
-\infty
, whereas
\mathbb{R}^{2}
only has one end pointing outside, the
\infty
. More precisely, consider the system of spaces

\mathcal{E}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\mathrm{Closure}(X\backslash K)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K\subset X\ \mathrm{compact}\}.

This forms an inverse system (a.k.a. pro-object) in the category of spaces and thus, a profinite space. The
set of ends
of
X
is a connected component of this system of spaces, i.e.,

e(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\lim_{K}\pi_{0}\ \bigg(\mathcal{E}(X)\bigg)

equipped with the inverse limit topology.

Remark 1.1.2
.

For those spaces without a boundary, its end can be interpreted as an "ideal boundary" which captures the different possible notions of infinity. Some common instances are one-point compactification in point-set topology, asymptotic behaviors in geometric topology, boundary at infinity in hyperbolic geometry, and so on.

Here is a more refined definition of end spaces.

Definition 1
.

The end space
e(W)
of a space W is the space of paths

\omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\big([0,\infty],\{\infty\}\big)\to(W^{\infty},\{\infty\})

with the compact-open topology such that
\omega^{-1}(\infty)=\{\infty\}
. Here,
W^{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=W\cup\{\infty\}
is the one-point compactification of
W
.

For a topological space
W
, its end space
e(W)
can be realized as a homotopy theoretic model for the behavior of
W
at infinity.

Example 1.1.3
.

Here are some basic examples:

•

If
K
is compact, then
e(K)=\emptyset
.

•

The real line has two components at infinity corresponding to the two distinct connected open neighborhoods
(-\infty,-k)
and
(k,\infty)
, for
k\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus,
e(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{S}^{0}=\{\pm\infty\}
. Therefore, an end space need not be necessarily connected.

•

For all
n\geq 2
,
\mathbb{R}^{n}
has only one end since
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash K
has only one unbounded component for any compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, thus
e(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cong\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
.

Homology at infinity

A suitable framework to study the theory of end spaces is the proper homotopy theory, in the sense that a map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to W
is a
proper homotopy equivalence
if
f
is a proper
1
1

1

inverse image of compact is compact
map, and it induces homotopy equivalence in the proper category. The properness of the
f
is equivalent to extending
f
to infinity via
f^{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V^{\infty}\to W^{\infty}
. Just as ordinary homology theory is good enough to study compact spaces,
locally finite homology
plays a key role for non-compact spaces. Recall that the
singular homology
groups
H_{*}(W)
are defined as the homology theory associated to the singular chain complex
S_{*}(W)
, i.e.,
H_{*}(W)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H_{*}(S_{*}(W))
. By replacing the singular chain complexes with locally finite chain complexes
S_{*}^{lf}(W)
, one defines the
locally finite homology
groups
H_{*}^{lf}(W)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H_{*}(S_{*}^{lf}(W))
. From the definition of these chain complexes, one immediately verifies that if
K
is any compact space, then
H_{*}(K)=H_{*}^{lf}(K)
(
[
105
, Example 3.4]
). These complexes provide us with the following definition:

Definition 2
.

The
singular homology at
\infty
of a space
W
is defined as
H_{*}^{\infty}(W)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H_{*}(S_{*}^{\infty}(W)
, where

S^{\infty}(W)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\textrm{Cone}(i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S_{*}(W)\to S_{*}^{lf}(W))_{*+1}

of the inclusion
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S_{*}(W)\to S_{*}^{lf}(W)
obtained by viewing a
r
-simplex
\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Delta^{r}\to W
as a locally finite singular chain (
[
105
, Definition 3.8]
).

These so-defined homology groups are related by the following long exact sequence:

\dots\to H^{\infty}_{r}(W)\to H_{r}(W)\xrightarrow{i_{*}}H^{lf}_{r}(W)\to H_{r-1}^{\infty}(W)\to\dots

Example 1.1.4
.

The following provides some first examples:

•

Let
K
be any compact space. Then
S_{*}(K)=S_{*}^{lf}(K)
and so the group
H^{\infty}_{*}(K)
is trivial.

•

Using Poincaré-Lefschetz type argument, one observes that the homology at infinity of any smooth open contractible
n
-manifold is that of a sphere of dimension
n-1
. For
n\geq 1
, let
W=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times K
. Then we have
H_{*}(W)=H_{*}(K)
and
H_{*}^{lf}(W)=H_{*-n}(K)
. And so,

H_{*}^{\infty}(W)=H_{*}(K\times S^{n-1})=H_{*}(K)\oplus H_{*-n+1}(K).

Thus, the homology at infinity
H_{*}^{\infty}(W)
of a space
W
is the proper homotopy invariant given by the difference between homology
H_{*}(W)
and locally finite homology
H^{lf}_{*}(W)
and the compactness of
W
is determined by the vanishing of
H_{*}^{\infty}(W)
.

Homotopy at infinity

In a similar vein as above, one has a parallel theory for homotopy groups. As in the case of end spaces, let us assume
X
is a
\sigma
-compact topological space with the compact subspaces
K_{n}\subset X
partially ordered by inclusion, and if
K\subset K^{\prime}
, we simply consider the inclusion
X\backslash K^{\prime}\subset X\backslash K
. A
base point at infinity
, denoted by
\star
, is given by an open subset
U\subset X
such that for any compact set
K
, there exists a compact set K’ with
K\subset K^{\prime}
and
U\backslash K^{\prime}
non-empty and simply connected.

Definition 3
.

Here is a definition of the fundamental group at infinity (
[
59
, P. 2]
):

•

Let
X
be any topological space with a base point at infinity
\star
. Then we define the
fundamental group at infinity of
X
based at
\star
as the inverse limit of the relative homotopy groups given as follows:

\pi_{1}^{\infty}(X,\star)=\lim_{\leftarrow}\pi_{1}(X\backslash K,U\backslash K)

where the inverse limit is over the cofinal family of compact subsets
K
of
X
such that
U\backslash K
is simply connected, partially ordered by inclusion, and using the natural induced maps on the fundamental groups.

•

We say that
X
is
simply connected at infinity
if
\pi_{1}^{\infty}(X,\star)
is trivial.

Alternatively, the space
(X,\star)
is said to be
simply connected at infinity
if for every compact
K\subsetneq X
, there exists
K\subsetneq K^{\prime}\subsetneq X
such that the induced map on the fundamental groups

\pi_{1}(X\backslash K^{\prime})\xrightarrow{0}\pi_{1}(X\backslash K)

is the trivial map.

Example 1.1.5
.

Here are some basic examples:

•

If
K
is compact, then the group
\pi_{1}^{\infty}(K)
is trivial.

•

For all
n\geq 0
, we have
\pi_{1}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cong\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1})
. Hence,
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is simply connected at infinity if and only if
n\geq 3
.

We are now in a position to spell out the solution of
1.1.1
. The proof is due to the culmination of several of the preceding mathematical works (see
Section

1.2.1
; for historical contributions see
[
19
, §1.1]
) with the final breakthrough coming from Siebenmann
[
186
]
. Combined with the astonishing work of Perelman and Freedman on the low-dimensional Poincaré conjecture (
[
152
,
42
]
), Siebenmann’s result gives us the following characterization of Euclidean spaces:

Theorem 1.1.6
.

The following version is stated in
[
19
, Theorem 2]
.

1.

For
n\leq 2
,
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the unique open contractible
n
-manifold,

2.

For
n\geq 3
,
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the unique open contractible
n
-manifold that is simply connected at infinity.

As witnessed above, the assumption of simply connectedness at infinity is necessary for dimension 3 and above. This is due to the existence of certain
exotic
manifolds, which by definition, are smooth contractible manifolds that are not homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We will revisit this situation shortly in
1.2.1
.

Remark 1.1.7
.

In terms of end spaces, we say that a space
X
is simply connected at infinity if
e(X)
is both simply connected and connected as a space. In fact, rather than just studying the
\pi_{1}^{\infty}
, one can study the "homotopy type at infinity" in the context of proper homotopy theory, for which we redirect the interested reader to
[
105
, Chapter 9]
.

It is time to transport this story into the realm of algebraic geometry!

1.2
Smooth Affine Schemes up to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility

In parallel to the topological situation, one observes that affine spaces
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
are one of the most basic objects of study in algebraic geometry. They are smooth, affine, and
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible - equivalent to that of a base field
k
. The latter property implies that many of the known algebro-geometric theories become trivial for
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
. Hence, the following question is inevitable:

Question 1.2.1
.

Is
\mathbb{A}^{n}
the unique smooth affine
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme of dimension
n
?

Alternatively, for a base field
k
, if a smooth affine scheme of dimension
n\geq 0
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
, then is it also isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
as a
k
-scheme? We will begin with a historical outlook of
1.2.1
.

1.2.1
Exotic Affine Varieties

The study of real and complex manifolds up to topological contractibility is a far-reaching concept that has several connections among nearby fields. The sophistication of algebraic geometry is the ability to forget the algebraic structure, when necessary, allowing one to compare it with the underlying topological structure. These are achieved by certain
realization functors
. As a consequence, one might obtain spaces that are topologically contractible but not contractible in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category. As a linkage to topological situation, a scheme that is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible but not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
will be called
exotic
. Experience from the topological situations from the preceding sections humbles one to walk through
1.2.1
dimension-by-dimension. Any 1-dimensional smooth complex contractible manifold is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}
(use
Theorem

1.1.6
with
\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}
). A difficulty presents itself already in the next step.

Exotic complex surfaces

The complex space
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is a contractible complex manifold. But apparently, this is not the only one. The first counter-example was constructed in 1971 in the landmark paper by C.P. Ramanujam
[
172
]
in response to a question of M.P. Murthy on the Zariski cancellation in dimension 2. He hoped that the hypotheses of a smooth affine rational complex variety
S
being topologically contractible automatically imply
S
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. But to his (our) much surprise, he constructed a counter-example himself, now called the
Ramanujam surface
in his eponym. The working strategy of his counter-example can be outlined as follows:

Example 1.2.2
.

Take the complex projective plane
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Consider a cubic with a cuspidal (or flex) point
q
and another non-degenerate conic
Q
such that their intersection multiplicity at a point
p\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
is 5 (
m_{p}(C\cap Q)=5
) and another point
r\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
where they intersect transversally (
m_{r}(C\cap Q)=1
). Such a
Q
exists either by avoiding the situation where
p=q
or by deducing the group law on the smooth locus of
C
. Now, consider the blowup
X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Bl_{r}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
at the point
r

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}\quad\textrm{such that}

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}(C)=\widehat{C}\quad\text{and}\quad\pi^{-1}(Q)=\widehat{Q}

where
\widehat{C}
and
\widehat{Q}
are the strict transforms of
C
and
Q
respectively. The Ramanujam surface is defined by setting

\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\backslash\{\widehat{C}\cup\widehat{Q}\}

He then shows that
X
is simply connected with trivial homology class
H_{1}(X)=(1)
and that
\pi_{1}(X)
is an Abelian group. Hence, by the Hurewicz theorem, one gets that
\pi_{1}(X)\cong H_{1}(X)=(1)
, whence
X
is topologically contractible. However, he
[
172
, §3]
showed that
\pi_{1}^{\infty}(X)\neq 0
, while the complex affine spaces
\mathbb{C}^{n}
are all simply connected at infinity. Since
\pi_{1}^{\infty}
is a homeomorphic invariant, this, in particular, implies that
X\ncong\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

But in the same paper, he proves that under a stronger assumption,
\mathbb{C}^{2}
can be uniquely identified:

Theorem
.

Let
X
be a non-singular complex algebraic surface which is contractible AND
simply connected at infinity
. Then
X
is isomorphic to the affine two-space as an algebraic variety.

Following this attractive construction, several analogs of Ramanujam surfaces have been studied and since then established. In 1988, Gurjar-Miyanishi
[
94
]
classified all the acyclic affine surfaces with log Kodaira dimension at least 1. Following this, Tom Dieck-Petrie studied a family of these smooth affine surfaces, which is now eponymously called the
tom Dieck-Petrie surface
defined as follows:

Example 1.2.3
.

For integers
m>l\geq 2
with
m,l
coprime, the
tom Dieck-Petrie surface

\nu_{m,l}
is a complex surface defined by the following equation:

\nu_{m,l}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\biggl\{\frac{(xz+1)^{m}-(yz+1)^{l}-z}{z}=0\biggr\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}

Let us now spell out some of the interesting properties of
\nu_{m,l}
. The smooth affine surface
\nu_{m,l}
is topologically contractible
[
194
, Theorem A]
but is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. This is because the former has log Kodaira
\bar{\kappa}(\nu_{m,l})=1
, but the latter has
-\infty
. As a consequence, let us also note that
\nu_{k,l}
cannot be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected (
15
). Indeed, due to the classification theory of
[
18
]
, any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected variety is necessarily
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruled
2
2

2

this is also called
log-uniruled
in the literature
(cf.
24
) and hence has log Kodaira dimension
-\infty
.

Another elegant tool to prove the topological contractibility is via the machinery of
affine modifications
. In their classical paper, Kaliman-Zaidenberg establish the conditions under which the topology is preserved under these modifications. Due to
[
113
, Example 3.1]
, we have that
\nu_{m,l}
is obtained as the affine modification of
\mathbb{A}^{2}
along the divisor
\mathcal{C}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x^{l}-y^{m}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}
with center
(1,1)\subset D
; here
\mathcal{C}
is the cuspidal curve which is topologically contractible (even
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible!). Thus, due to
[
113
, Corollary 3.1]
, one sees that
\nu_{m,l}
is topologically contractible. Essentially, exploiting the affine modification techniques and higher Chow groups calculation,
[
66
, Theorem 3.2]
also shows that
\nu_{k,l}
is in fact
stably
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
- a phenomenon by which an algebraic variety
(X,x)
becomes
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible after finitely many suspensions

(X,x)\wedge(\mathbb{P}^{1},\infty)\wedge\dots\wedge(\mathbb{P}^{1},\infty)\sim_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}*.

Since
\nu_{m,l}
is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected from the above discussion, one could also learn from this that the affine modifications do not preserve
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connectedness in general (cf.
[
66
, Example 2.32]
).

The characterization of Ramanujam on the affine plane does not generalize to higher dimensions, in the sense that there are smooth complex affine varieties in all dimensions
n\geq 3
which are topologically contractible, even simply connected at infinity, but not isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. In fact, due to a theorem of Ramanujam-Dimca (
[
215
, Theorem 3.2]
) we have the following:

Theorem
.

Let
X
be a contractible smooth affine algebraic variety. If
dim_{\mathbb{C}}X=n\geq 3
, then
X
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.

The proof involves the usage of
h
-cobordism and Lefschetz hyperplane section theorems. But vaguely put, in dimensions
d\geq 3
, one can obtain such an exotic affine variety by simply taking the product of one such topologically contractible surface with the desired number of affine spaces
\mathbb{A}^{n}
.

Remark 1.2.4
.

We shall see that due to the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theoretical characterization of the
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
(
Theorem

4.3.1
), such exotic surfaces are detected by
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility. In particular, this will show that there are topologically contractible surfaces that are not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, establishing that
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility is a considerably stronger notion than the topological contractibility (at least over fields admitting an embedding into
\mathbb{C}
).

Exotic real threefolds

Recall from
Theorem

1.1.6
that simply connectedness is necessary from dimension 3. The following pioneering example is one of its kind. J.H.C. Whitehead in 1934 proposed the following argument
[
211
]
in an attempt to prove the Poincaré conjecture for closed 3-manifolds: start with a homotopy equivalence
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}M\to S^{3}
. Removing a point, we have an open contractible manifold
M\backslash\{\bullet\}
and a continuous map
\widetilde{f}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}M\backslash\{\bullet\}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Now, he essentially argued that "an open contractible 3-manifold that is homotopic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
" and so that this homeomorphism
\widetilde{f}
would extend by continuity across infinity, inducing a homeomorphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}M\to S^{3}
. This proof collapsed soon after his own counter-example
[
212
]
, where he produced an open contractible 3-manifold that is not homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. This is called the
Whitehead manifold

\mathcal{W}
(
Fig.

1.1
) in his eponym.

Figure 1.1
:
Whitehead Continuum,
[
195
]

Following this exotic threefold, higher-dimensional analogues were established and their associated cancellation questions were studied deeply by several mathematicians exploiting tools available from the solution of the higher-dimensional Poincaré conjecture and the surgery theory (see the amazing cumulative works of
[
85
,
146
,
169
,
148
,
191
,
49
]
). A crucial property was then observed: the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
stayed simply connected in the complement of any compact subset, but the Whitehead manifold did not, and thus began the utility of topology at infinity!

Spaces such as the
Whitehead manifolds

\mathcal{W}
constitute the basic motivation to study the obstruction theory related to the characterization of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
and further in constructing motivic invariants at infinity (
Section

C.2
) to distinguish exotic affine
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible varieties. In
Chapter

5
, we will visit yet another
aesthetically notorious
family of exotic affine threefolds
\mathcal{K}
, which plays the analogous role of the Whitehead manifold in algebraic geometry.

Moving forward…

The
1.2.1
can be considered, in part, as the genesis of this script. In alignment with topology, this desired characterization is known as the
motivic open Poincaré conjecture
. The existence of exotic varieties as noted above restricts one from obtaining a complete solution to
1.2.1
as in
Theorem

1.1.6
. One of the vivid dreams in this direction is to have a motivic homotopy at infinity (
[
19
, Question 5.4.4]
, also see
Section

C.2
). To sum up, our aim in this script can be concisely articulated as follows:

•

Study the
1.2.1
in low dimensions over fields and extend the characterization to a more general base scheme in relative dimensions up to 2. In the sequel, we will also review the situation in dimensions
\geq 4
and illustrate a key usage of a theorem from affine algebraic geometry in harnessing plenitude of relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes (
Section

4.4
).

•

Study the theory of exotic affine varieties and extend the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of Koras-Russell threefolds (
Section

5.1
) and its generalized version (
Section

5.2
) over perfect fields and Noetherian base schemes. Following this, we study the compact analogs of this problem and prove the existence of exotic motivic spheres (
Section

5.3
) in all dimensions
\geq 4
.

Chapter 2
Motivic Homotopy Theory

Chapter Summary

In this chapter, we establish the foundations of (unstable) motivic homotopy theory. In
Section

2.1.1
, we construct the unstable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
\mathcal{H}(S)
after providing the necessary motivation and the framework of simplicial Nisnevich sheaves to build such a category. We describe the crucial motivic localization functor
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
in
Section

2.1.2
and explain its ability to retain both the other localizations, namely
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
and
\mathbb{L}_{Nis}
. The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-model structure is dealt with in
Section

2.1.3
. In the sequel, we define the central jargon of our script - the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility in
Section

2.1.4
and various important revolving notions such as
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connectedness (
Section

2.1.5
),
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigidity (
Section

2.1.6
), and
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connectedness (
Section

2.1.7
). This brings us to the fundamental notion of functoriality (
Section

2.1.8
), which plays a key role in motivic homotopy theory. We then apply the functoriality in the case when the space is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible and obtain preliminary results for future applications.
Section

2.1.10
and subsequent parts will play a pivotal role in
Chapter

4
, as they provide a framework for working relative to a base scheme. We finally conclude with the necessary background on Milnor-Witt
K
-theory in
Section

2.2
, which finds its application in
Chapter

5
.

2.1
The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Homotopy Category

In this section, we begin with the fundamentals of the construction of the unstable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory (a.k.a. Motivic Homotopy Theory) associated to the category of smooth, separated schemes of finite type over a base scheme
S
. To describe the homotopy theory of motivic spaces, one takes two approaches: the traditional approach via the language of model categories (cf.
Section

A.2
) as established by Quillen
[
170
]
or the relatively modern language of
(\infty,1)
-categories (e.g.,
[
136
]
). As far as our script is concerned, we will freely use both of these approaches while predominantly using the former approach. The latter approach using the
\infty
-machinery will be exploited at instances where it makes the context more tractable. Nevertheless, the reader should worry less about this aspect as all our formulations can very well be illustrated via both languages using modern bridges that exist between these two worlds (one such bridge is here:
[
173
, §2]
). For an exposition-type background, see
[
40
,
55
]
.

Running Convention
Throughout this chapter, unless mentioned otherwise:
S
will be a locally Noetherian base scheme of finite Krull dimensions,
k
will denote a field with its units
k^{\times}
,
\mathrm{Sch}_{S}
= separated schemes of finite type
S
,
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
= smooth separated schemes of finite type over
S
.

Why build on simplicial presheaves and not on schemes themselves?

The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category on
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
is built on the category of simplicial presheaves
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
. It is natural to ponder why not define it directly on
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
. The reason is that though the category
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
is complete (i.e., it has all limits) but it is very far from being co-complete (i.e., it lacks colimits), and it is common knowledge that colimits are indispensable to build a homotopy theory on any category. A convenient solution is to "formally add" the desired colimits to
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
. In the categorical language, this is equivalent to taking the
presheaf category on
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
. This is achieved by the Yoneda embedding defined via the following fully faithful functor,

\displaystyle Y\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathrm{Sm}_{S}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Fun}((\mathrm{Sm}_{S})^{op},\mathcal{S}et)=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Psh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})

\displaystyle\hskip 31.29802ptX\mapsto\bigg(U\mapsto R_{U}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sm}_{S}}(U,X)\bigg)

Here,
R_{U}
is the representable presheaf functor
1
1

1

also called the
contravariant
\operatorname{Hom}
functor
on
U
. The advantage is that we can now exploit the model categorical framework already built for the category of presheaves (explore more in
Section

A.2.4
). Any presheaf can be viewed as a simplicial presheaf when equipped with the "discrete" simplicial structure. Summing up, we have a series of functors

\mathrm{Sm}_{S}\xhookrightarrow{Y}\operatorname{Psh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})\xrightarrow{D}\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})

where
D
represents the ’discrete’ simplicial structure on the category of presheaves. However, this approach has not yet another issue. Let
U,V\subset X
be an open (Zariski) subscheme of a scheme
X
such that
X=U\cup V
. Then consider the following squares

\hbox to97.38pt{\vbox to46.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 48.68806pt\lower-23.15279pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
