\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

The first square (on the left) is a pushout in
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
, where
U\cap V
is the fiber product of schemes. However, the corresponding square (on the right) obtained via taking the representable presheaves does not stay as a pushout in
\operatorname{Psh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
unless
X=U
or
X=V
. In other words, by a priori defining spaces as presheaves, the union as presheaves
U\cup_{\operatorname{Psh}}V
is not the same as the categorical union
X
. Recall from classical topology that squares that satisfy this analogous property are called
Mayer-Vietoris squares
. Stated in analogy, this issue can be seen as a
failure of Mayer-Vietoris property
for schemes. A suitable way to resolve this is to modify the squares themselves as follows.

Definition 4
.

An
Elementary Distinguished Square
(EDS, for short) in
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
is a square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{-1}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

such that
p
is an étale morphism,
j
is an Zariski open immersion such that the pullback

p^{-1}(X\backslash U)\xrightarrow{\cong}X\backslash U

is an isomorphism (here
X\backslash U
is equipped with the reduced subscheme structure with support in the closed subset
X\backslash U
).

Example 2.1.1
.

An important class of EDS is provided by Zariski open coverings of the form
X=U\cup V
. Here, one takes
p=j_{V}
as an open embedding and the condition that
p^{-1}(X\backslash U)\to X\to U
is an isomorphism is equivalent to the condition that
U\cup V=X
.

One can easily see that an EDS is a pushforward in
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
. In other words,
X
is recovered as the colimit of the diagram
U\leftarrow p^{-1}(U)\to V
. This guarantees us the Mayer-Vietoris-type property for these kinds of squares. Now, recall that an open covering for schemes generalizes the notion of open coverings via families of maps, which is formulated via the notion of Grothendieck topologies (see
Section

A.1
). The strategy to coin a suitable category of spaces that also includes the analogous Mayer-Vietoris style property is to find a Grothendieck topology that is generated by these EDS as its "open coverings". To our fortune, there does exist such a topology and even more, it is super-convenient in motivic homotopy theory (cf.
Section

A.1.3
)

The Nisnevich topology

The
Nisnevich topology
, eponym to Yeo A. Nisnevich, appeared in
[
163
]
as the
completely decomposed topology
or the cd-topology, whose initial motivation was to construct certain descent for spectral sequences converging to various algebraic
K
-theory groups. For our purposes, we will only need the definitions and some properties of this topology. The following is one of the several equivalent definitions (see
Section

A.1.3
) of Nisnevich topology due to
[
158
, §3.1]
.

Definition 5
.

A finite family of étale morphisms
\{p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U_{i}\to X\}_{i\in I}
is a
Nisnevich cover
if and only if for each point
x\in X
and some index
I
, there is an
i\in I
and a point
y\in U_{i}
with
p_{i}(y_{i})=x_{i}
such that the induced map on the corresponding residue fields
\kappa(x)\to\kappa(y)
is an isomorphism. The
Nisnevich topology
on
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
is the Grothendieck topology generated by these Nisnevich covers.

Any Zariski cover is a Nisnevich cover. On the other hand, an étale cover is Nisnevich only if it is surjective on
k
-points for all fields
k
, as illustrated by the following example (
[
6
, Example 3.41]
).

Example 2.1.2
.

Let
k
be a field with characteristic unequal to 2 and let
a\in k^{\times}
. Consider a covering of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
by
j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}\backslash\{a\}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
and an étale map
p\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}\backslash\{0\}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
defined by
x\mapsto x^{2}
. Then this étale cover is Nisnevich if and only if
a=b^{2}
, for some
b\in k
.

An EDS as in
4
is closely related to Nisnevich covers as follows (
[
6
, Example 3.45]
).

Example 2.1.3
.

The Nisnevich cover from the
Example

2.1.2
is not an EDS. However, upon removing one of the square roots of
a
from
\mathbb{A}^{1}\backslash\{0\}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}\backslash\{0,b\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}\backslash\{a\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

The square becomes an EDS. For this reason, one sometimes calls an EDS the
Nisnevich distinguished square
.

The following furnishes the definition of a presheaf to be a sheaf in the Nisnevich topology (
[
202
, Definition 2.2]
).

Definition 6
.

A contravariant functor
\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathrm{Sm}_{S}^{op}\to\operatorname{Set}
is called a
Sheaf in Nisnevich topology
if the following two conditions hold:

1.

\mathcal{F}(\emptyset)=*
(takes the initial to the final object),

2.

for any EDS as in
4
, the square of
\operatorname{Set}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(p^{-1}(U))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

is cartesian, that is,
\mathcal{F}(X)\simeq\mathcal{F}(U)\times_{\mathcal{F}(p^{-1}(U)}\mathcal{F}(V)
.

Presheaves satisfying the conditions of
6
are said to satisfy the
Brown-Gersten property
or the
BG property
(
[
158
, P. 99]
), which can be seen as the analogous excision property for the Nisnevich topology. The terminology is chosen so that the authors in
[
41
]
studied this in the Zariski site. The following affirms that Nisnevich topology is indeed the right choice if we expect to have a Mayer-Vietoris type result in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category.

Lemma 2.1.4
.

Any EDS is a co-cartesian square in the category
\operatorname{Shv}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
. In particular, the canonical morphism

V/(U\times_{X}V)\to X/U

is an isomorphism of Nisnevich sheaves.

Proof.

See
[
158
, Lemma 1.6]
.
∎

2.1.1
The Unstable Construction

SLOGAN:
The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory is the homotopy theory of simplicial presheaves on
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
satisfying Nisnevich descent and
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance.

In this section, we will introduce the fundamental tools that will aid us in constructing the category of motivic spaces
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
and eventually the unstable motivic homotopy category
\mathcal{H}(S)
. For the sake of exposition, we will use the language of model categories. Among all the popular model structures on motivic spaces, the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local projective model structure
is conveniently suited in terms of functoriality, and so we will make use of this model structure. For various interesting accounts on the available model structures on simplicial presheaves, the reader can refer to
[
35
,
71
,
73
,
69
,
107
]
.

Bousfield Localizations

The advantage of using the local projective model structure is that every scheme is both a local projective fibrant and cofibrant as a presheaf and sheaf. However, we have that the category
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
embeds fully faithfully in the local projective homotopy category of simplicial presheaves; thus, to have a distinguished identification of smooth schemes in
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
, we would need to further enlarge the class of (local projective) weak equivalences. This is done via the process called
Bousfield localizations
, which can be thought of as an analogous localization of rings from commutative algebra (see
Section

A.2.2
for a background). To build the category of spaces, we need to undergo two such localizations: the Nisnevich localization and the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-localization that we describe below.

Nisnevich descent

Consider the category of simplicial presheaves
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
equipped with the Nisnevich topology. Let
U_{\bullet}
be an object in
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
such that
U_{n}
is a presheaf of sets in
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
, for each
n
. Suppose that the map
U\to V
is a Nisnevich cover, then one defines the Čech complex
\check{U}\to V
associated to
U\to V
by defining
\check{U}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=U\times_{V}\dots\times_{V}U
2
2

2

the self-product of
U
taken
(n+1)
-times over
V
. Then the Bousfield localization of the
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
with respect to this Čech covering exists and which is called the
Nisnevich localization
.

Theorem 2.1.5
.

(
[
6
, Theorem 3.33]
)
The Bousfield localization of
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
with respect to the class of Čech covers
\check{U}_{\bullet}\to V
exists. We denote this localization functor by
\mathbb{L}_{Nis}\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
.

We say that a simplicial presheaf
X\in\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
satisfies
Nisnevich descent
if for every Čech covers
\check{U}_{\bullet}\to X
, we have that the

{\mathrm{hocolim}_{n}\ U_{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}X}

is a weak equivalence of simplicial presheaves. More generally, one considers descent with respect to more general covers than the Čech covers in any Grothendieck topology
\tau
, which are called hypercovers. Recall that a covering
U_{\bullet}\to X
is called a
hypercover
if each
U_{n}
is a coproduct of representables, the induced map
U_{0}\to X_{0}
is a
\tau
-cover and each
U^{\Delta^{n}}\to U^{\partial\Delta^{n}}
is a
\tau
-cover in degree 0.

\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Localization: inverts
\mathbb{A}^{1}

At this point, it is not clear as how one should define contractible schemes in this category. To construct a homotopy theory on
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
that captures the underlying structure of
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
, we need to enlarge weak equivalences that are more intrinsic and that are parametrized by the objects of
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
itself. To remedy this, we enlarge the category
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
with respect to another natural class of morphisms. Recall that in topology, homotopies are parametrized with respect to an interval
[0,1]
. As
[0,1]
is not an algebraic variety, we replace it by the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Let
I
denote the class of maps
\{\mathbb{A}^{1}\times U\to U\mid U\in\mathrm{Sm}_{S}\}
in
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
. Alternatively, one can also choose
3
3

3

this is possible since
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
is essentially small
a subset
J\subseteq I
such that
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
varies over a representative of each isomorphism class of
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
and we will need to invert these classes of morphism as well. This process is called the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-localization
. In other words, all the projection maps

\{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}\times U\to U\mid U\in\mathrm{Sm}_{S}\}

would have now been inverted in the to-be-constructed
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category of schemes.

Definition 7
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
. Then we say that
\mathcal{F}
is said to satisfy
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance
if the induced morphism

\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{F}(X)\to\mathcal{F}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1})

of the natural projection
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to X
is a bijection. Such presheaves are said to be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant and we denote this subcategory of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant presheaves by
\operatorname{Psh}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})\subset\operatorname{Psh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
.

Example 2.1.6
.

Interestingly, note that not all (even representables!) presheaves are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant. The affine scheme
\mathbb{G}_{m}
represents the sheaf of units and is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant (cf.
Example

2.1.26
). On the other hand, the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
represents the sheaf of global sections, but it is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant (cf.
Example

2.1.27
).

Remark 2.1.7
.

Note that since
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\times U\to U
has a section, it is always split injective. Hence,
\mathcal{F}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant provided
\mathrm{pr}^{*}
is surjective. Moreover, observe that
\mathcal{F}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant if and only if the induced sections
i_{0}^{*}=i_{1}^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\times U)\to\mathcal{F}(U)
agree, for all
U\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
(
[
147
, Lemma 2.16]
).

Proposition 2.1.8
.

(
[
6
, Proposition 3.55]
)
The Bousfield localization of
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
with respect to the class of morphisms
J
exists. We denote this
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-localized category by
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
.

The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-localization functor
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Psh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})\to\operatorname{Psh}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
is the left adjoint to the inclusion functor
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Psh}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Psh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
.

With these two localizations set in place, we can now finally define the motivic homotopy category.

Definition 8
.

For a base scheme
S
, the category of
motivic
S
-spaces
, denoted
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
, is the category of simplicial presheaves over
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
. The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
, denoted
\mathcal{H}(S)
, is the homotopy category of
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
obtained by imposing the Nisnevich descent and the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-localization

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(S)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Shv}_{Nis}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})\cap\operatorname{Psh}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})\subset\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Spc}_{S}

There are also other variants that one could be interested in. For instance, by only imposing the Nisnevich descent, one forms the Nisnevich local homotopy category, denoted by
\mathcal{H}(S)_{Nis}
, and similarly, by only imposing the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance, one has the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local homotopy theory
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
. In our script, we will only be concerned about the homotopy category with both the localizations and hence only about
\mathcal{H}(S)
.

Pointed Motivic Spaces

One similarly has a parallel theory for the pointed
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category by considering the pointed versions of motivic spaces. For instance,

•

The affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
is pointed by 1,

•

The multiplicative scheme
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is pointed by 1,

•

The projective line is pointed by
\infty
(in fact, here the base point does not matter, as
(\mathbb{P}^{1},\infty)\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{1},x)
, for any
x\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
),

•

Any general motivic space
X
is pointed by the zero object
*
and so, we have
X_{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\sqcup{*}
.

The corresponding pointed motivic spaces is denoted by
\operatorname{Spc}_{S,\bullet}
and the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category is denoted by
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}
. There is an adjunction

(-)_{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}(S)\leftrightharpoons\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{U}

where
\mathcal{U}
is the right adjoint that forgets the base point. One can invoke several operations in
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}
as one does in topology. For instance, if
Y\to X
is a map of pointed motivic spaces, then the cofibre in
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}
can be computed as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y/X}

with the cofiber
Y/X
pointed by the image of
Y
. Similarly, using the zero object
*
in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
, we have suspensions in
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma X}

for any motivic space
X
. Moreover, if
X\vee Y
denotes the coproduct of motivic spaces, then we have the smash product in
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}
given by

X\wedge Y\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{X\times Y}{X\vee Y}.

The stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory
{\mathrm{SH}(S)}
is then obtained by formally inverting the motivic sphere
\mathbb{P}^{1}
(see
Section

5.3.1
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{SH}(S)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}[(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{-1}]

which gives the following functors:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}\xrightleftharpoons[\Omega^{\infty}]{\Sigma^{\infty}}\mathrm{SH}(S)

For more background on the stable theory, one can refer to
[
5
,
202
,
109
,
100
,
73
,
72
]
.

\mathbb{L}_{Nis}\leftrightharpoons\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
destroy each other

Although the definition of
\mathcal{H}(S)
is formally straightforward, there is a practical difficulty when one tries to do some computations from scratch. The localization functors
\mathbb{L}_{Nis}
might destroy the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
need not preserve the (Nisnevich) sheaf nature. Consider the following instance (
[
158
, §3, Example 2.7]
):

Example 2.1.9
.

Let
U_{0}=\mathbb{A}^{1}\backslash\{0\}
and
U_{1}=\mathbb{A}^{1}\backslash\{1\}
, and let
U_{01}=U_{0}\cap U_{1}
. Since both
U_{0}
and
U_{1}
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant (cf.
Example

2.1.26
), it follows that
U_{01}
is also
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant. Now, for a choice of closed embedding of
j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U_{01}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}
(for some
n
), define the coproduct

F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(U_{0}\times\mathbb{A}^{n})\bigsqcup_{U_{01}}(U_{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{n})

which is a (non-smooth) scheme
F\in\mathrm{Sch}_{S}
. Here, the morphism
U_{01}\to U_{i}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}
is the product of
j
with an open embedding
U_{01}\to U_{i}
, for
i=0,1
. Now, let
X
be any connected scheme. Consider the sheaf

R_{F}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Hom}(X,F)=\operatorname{Hom}_{Sch_{S}}(X,U_{0}\times\mathbb{A}^{n})\bigsqcup_{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sm}_{S}}(X,U_{01})}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sm}_{S}}(X,U_{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}).

Since
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
preserves pushouts, we have that

\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}R_{F}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(U_{0}\times\mathbb{A}^{n})\sqcup_{\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(U_{01})}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(U_{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}).

The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-localization contracts
\mathbb{A}^{n}
s and moreover due to the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance of
U_{0},U_{1},
and
U_{01}
, we get

\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(U_{i})=U_{i}\ \ ;\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(U_{01})=U_{01}

for
i=0,1
and we finally obtain its (associated)
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant sheaf as

\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}R_{F}\simeq U_{0}\sqcup_{U_{01}}U_{1}.

We claim that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}R_{F}
cannot be a sheaf. Suppose that it were a sheaf; then it should agree with its sheafification. But as sheafification is a left adjoint, it preserves pushouts, and hence we have

\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}R_{F}\simeq U_{0}\sqcup_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}U_{1}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}

which is a contradiction to the fact that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}R_{F}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant, because
\mathbb{A}^{1}
is not (cf.
Example

2.1.27
)! Hence, we conclude that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}R_{F}
cannot be a sheaf.

The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Singular Functor

In terms of computation, a practical replacement for
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
functor is the motivic singular chain functor
\operatorname{Sing}_{*}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
. In analogy with topology (cf.
Example

A.2.10
), it is obtained from the following
algebraic
n
-simplex
:

\Delta^{n}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n}]/\biggr(\sum_{i=0}^{n}t_{i}-1\biggl).

The space
\Delta^{n}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
exists as a smooth affine
k
-scheme that is (non-canonically) isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
. One of the main utilities of this space is to construct the (Suslin-Voevodsky)
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-singular chain functor

\operatorname{Sing}_{*}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigg(U\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sm}_{k}}(\Delta_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{*}\times U,X)\bigg).

One shows that this gives a cosimplicial scheme
\Delta^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{Fun}(\Delta,\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
. The following are some of the evident advantages of this functor:

Lemma 2.1.10
.

For any
F\in\operatorname{Psh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
, the presheaf
\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}_{*}(F)
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant.

Proof.

This is
[
158
, §3, Corollary 3.5]
.
∎

Proposition 2.1.11
.

If
F\in\operatorname{Psh}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
, then
F\to\operatorname{Sing}_{*}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(F)
is an equivalence of presheaves.

Proof.

See
[
158
, P. 87]
.
∎

Proposition 2.1.12
.

The canonical morphism
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\to\operatorname{Sing}_{*}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
is an equivalence of functors.

Proof.

This is
[
158
, Corollary 3.8]
.
∎

Corollary 2.1.13
.

The functor
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
preserves finite products.

Proof.

This follows from the identification of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\simeq\operatorname{Sing}_{*}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
in
2.1.12
. Note that since
\Delta^{op}
is sifted (for e.g., see
[
136
, Remark 5.4.4.8]
), colimits indexed over
\Delta^{op}
commutes with products. As a result,
\operatorname{Sing}_{*}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
preserves finite products and so does
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
.
∎

The following is an illustration of
Corollary

2.1.13
in action.

Example 2.1.14
.

For all
n
and for all
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
, the canonical projection
X\times\mathbb{A}^{n}\to X
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{H}(S)
. This is clear for
n=1
. For
n\geq 2
, this is a straightforward application of
Corollary

2.1.13
.

2.1.2
The Motivic Localization Functor

Recall that
\mathbb{L}_{Nis}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
might cancel out each others’ power. A wise way to retain both of its powers while preserving the categorical constraints is to iterate them infinitely many times. The resulting functor is called the
Motivic Localization Functor,
denoted by
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
. In categorical terms,
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
is obtained as the left adjoint of the inclusion functor
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}(S)\subset\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. It is explicitly defined as follows:

\mathbb{L}_{mot}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\textrm{colim}(\mathbb{L}_{Nis}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathbb{L}_{Nis}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathbb{L}_{Nis}\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\to\dots..)=(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\circ\mathbb{L}_{Nis})^{\circ\mathbb{N}}

and so described in this context, we have

\mathcal{H}(S)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{L}_{mot}(\operatorname{Spc}_{S}).

How does
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
preserve both localizations?

For any simplicial presheaf
\mathcal{F}
, the
\mathbb{L}_{mot}(\mathcal{F})
is a sheaf that satisfies both Nisnevich descent and
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance. Indeed, note that the following presentations of
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
are equivalent as a consequence of the cofinality (cf.
54
):

\displaystyle\textrm{colim}(\mathbb{L}_{Nis}\circ\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\to(\mathbb{L}_{Nis}\circ\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})^{2}\to\dots..)

\displaystyle\textrm{colim}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\circ\mathbb{L}_{Nis}\to(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\circ\mathbb{L}_{Nis})^{2}\to\dots..)

The former is a colimit computed in
\operatorname{Shv}_{Nis}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
, which is closed under filtered colimits and hence the resulting object is again a (Nisnevich) sheaf. The latter is a colimit computed in
\operatorname{Psh}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
, which is also closed under filtered colimits, hence it preserves
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance. The property that both of these categories
\operatorname{Shv}_{Nis}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
and
\operatorname{Psh}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
are closed under filtered colimits is because they are
accessible
subcategories of
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
(
[
136
, Example 5.2.4.7]
).

Proposition 2.1.15
.

The following are some of the essential preliminary properties of
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
:

1.

The functor
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
preserves colimits.

2.

The functor
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
preserves finite products.

3.

The functor
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
is locally cartesian.

Proof.

(1) follows from the fact that
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
is a left adjoint. (2) follows from the observation that both
\mathbb{L}_{Nis}
and
\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
preserve finite products. The
\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
is given as the sifted colimit of right adjoint functors. Now, from the presentation of
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
, it is constructed as the filtered colimits, which are known to preserve finite products. (3) follows from the facts that both
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
and
\mathbb{L}_{Nis}
is locally cartesian and
\mathbb{L}_{Nis}
is left exact (
[
104
, Corollaries 3.5 & 3.9]
).
∎

Remark 2.1.16
.

It in important to note that in some literature such as
[
19
,
25
,
174
]
, it is common to call the category of simplicial presheaves as motivic spaces and the localized category
\mathbb{L}_{mot}(\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S}))=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}(S)
as the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category. On the other hand, literature such as
[
6
]
terms the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-localized category as the category of spaces, which, in comparison, is the same as the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
\mathcal{H}(S)
. In this script, we have adopted the former approach.

The bird-eye view

All this information can be summarized as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}(S)_{Nis}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{\operatorname{Psh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{\operatorname{Spc}_{S}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{\mathcal{H}(S)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{\mathcal{H}(S)}_{\bullet}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{\mathrm{Sm}_{S}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}(S)_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{SH(S)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{mot}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{Nis}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{\mathbb{L}_{mot}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{\sqcup\{*\}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{mot}}

2.1.3
The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Model Structure

We will now brief on the choice of a model structure for
\mathcal{H}(S)
. For a detailed account of the background on the model categories, one can refer to
Section

A.2
. For the scope of this thesis, we will use the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local projective model structure (for e.g., see
[
38
,
35
,
6
]
). We will use the model structures developed for simplicial presheaves to define a model structure on
\mathcal{H}(S)
assuming the material in
Section

A.2.4
.

Definition 9
.

The following definitions forms the fundamental toolkit for identifying an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local model structure on
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
:

1.

A motivic space
Z\in\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
is called
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local
if it is local projective fibrant and the induced map

\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Hom}(U,Z)\to\operatorname{Hom}(U\times\mathbb{A}^{1},Z)

is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets with respect to the canonical projection
\mathrm{pr}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to U
. Hence, a Nisnevich sheaf is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local if and only if it is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant.

2.

A morphism of motivic spaces
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
is called an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local weak equivalence
if

(Qf)^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Hom}(QY,Z)\to\operatorname{Hom}(QX,Z)

is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets, for any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local space
Z
. Here
Q
is the local projective cofibrant replacement functor (cf.
36
)

The following result precisely gives us the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-model structure on
\mathcal{H}(S)
. The version stated here is due to Blander (
[
35
]
) as expressed in (
[
183
, Theorem 6.2.7]
):

Theorem 2.1.17
.

The motivic homotopy category
\mathcal{H}(S)
is equipped with respect to the following classes of maps

•

\mathcal{W}
eq as
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local weak equivalences,

•

\mathcal{F}
ib as objectwise fibrations

•

\mathcal{C}
of as
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local projective cofibrations

forms a simplicial proper cellular model category. This is called the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local projective model structure
on
\mathcal{H}(S)
.

The following fact is useful in identifying the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local spaces (
[
6
, Remark 3.58]
): a motivic space
Z\in\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local
if it satisfies the following:

•

takes values in Kan complexes (hence, fibrant in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
)

•

satisfies Nisnevich hyperdescent (hence, fibrant in
\mathcal{H}(S)_{Nis}
)

•

if
Z(U)\to Z(U\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets for al
U\in\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
(hence, fibrant in
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
).

Thus, it is important to note that the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local spaces are precisely the fibrant objects in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
\mathcal{H}(S)
.

2.1.4
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility

One of the essential notions in the realm of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory is that of schemes that are contractible. This notion is central to formulating our main results in
Chapter

4
and
Chapter

5
. We shall now see the definition and some basic examples. Later in
Section

2.1.10
, we shall be a bit more expansive and connect this notion with the contents of our script.

Definition 10
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a motivic space over an arbitrary scheme
S
. We say that
\mathcal{X}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
if the structure morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{X}\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in the category of motivic spaces
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
(or) equivalently,
f
is an isomorphism in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
\mathcal{H}(S)
, that is,
\mathbb{L}_{mot}(f)
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{H}(S)
.

Example 2.1.18
.

There are plenitude examples of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible spaces in nature:

1.

The affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, by the very construction. In fact, any affine space
\mathbb{A}^{n}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible given by the naïve
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy

\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}\quad(t,x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mapsto(tx_{1},\dots,tx_{n})

2.

The singular cuspidal curves
C_{r,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x^{r}-y^{s}=0\}
, for coprime integers
r,s
, are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
[
11
, Example 2.1]
. In fact, the normalization map

\mathbb{A}^{1}\to C_{r,s}\quad\text{defined as }\quad w\mapsto(w^{s},w^{r})

is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence of motivic spaces,

3.

Any vector bundle is, in fact, an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalence
[
158
, Example 2.2]
. More generally, any Zariski and Nisnevich locally trivial bundles with
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible fibers are seen to be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible. In fact, in
Chapter

4
we will see that the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility captures all the Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-bundles in low relative dimensions up to 2.

We shall encounter several other examples as we progress!

We will now recall the analogous notions of connectedness and path-connectedness in algebraic geometry.

2.1.5
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Connectedness

Recall that in topology, a space is
connected
if it cannot be written as a union of two proper non-empty open subsets. This is captured by the set of path components
\pi_{0}
that counts the number of distinct components of a space. Similarly, there is a notion for schemes, which is invariant under connected components.

Definition 11
.

Let
X
be a
k
-scheme. The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected component sheaf
of a scheme
X
is the Nisnevich sheaf associated to the presheaf

\mathrm{Sm}_{k}\ni U\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}(k)}(U,X)=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}[U,X]_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}

for all
U\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
and is denoted by
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)
. We say that a scheme
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected
if
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)\to\operatorname{Spec}k
is isomorphism of sheaves. Similarly,
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-disconnected
if it is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected.

In other words,
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)
can be seen as the sheafification of the presheaf of sections
X(U)
in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category. Note that for any space
X
,
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)
is only a (Nisnevich) sheaf of sets as in the case of classical topology.

Remark 2.1.19
.

For a simplicial presheaf
X
, one can also define the sheaf of
simplicial connected components
, denoted
\pi_{0}^{s}(X)
, given by the sheafification of

U\mapsto[U,X]_{\mathcal{H}o_{s}}

where
\mathcal{H}o_{s}
is the simplicial homotopy category (
[
18
, Definition 2.1.1]
). The two so-mentioned sheaves are related by the motivic localization functor
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
as follows:

\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)\simeq\pi_{0}^{s}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X))

This is sometimes used as an alternative definition of the sheaf of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected components.

The following shows the invariant nature of
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
that will be later useful when dealing with
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility.

Example 2.1.20
.

Every
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected smooth scheme
X
over a locally Henselian scheme
S
(for instance, the spectrum of a field) admits an
S
-point (
[
158
]
, Remark 2.5). In particular, having an
S
-point is an inherent property of a simplicial sheaf which is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant under
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences.

The following result, called the
Unstable 0-connectivity theorem
, is fundamental for these sheaves:

Theorem 2.1.21
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is a space, then the canonical map
\mathcal{F}\to\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{F})
is an epimorphism after Nisnevich sheafification. In particular, if
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
is a
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence of spaces and
\mathcal{F}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected, then
\mathcal{G}
is also
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected.

Proof.

This is
[
158
, §2, Corollary 3.22]
.
∎

\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance of
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}

Recall from
7
that a presheaf is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant if the induced map of the canonical projection is a bijection. By design, we have that the
presheaf
of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected components
U\mapsto[U,X]_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant. This is also true classically. Indeed, we have the set of path components parametrized by the interval
I=[0,1]
(which can be seen as a presheaf via
\pi_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Y\mapsto[Y,X]
, for locally contractible spaces
X,Y
) can be shown to be
I
-invariant. As the presheaf
\pi_{0}
is discrete, it implies that its sheafification stays
I
-invariant (see
[
158
, P. 87, Example 2]
). The analogous question in the motivic setting is the famous
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance conjecture of Morel:

Conjecture
.

The sheaf of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected components
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant.

The conjecture does hold true in some cases (cf.
[
43
, Remark 2.5]
), but it has been proven to fail in general (
[
22
]
,
[
27
, §4]
). However, we have the following universal property of
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
.

Lemma 2.1.22
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be any space and
\mathcal{F}
be any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant sheaf. Then any morphism
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{F}
uniquely factors via
\mathcal{X}\to\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{F})
.

Proof.

This is due to
[
27
, Lemma 2.8]
.
∎

Higher
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy sheaves

To conclude, note that subsequent to
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
, one can also define the higher
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy sheaves by taking maps from the simplicial motivic spheres as follows.

Definition 12
.

Let
(\mathfrak{X},x)
be any pointed space. Then the
i
-th
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy sheaf is the Nisnevich sheaf on
\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
associated with the presheaf

\mathrm{Sm}_{k}\ni U\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}(k)}(S^{i}\wedge U_{+},(\mathfrak{X},x))

for
U\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
. It is denoted by
\pi^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}_{i}(\mathfrak{X},x)
.

Like in topology, one shows that
\pi_{1}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
is Nisnevich sheaf of groups and
\pi_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
are all Nisnevich sheaf of Abelian groups for all
i\geq 2
(
[
160
]
). Recall that the Whitehead theorem states that any weak equivalence between CW-complexes is also a homotopy equivalence. The following is the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-analog of it due to
[
158
, §3, Proposition 2.14]
:

Proposition 2.1.23
.

Assume that
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{Y}
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected pointed spaces. Then a morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence if and only for any choice of base point
x\in\mathfrak{X}
with
y\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=f(x)
the induced morphism

\pi_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathfrak{X},x)\to\pi_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathfrak{Y},y)

is an isomorphism of Nisnevich sheaves.

Example 2.1.24
.

The higher
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy sheaves
\pi_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
(for
i\geq 1
) form primordial examples of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant sheaves (See
Theorem

B.0.2
).

2.1.6
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Rigid Schemes

In this section, we study certain special kinds of motivic spaces that can be understood as ’discrete’ schemes and have trivial higher
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopies.

Definition 13
.

A scheme
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\operatorname{Spec}k
is called
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid
if for any smooth scheme
U\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
, the induced morphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sm}_{k}}(U,X)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sm}_{k}}(U\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},X)

of the canonical projection
U\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to U
is a bijection.

Therefore, a scheme is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid if it is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local when viewed as a space and
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant when viewed as a presheaf. The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid schemes stay
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant under (reasonable) field extensions (
[
18
, Lemma 2.1.11]
):

Lemma 2.1.25
.

A smooth
k
-scheme
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid
if and only if for every finitely generated separable extension
L/k
the map

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Spec}L,X)\to\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Spec}L\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},X)

induced by the canonical projection
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\times\operatorname{Spec}L\to\operatorname{Spec}L
is a bijection.

Examples of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid schemes

Any 0-dimensional scheme over a field
k
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid (for a generalization of this result, see
4.1.3
). Abelian varieties, Smooth projective curves of positive genus, and smooth subvarieties and products of an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid variety all constitute examples of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid schemes (see also
[
18
,
19
,
43
]
). The following portray some common occurrences.

Example 2.1.26
.

The affine scheme
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid over any reduced commutative ring
R
.

Proof.

For simplicity, let us prove this over fields
R=k
, noting that the proof is essentially the same over any such
R
. Note that as a presheaf
\mathbb{G}_{m}=k[Y,Y^{-1}]
induces a functor
\mathbb{G}_{m}(U)=\mathcal{O}_{U}^{\times}
, the sheaf of units. Being a Zariski presheaf, it suffices to check the property (
13
) locally on affine schemes. Indeed, let
X=\operatorname{Spec}A
be any affine scheme where
A
is any finitely generated
k
-algebra, then we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sm}_{k}}(X,\mathbb{G}_{m})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sm}_{k}}(X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},\mathbb{G}_{m}).

Since
A
is reduced, we have that
\mathbb{G}_{m}(X)=\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}\cong A^{\times}
. On the other hand, since
X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\cong\operatorname{Spec}A\times_{k}\operatorname{Spec}(k[x])\cong\operatorname{Spec}(A\otimes_{k}k[x])\cong\operatorname{Spec}A[x]
, we have that
\mathbb{G}_{m}(A[x])\cong A^{\times}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Example 2.1.27
.

The projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid. This can be tested even on the simplest space
X=\operatorname{Spec}k
. This is because every embedding
\operatorname{Spec}k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
is isomorphic to a constant embedding, but there are clearly non-constant embeddings
\mathbb{A}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and as a result

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sm}_{k}}(\operatorname{Spec}k,\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sm}_{k}}(\operatorname{Spec}k\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k},\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k})

is not surjective (
Remark

2.1.7
). For the same reason,
\mathbb{A}^{1}
also cannot be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid.

Consider the functor
\mathrm{h}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathrm{Sm}_{k}\to\mathcal{H}(k)
that is obtained by composing the corresponding functors in the diagram
2.1.2
. Then we have that
\mathrm{h}
embeds the full subcategory of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid schemes fully faithfully into the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category (
[
18
, Lemma 2.1.9]
). This has the following consequences:

Lemma 2.1.28
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
be two
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid schemes, then we have the following:

1.

for any
U\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
, the canonical map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sm}_{k}}(U,X)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}(k)}(U,X)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=[U,X]_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 68.2866ptf\mapsto[f]

is a bijection,

2.

the canonical map
X\to\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)
is an isomorphism of (Nisnevich) sheaves. As a result,
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected if and only if
X\cong\operatorname{Spec}k
,

3.

\mathrm{h}(X)\simeq_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathrm{h}(Y)\iff X\cong Y
, that is, two
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid schemes are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weakly equivalent if and only if they are isomorphic as
k
-schemes.

Local nature

\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid schemes demonstrate one of the several stark dichotomies that exist between classical topology and algebraic geometry. Classically, every smooth manifold admits a local base that consists of smooth contractible neighborhoods, and thereby, the local system is a collection of connected spaces. In contrast, every scheme that is locally of finite type has a base that built out of products of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
s (
[
43
, Lemma 2.3]
), in particular, they are product of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid varieties, whence in general, is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected unless it is trivial (
Lemma

2.1.28
(2)). Nevertheless, they are important as they form the building blocks of the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-model structure:

Lemma 2.1.29
.

A smooth scheme of finite type is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local fibrant if and only if it is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid.

Proof.

[
19
, Lemma 4.1.8]
.
∎

2.1.7
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Chain Connectedness

We now focus on the analogous notion of path connectedness in algebraic geometry. Recall from topology that a space
X
is
path connected
if any two given points
x,y
can be joined by a map
\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}[0,1]\to X
such that
\gamma(0)=x
,
\gamma(1)=y
. The analogous notion in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy, established by Asok-Morel (
[
18
]
), is that of being "connected by the images (chains) of the affine line".

Definition 14
.

Given
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
and a finitely generated separable extension of
L/k
, and points
x_{0},x_{1}\in X(L)
an
elementary
\mathbb{A}^{1}
equivalence
between
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
is a morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}\to X
such that
f(0)=x_{0}
and
f(1)=x_{1}
. Two points
x,y\in X(L)
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-equivalent if they are equivalent with respect to the equivalence relation generated by elementary
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-equivalence.

Definition 15
.

A
k
-scheme
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected
if for any finitely generated separable field extension
L/k
,
X(L)
is non-empty and for all rational points
x,y\in X(L)
, there exists an integer
N
and a sequence of points
x=x_{0},\dots,x_{N-1},x_{N}=y\in X(L)
and an elementary
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences
f_{1},\dots,f_{N}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{L}\to X
such that
f_{i}(0)=x_{i-1}
and
f_{i}(1)=x_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq N
: any two points can be connected by the images of a chain of maps from
\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

Alternatively, one says that a variety
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected if the set of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-equivalence classes of
L
-points
X(L)/\sim
consists of exactly one element.

Definition 16
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
. Then the
sheaf of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected component of
X
is defined as

\pi_{0}^{ch}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\pi_{0}^{s}(\operatorname{Sing}_{*}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)).

We say that
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected if
\pi_{0}^{ch}(X)\simeq\operatorname{Spec}k
.

Various relations have been studied between
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
and
\pi_{0}^{ch}
(for example, see
[
18
,
27
]
). We will list a few that lie within the scope of our script.

Lemma 2.1.30
.

For
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
, the map
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\pi_{0}^{ch}(X)\to\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)
is an epimorphism of Nisnevich sheaves.

In topology, any path-connected space is also connected. But the converse is not true (e.g., topologist’s sine curve). The following facts show us the striking affinity for schemes as well:

Proposition 2.1.31
.

If
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected, then it is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected.

Proof.

[
19
, Proposition 4.2.4]
.
∎

Asok and Morel also conjectured (
[
18
, Conjecture 2.2.8]
) that for smooth schemes over
k
, the above map
\phi
is an isomorphism, that is, it is also a monomorphism. They also observed that this should hold provided
\operatorname{Sing}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}_{*}(X)
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local for a space
X
and showed that this is indeed true for smooth proper varieties (
[
18
, Corollary 2.4.4]
). However, this has now proven to be false in generality due to the work of
[
27
, §4.1]
. This tells us that being
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected is indeed stronger than being
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected.

Definition 17
.

We say that an
n
-dimensional smooth
k
-variety
X
is
covered by affine spaces
if
X
admits an open affine cover by finitely many copies of
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
such that the intersection
4
4

4

this is automatic if
k
is an infinite field
of any two copies of
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
has a
k
-point.

Example 2.1.32
.

The following are some (non-)examples of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected varieties:

•

Any variety that is covered by affine spaces in the sense of
17
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected. These include rational smooth proper varieties of dimension
\leq 2
, smooth proper toric varieties, and generalized flag varieties for connected reductive groups over
k
.

•

The Koras-Russell threefolds (
[
66
, Example 2.28]
) and Generalized Koras-Russell varieties are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected (cf.
Lemma

5.3.7
).

•

The Ramanujam surface
Example

1.2.2
and the tom Dieck-Petrie surfaces
Example

1.2.3
are not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected.

Generalizing the sheaf of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected components, the authors in
[
27
]
construct the
universal
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant sheaf
in the following sense: Set
\mathcal{S}(-)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\pi_{0}^{ch}(-)
, then for any Nisnevich sheaf
\mathcal{F}
, the canonical morphism
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{F})
is surjective and so consider

\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathcal{S}^{2}(\mathcal{F})\to\dots\to\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathcal{F})\to\dots

where
\mathcal{S}^{n+1}(\mathcal{F})
is inductively defined as
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathcal{F}))
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The
universal
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant sheaf is defined as

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\end{subarray}}\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathcal{F})

Then they show
[
27
, §2]
that for any sheaf
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant and for any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant sheaf
\mathcal{G}
, any morphism
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
uniquely factors via
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})
. Moreover, if
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{F})
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant, then the canonical map
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})\to\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{F})
is an isomorphism.

2.1.8
Functoriality

Given any category, a vital tool to establish is its functoriality with respect to its morphisms. The functoriality in motivic homotopy theory is well-established and facilitates handling certain problems from a categorical viewpoint. For a comprehensive overview of functoriality, we recommend the following resources:
[
158
, §3]
,
[
21
,
45
]
. For other recent accounts, one can refer to
[
104
,
75
,
26
,
174
]
. In alignment with the material presented in our script, we will restrict ourselves to the base change functoriality, which we shall apply to the special case when the spaces are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible.

The Base Change Functor

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
be a morphism of base schemes
5
5

5

By a base scheme, we mean a Noetherian separated scheme of finite Krull dimension
. Then the pullback along
f
defines a functor

\displaystyle f^{\bullet}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathrm{Sm}_{S}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Sm}_{T}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptU\hskip 5.69054pt\longmapsto U_{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=U\times_{S}T.

Precomposition with
f^{\bullet}
, we obtain another colimit preserving functor at the level of motivic spaces defined as

\displaystyle f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Spc}_{T}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spc}_{S}

\displaystyle\hskip 19.91692pt\mathcal{F}\hskip 14.22636pt\longmapsto\mathcal{F}\circ f^{\bullet}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=U\mapsto\mathcal{F}(U_{T}).

By left Kan extension (cf.
Section

A.2.5
), we see that
f_{*}
admits a left adjoint
f^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Spc}_{S}\to\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
on the level of motivic spaces

f^{*}\dashv f_{*}

which is a strict symmetric monoidal category and preserves limits. Since every (pointed) motivic space is a colimit of representable motivic spaces (cf.
Corollary

A.2.16
), we can characterize
f^{*}
by the following formula

\displaystyle f^{*}(X_{+})=(X\times_{S}T)_{+}

(2.1)

for all
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
.

Smooth Base Change

If furthermore,
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
is a smooth morphism of base schemes, then composition with
f
defines a functor
f_{\bullet}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathrm{Sm}_{T}\to\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
. Precomposition with this functor defines another functor
f^{\bigstar}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Spc}_{S}\to\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
which itself admits a left adjoint
f_{\sharp}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Spc}_{T}\to\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
via (enriched) Kan extension, that is

f_{\sharp}\dashv f^{\bigstar}\dashv f_{*}

Again, using the fact that any motivic space is a colimit of representable ones,
f_{\sharp}
can be characterized by the formula

f_{\sharp}(X\xrightarrow{g}T)_{+}=(X\xrightarrow{g}T\xrightarrow{f}S)

for every
X\in\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
. In other words,
f_{\sharp}
is simply induced by the forgetful functor
\mathcal{U}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathrm{Sm}_{S}\to\mathrm{Sm}_{T}
. On the other hand, if
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Z\to S
is a morphism, then the canonical
S
-morphism
Z\times_{S}T
defines a map
B(Z)\to f_{*}f^{\bigstar}B(Z)
which is natural in
Z
and
B\in\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
, and consequently, a natural transformation
\operatorname{Id}_{\operatorname{Spc}_{S}}\to f_{*}\circ f^{\bigstar}
. The following lemma says these two functors agree whenever
f
is a smooth morphism:

Lemma 2.1.33
.

If
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
is a smooth morphism of base schemes, the adjoint

f^{*}\to f^{\bigstar}

of the natural transformation
\operatorname{Id}_{\operatorname{Spc}_{S}}\to f_{*}\circ f^{\bigstar}
is a natural transformation.

Lemma 2.1.34
.

If
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
is a morphism of base schemes. Then
f^{*}
preserves finite objects, dualizable objects. Furthermore, if
f
is smooth, then
f_{\sharp}
preserves finite objects.

Proof.

[
174
, Lemma 3.11]
.
∎

Convention recap:
For a morphism of
S
-schemes
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
, some authors (by abusive of terminology) use that
f
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\mathcal{H}(S)
or that
f
is an isomorphism of motivic spaces. We have made a clear distinction in the following sense: we will adopt to say either that
f
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
or equivalently, that
f
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{H}(S)
.

The following crucial fact gives us the concise base change functoriality relating the category of motivic spaces and the associated
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category. We will denote by
Lf^{*}
(resp.
RF_{*}
) the left (resp. right) derived Quillen functor on the level of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy categories (cf.
Section

A.2.2
).

Proposition 2.1.35
.

[
63
]

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
be a morphism of schemes, let
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
and
\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
be the category of motivic spaces endowed with their respective local projective model structures, and let
\mathcal{H}(S)
and
\mathcal{H}(T)
be their respective localizations with their
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local projective model structure. Then the following holds:

1.

The adjoint pair
(f^{*},f_{*})
induces Quillen adjunctions

\operatorname{Spc}_{S}\mathrel{{\mathop{\underset{f^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\limits}}\operatorname{Spc}_{T}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{H}(S)\mathrel{{\mathop{\underset{Lf^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\limits}}\mathcal{H}(T).

2.

If
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
is smooth, then the adjoint pair
(f_{\sharp},f^{\bigstar})
induces Quillen adjunctions

\operatorname{Spc}_{T}\mathrel{{\mathop{\underset{f_{\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}}\limits}}\operatorname{Spc}_{S}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{H}(T)\mathrel{{\mathop{\underset{Rf_{\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}}\limits}}\mathcal{H}(S).

Proof.

The fact that
(f^{*},f_{*})
(resp.
(f_{\sharp},f^{\bigstar})
when
f
is smooth) induces a Quillen adjunction between the corresponding model categories is a consequence of using the projective model structure, and this follows from
[
136
, Proposition A.2.8.7]
. Indeed, the first assertion follows from the observation that if
U_{\bullet}\to X
is a Nisnevich hypercover of a smooth
S
-scheme
X
, then
(U_{T})_{\bullet}\to X_{T}
is a Nisnevich hypercover of
X_{T}
and that for every smooth
S
-scheme
X
,
(X\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S})\cong X_{T}\times_{T}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{T}
which implies by
[
136
, Proposition A.3.7.9]
that the Quillen adjunction

\operatorname{Spc}_{S}\mathrel{{\mathop{\underset{\mathrm{id}\circ f^{*}}{\longrightarrow}}\limits}}\operatorname{Spc}_{T}

induces a Quillen adjunction

\mathcal{H}(S)\mathrel{{\mathop{\underset{\mathrm{id}\circ f_{*}\circ\mathrm{id}}{\longrightarrow}}\limits}}\mathcal{H}(T)

The second assertion follows for the same reasons after observing that when
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
is smooth, a Nisnevich hypercover
U_{\bullet}\to X
of a smooth
T
-scheme
X
is again a Nisnevich hypercover of
X
considered as a smooth
S
-scheme via the composition with
u
and that for every smooth
T
-scheme
X
,
X\times_{T}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{T}\cong X\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
as schemes over
S
.
∎

We now present several consequences of this base change functoriality, which will play a crucial role in
Chapter

4
.

Corollary 2.1.36
.

[
63
]

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
be a morphism of schemes. Then the following holds:

1.

For every morphism
u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
between smooth
S
-schemes which is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
, the morphism
u_{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y_{T}\to X_{T}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
.

2.

If
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
is smooth, then every morphism
u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
between smooth
T
-schemes which an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
is also an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
.

Proof.

For the first assertion, we consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spc}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spc}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lf^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

The functor
f^{*}
:
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}\to\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
maps the presheaves associated to the smooth
S
-schemes
X
and
Y
to the presheaves associated to the smooth
T
-schemes
X_{T}
and
Y_{T}
respectively and maps the natural transformation associated to
u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
onto that associated to
u_{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y_{T}\to X_{T}
. On the other hand, by construction of the projective model structure on
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
, the presheaves associated to
X
and
Y
are cofibrant objects of
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
, whence of
\mathcal{H}(S)
by definition of left Bousfield localization. Thus,
u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences between cofibrant objects of
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
, and since
f^{*}
is a left Quillen functor (that is, using the adjunction
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
), it follows from Brown’s lemma (see
Lemma

A.2.27
) that the image
u_{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y_{T}\to X_{T}
of
u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
. The second assertion follows from the same reasoning using the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spc}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spc}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lf_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

and the fact that
f_{\sharp}
maps the presheaves associated to the smooth
T
-schemes
X
and
Y
to those associated to
X
and
Y
considered as smooth
S
-schemes via the composition with
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
.
∎

The following portrays that relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences behave well under smooth base change.

Corollary 2.1.37
.

[
63
]

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
and
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Z\to Y
be smooth morphisms between smooth schemes over a scheme
S
. If
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Z\to Y
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{Y}
and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{X}
, then
h=f\circ g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Z\to X
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{X}
.

Proof.

Since
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
is a smooth morphism,
Corollary

2.1.36
(2) implies that
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Z\to Y
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{X}
and the assertion then follows from the "two out of three" axiom for weak equivalence in a model category (cf.
34
[2.]).
∎

Remark 2.1.38
.

In the setting of
Corollary

2.1.37
, the implication "
f
and
g
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{X}

\Rightarrow

g
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{Y}
" does not hold in general, see
Example

2.1.45
and
Example

4.2.13
.

2.1.9
Base Change under
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility

We now apply these results to the case when the space is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, which we will need in both
Chapter

4
and
Chapter

5
.

Proposition 2.1.39
.

[
63
]

Let
u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
S
-scheme. Then for any arbitrary morphism of schemes
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
, the induced map
u_{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X_{T}\to T
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
T
-scheme, where we have denoted the scheme-theoretic pullback by
X_{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\times_{S}T
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward application of
Corollary

2.1.36
(1).
∎

This proposition has several interesting implications, as presented in
[
63
]
:

Corollary 2.1.40
.

Let
X
be a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme over a field
k
. Then for every field extension
k\subset L
(separable or not),
X_{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(k)}\operatorname{Spec}(L)
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
L
-scheme.

Proof.

A proof for the case when
L/k
is a finite separable field extension is given in
[
183
, Proposition 6.2.15]
. But this in fact extends to any arbitrary field extensions due to
2.1.39
.
∎

Corollary 2.1.41
.

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
S
-scheme. Then for every point
s
of
S
(closed or not) with residue field
\kappa(s)
, the scheme theoretic fiber
X_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\times_{S}\operatorname{Spec}\kappa(s)
of
f
over the point
s
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
\kappa(s)
-scheme.

Corollary 2.1.42
.

Let
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Z\hookrightarrow S
be a closed immersion of schemes. If
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
S
-scheme, then
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\times_{S}Z\to Z
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
Z
-scheme.

The following example complementing
Corollary

2.1.40
illustrates the fact that the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility property does not necessarily descend under arbitrary morphisms
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
:

Example 2.1.43
.

Let
k
be an imperfect field of characteristic
p>0
with perfection
k^{\mathrm{perf}}
and let
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Spec}k^{\mathrm{perf}}\to\operatorname{Spec}k
be the associated base change morphism. Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\operatorname{Spec}k
be a non-trivial
k
-form of the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
. Since the affine line does not admit non-trivial separable form
[
176
]
, it follows that the base change
X_{k^{\mathrm{perf}}}
of
X
by
g
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k^{\mathrm{perf}}}
, whence that
X_{k^{\mathrm{perf}}}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
k^{\mathrm{perf}}
-scheme. On the other hand,
X
is not an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
k
-scheme. Indeed,
X
being a non-trivial
k
-form of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
, it follows from
[
176
, Lemma 1.1]
that either
X(k)=\emptyset
or
X
is isomorphic to the complement in the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
of a closed point
x
whose residue field
\kappa(x)
is a purely inseparable extension of
k
. In the first case, the non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of
X
over
k
follows from
Example

2.1.20
, and in the second case, it follows from
[
3
, Theorem 4.1]
which asserts that such a non-trivial form
X
has a non-trivial Picard group (cf.
Example

2.1.52
).

Remark 2.1.44
.

[
141
, Remark 3.17]

If
u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
, then its base change with respect to an arbitrary morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
is recovered as that of the (usual) scheme-theoretic pullback. As a consequence, this implies crucially that the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sm}_{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sm}_{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spc}_{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spc}_{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}(T)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{mot,S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{mot,T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lf^{*}}

commutes. Recall the notations from
Section

2.1.8
. Here,
Lf^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Spc}_{S}\to\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
is the induced derived pullback functor on the respective
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy categories and
\mathbb{L}_{mot,T}
(resp.
\mathbb{L}_{mot,S}
) is the corresponding motivic localization functor defined on
\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
(resp.
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
). In other words, for a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme
u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
, the derived pullback can be retrieved as the scheme-theoretic pullback and vice-versa:

\begin{split}Lf^{*}(\mathbb{L}_{mot,S}(X))&\simeq Lf^{*}(\operatorname{Id}_{S})\quad(X\ \text{is}\ \mathbb{A}^{1}\text{-contractible})\\
&\simeq\operatorname{Id}_{T}\quad(\text{functoriality of}\ Lf^{*})\\
&\simeq\mathbb{L}_{mot,T}(T)\\
&\simeq\mathbb{L}_{mot,T}(X_{T})\quad(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{pullbackfunctor})\\
Lf^{*}(\mathbb{L}_{mot,S}((X))&\simeq\mathbb{L}_{mot,T}(f^{*}(X))\quad(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{pshv-pullback}})\\
\end{split}

(2.2)

2.1.10
Relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility of Smooth Schemes

We will now definitively clarify the meaning of the phrase
relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility
that we extensively use in the chapters ahead.

Definition 18
.

For any
k
-schemes
X,Y\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
, we say that a morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
is
relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
if
f
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in the category of motivic
Y
-spaces
\operatorname{Spc}_{Y}
or equivalently,
X
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category of
Y
, denoted
\mathcal{H}(Y)
.

To emphasize this relativeness, we shall call the morphism
f
a
relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence
of motivic spaces. Being "relatively"
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible is a subtle notion, and the need for such a distinction in a general setting is demonstrated by the following example. Observe also that this example is a non-pathological and, in fact, a fundamental instance:

Example 2.1.45
.

[
141
, Example 3.15]

Consider the smooth morphism of
k
-schemes

\begin{split}&g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Z\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\\
&(x,y)\mapsto x^{2}y^{2}+y\end{split}\hskip 34.1433pt\hbox to164.78pt{\vbox to52.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 82.39139pt\lower-26.33943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(2.3)

We have that both
Y
and
Z
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over
X
. Therefore,
g
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
. But
g
cannot be an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}}
. Suppose it was so, then by
Corollary

2.1.41
, every fiber of
f
over a point of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
would be an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme over the corresponding residue field. But observe that the fiber of
f
over
0
is the disjoint union of a copy of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
and a copy of
\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
. The latter component is not even
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected (
Example

2.1.26
) in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
and so the fiber cannot be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected, whence cannot be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible (see
Example

2.1.54
). Therefore, we conclude that
g
cannot be a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence.

Retreating to
Corollary

2.1.36
(1), one is intrigued to ask the following: for a morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
between smooth
S
-schemes, under what assumptions is there a (suitable) morphism
u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to S
such that whenever the base change
f_{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y_{T}\to X_{T}
of
f
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{T}
, then
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. The
Example

2.1.43
illustrates that faithful flatness of
g
is, for instance, not sufficient. Alternatively, we can ask the following question:

Question 2.1.46
.

For a smooth morphism of schemes
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
, does the property that all fibers of
f
over points of
s
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over the corresponding residue fields imply that
X
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
S
-scheme?

We note that such a desired property (
2.1.46
) holds true in the stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
\mathrm{SH}(S)
owing to the fact that the family of functors
i_{s}^{!}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathrm{SH}(S)\to\mathrm{SH}(\kappa(s))
,
s\in S
, is conservative (see e.g.
[
54
, B20]
). A recent remark due to
[
9
, Proposition 2.1]
points out that this property is, to our much surprise, also true unstably)! In fact, the solution to
2.1.46
follows as a consequence of the
gluing theorem
(
[
158
, Theorem 2.21]
), and this remains true for any Grothendieck topology that is at least as fine as the Nisnevich topology.

Theorem 2.1.47
.

Let
S
be any locally Noetherian scheme of finite Krull dimension. Then for any motivic space
\mathcal{F}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{\#}j^{*}\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{*}i^{*}(\mathcal{F})}}

is a homotopy cocartesian in
\mathcal{H}(S)
. Here,
j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\hookrightarrow S
is associated with the open immersion of an open subscheme
U\subset S
.

Proof.

This is
[
158
, Theorem 2.21]
.
∎

The following is a powerful consequence of
Theorem

2.1.47
.

Proposition 2.1.48
.

[
63
]

For a scheme
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
smooth over a Noetherian scheme
S
of finite Krull dimension, the following properties are equivalent:

1.

The morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak-equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
.

2.

For every point
s
of
S
with residue field
\kappa(s)
, the scheme theoretic fiber of
f
over
s
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
\kappa(s)
-scheme.

Proof.

Since our hypotheses differ slightly from those in
[
9
, Proposition 2.1]
, we will now sketch the argument. The implication
(1)\implies(2)
follows from
Corollary

2.1.41
. Let us now prove the converse: given a smooth scheme
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
over a scheme
S
of finite Krull dimension, we want to show that if
f_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X_{s}\to\operatorname{Spec}\kappa(s)
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\kappa(s)}
for every point
s\in S
, then
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. We argue by induction on
\dim S
. The case
\dim S=0
reduces to the observation that a smooth morphism

X\longrightarrow S,\qquad S=\operatorname{Spec}R

with
R
Artinian local, is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence if and only if

X\times_{S}S_{\mathrm{red}}\longrightarrow S_{\mathrm{red}}

is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence (see
Remark

2.1.49
). Assume the claim holds for all schemes of dimension
\leq d
and let
\dim S=d+1
. Since the property for
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
to be an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
is local on the base
S
with respect to Nisnevich hypercovers, it suffices to verify the assertion in the case where
S
is the spectrum of an local ring
R
of dimension
d
, which we can further assume to be Henselian (this is due to
[
25
, Proposition A.3]
).

Let
j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\to S
be the open immersion and
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\{s\}\to S
be the complementary closed immersion. The open immersion
j
provides us an adjoint pair
(j^{*}\dashv j_{*})
given by
j^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}(S)\to\mathcal{H}(U)
, its right adjoint
j_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}(U)\to\mathcal{H}(S)
. Furthermore, since
j
is an open immersion (whence a smooth morphism), it provides another adjoint pair
(j_{\sharp}\dashv j^{*})
given by
j^{*}
and its left adjoint
j_{\sharp}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}(U)\to\mathcal{H}(S)
. Similarly, the closed immersion
i
provides us an adjoint pair
(i^{*}\dashv i_{*})
given by
i^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}(S)\to\mathcal{H}(\kappa(s))
and its right adjoint
i_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}(\kappa(s))\to\mathcal{H}(S)
.

Recall from
Section

2.1.8
that we have
j_{\sharp}j^{*}X=j_{\sharp}(X_{U})
, where
X\times_{S}U\xrightarrow{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}U\xrightarrow{j}S
is viewed as a smooth
S
-scheme via the composition
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\times_{S}U\to U
with the open immersion
j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\to S
. We have a canonical map
\widetilde{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}j_{\sharp}j^{*}X\to X
that corresponds to the immersion
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\times_{S}U\to X
and another canonical map
\widetilde{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}j_{\sharp}j^{*}X\to j_{\sharp}U
that corresponds to the projection
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\times_{S}U\to U
. On the other hand, the base change by
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\{s\}\to S
induces a canonical morphism
\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to i_{*}i^{*}X
in
\mathcal{H}(S)
and we trivially have a canonical map
\widetilde{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}j_{\sharp}U\to i_{*}i^{*}X
. These maps assemble into a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\sharp}j^{*}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\sharp}U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{*}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\psi}}

(2.4)

in
\mathcal{H}(S)
. Since
U\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\backslash\{s\}
has dimension strictly smaller than
d
, the restriction
f_{U}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X_{U}\to U
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\mathcal{H}(U)
by the induction hypothesis and so

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spec}\kappa(s)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

we have that
j_{\sharp}(f_{U})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}j_{\sharp}(j^{*}X)=j_{\sharp}X_{U}\to j_{\sharp}U
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\mathcal{H}(S)
. It follows that the canonically induced map of presheaves

\displaystyle X\xrightarrow{\simeq}j_{\sharp}U\cup_{j_{\sharp}X_{U}}X

(2.5)

is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. On the other hand, the proof of
[
158
, Theorem 2.21]
shows that
Eq.

2.4
is a homotopy co-cartesian and so the canonically induced map of presheaves

\displaystyle j_{\sharp}U\cup_{j_{\sharp}X_{U}}X\xrightarrow{\simeq}i_{*}i^{*}X

(2.6)

is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. Combining
Eq.

2.5
and
Eq.

2.6
, we see that the induced map

\displaystyle X\xrightarrow{\simeq}i_{*}i^{*}(X)

(2.7)

is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. But then we have
i_{*}i^{*}(X)=i_{*}(X_{s})
and by assumption
f_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X_{s}\to\operatorname{Spec}(\kappa(s))
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\kappa(s)}
which then implies that the motivic space
i_{*}(X_{s})
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
due to functoriality. The conclusion that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
follows from the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence of
Eq.

2.7
.
∎

Remark 2.1.49
.

Suppose
S
is a base scheme and let
i\colon S_{\mathrm{red}}\hookrightarrow S
be the reduction (a closed immersion defined by a nilpotent ideal, whose open complement is empty). Then the pullback
i^{\ast}\colon H(S)\longrightarrow H(S_{\mathrm{red}})
along
i
is an equivalence of unstable motivic homotopy categories on account of the Morel-Voevodsky localization theorem
[
158
, Theorem 3.2.21]
.

Remark 2.1.50
.

The cautious reader might have noticed a subtlety: our model structure (projective version) of
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
is different from that of the model structure used in
[
158
]
(injective version). While it is a good catch, also note that both of these model structures are give rise to the same
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category, essentially because the class of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences are the same in both these structures (one may also wish to refer to
[
107
, Theorem 2.2]
or
[
71
, Proposition 8.1]
).

Formal consequences of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility

Example 2.1.51
.

If the base field
k
admits an embedding into
\mathbb{C}
, then any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme is topologically contractible (
[
11
, Lemma 2.5]
).

Example 2.1.52
.

An
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme has a trivial Picard group. In fact, every vector bundle on an affine
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme has to be trivial (
[
11
, Corollary 6.2]
).

Example 2.1.53
.

Any non-trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid scheme (e.g.,
\mathbb{G}_{m}
) cannot be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, a fact attributed to the non-triviality of the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected component sheaf.

Example 2.1.54
.

Any smooth
k
-variety
X
of dimension
n
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible if and only if
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected and
\pi_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X,x)
is trivial, for all
0\leq i\leq n
. This is a natural consequence of
2.1.23
. Hence, such
X
admits an
S
-point (cf.
Example

2.1.20
).

Example 2.1.55
.

Any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible smooth affine scheme
X
has the integral motivic cohomology of the ground field, i.e.,
\mathrm{H}^{*,*}(X,\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathrm{H}^{*,*}(\operatorname{Spec}k,\mathbb{Z})
. In particular, in relation to the higher Chow groups of Bloch, all the Chow groups vanish for these schemes
\mathrm{CH}^{*}(X)\simeq\operatorname{Spec}k
(
[
11
, Corollary 6.1]
).

Homotopy purity

Purity isomorphism is an indispensable tool in the light of computation in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory and connects it to algebraic
K
