-theory and motivic cohomology. It is an analog of the tubular neighborhood theorem in differential topology and was established by Morel-Voevodsky
[
158
, Theorem 2.23]
.

Theorem 2.1.56
.

Let
S
be any Noetherian scheme of finite Krull dimension. Let
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed embedding of smooth schemes over
S
. Then there is a canonical isomorphism in
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}(k)
of the form

\frac{X}{X\backslash i(Z)}\simeq\operatorname{Th}(N_{Z}X)

where
\mathrm{Th(-)}
is the Thom space associated to the normal bundle
N_{Z}X
of
Z
in
X
.

A quick illustration: suppose a scheme
X
has an open cover
\{U\}
for which the normal bundle trivializes, and then we have that

U/(U\backslash(U\cap Z))\simeq(U\cap Z)_{+}\wedge(\mathbb{A}^{n}/\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\})

The following lemma will be useful for us later in
Chapter

5
.

Lemma 2.1.57
.

Let
Z\subset X
be any closed smooth subscheme of a smooth scheme
X
. Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to X^{\prime}
be a morphism such that
X^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=f^{-1}(Z)
are smooth schemes and the induced map
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}N_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime}\to f^{*}N_{Z}X
is an isomorphism. Let
\phi^{\prime}
be the Thom space defined by
\phi
. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}/(X^{\prime}\backslash Z^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X/(X\backslash Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Th}(N_{Z^{\prime}}X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Th}(N_{Z}X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}

commutes.

Proof.

This is due to
[
207
, Lemma 2.1]
.
∎

Motivic excision

The following theorem portrays an analog to the
excision style
result for
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy groups for smooth schemes, which we shall need in
Chapter

5
.

Theorem 2.1.58
.

Let
k
be an infinite field. Suppose
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected, and
j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\to X
is an open immersion of an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected scheme whose closed complement is everywhere of codimension
d\geq 2
. Fix a base point
x\in U(k)
. If furthermore
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-
m
-connected, for
m\geq d-3
, then the canonical morphism

j_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\pi_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(U,x)\to\pi_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X,x)

is an isomorphism for
0\leq i\leq d-2
and an epimorphism for
i=d-1
.

Proof.

This is due to
[
13
, Theorem 4.1]
.
∎

2.2
Milnor-Witt
K
-Theory

The objective of this short section is to introduce the fundamentals of the Milnor-Witt
K
-theory, only highlighting the basic features. We redirect the inquisitive readers to
Section

B.0.2
for an expansive background. Recall that the
Milnor Witt
K
-theory
is essentially an extension of Milnor’s
K
-theory
K^{\operatorname{M}}_{*}(F)
, which wisely mixes the generators and relations of Milnor
K
-theory and the Grothendieck Witt ring. It is isomorphic to the Grothendieck ring (
Section

B.0.2
) in degree 0 and the Witt ring (
Section

B.0.2
) in negative degrees.

Definition 19
.

Let
F
be any arbitrary field with its units
F^{\times}
. The
Milnor-Witt
K
-theory
or the
Milnor-Witt ring
, denoted as
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(F)
, is the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded associative unital ring freely generated by the formal symbols
[a]
of degree +1, for
a\in F^{\times}
and a symbol
\eta
of degree -1 (called the
Hopf element
) subjected to the following relations:

1.

[a][1-a]=0
, for any
a\neq 0,1
(Steinberg relation)

2.

[ab]=[a]+[b]+\eta[a][b]
, for any
a,b\in F^{\times}

3.

\eta[a]=[a]\eta
, for any
a\in F^{\times}

4.

\eta(\eta[-1]+2)=0

For units
a_{i}\in F^{\times}
, the symbols
[a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}]
usually means the product of
[a_{1}]\cdot\dots\cdot[a_{n}]
(for
i=1,\dots,n
) and
\eta[a]
means the product
\eta\cdot[a]
. For
a\in F^{\times}
, the class
\langle a\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1+\eta[a]
. By construction, the association

F\mapsto K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(F)

is functorial. Recall from
Section

B.0.2
that the Milnor Witt
K
-theory is related to the Milnor
K
-theory (
[
151
]
) by killing
\eta
, that is,
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}/(\eta)=K^{\operatorname{M}}_{*}(F)
. By convention, the image of the
n
-tuple product
[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
under this map is denoted by
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\}
. We will need the description of the following element of
GW(F)
, which in particular, states that the Milnor-Witt
K
-theory is
\epsilon
-commutative for
\epsilon\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=-\langle-1\rangle
. For all
n\geq 0
(see also
Lemma

B.0.13
), we have that

n_{\epsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle(-1)^{i-1}\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad(-n)_{\epsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\epsilon n_{\epsilon}.

Residue homomorphism

Briefly speaking, there is a homomorphism of (cochain) complex called the
residue homomorphism
with coefficients in the Milnor-Witt sheaf. Let
F
be a field and
v\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}F\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
be a discrete valuation with residue field
\kappa(v)
and valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{v}
and choose a uniformizing parameter
\pi=\pi_{v}
of
v
(see
Section

B.0.3
for more background).

Theorem 2.2.1
.

There is a unique homomorphism of graded Abelian groups

\partial^{\pi}_{v}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(F)\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*-1}(\kappa(v))

of degree -1 such that
\partial_{v}^{\pi}
commutes wit the multiplication by
\eta
and

1.

\partial_{v}^{\pi}([\pi,u_{1},\dots,u_{n}])=[\bar{u_{1}},\dots,\bar{u_{n}}]

2.

\partial_{v}^{\pi}([u_{1},\dots,u_{n}])=0

We call such a
\partial_{v}^{\pi}
the
residue homomorphism
. It turns out that the homomorphism
\partial_{v}^{\pi}
depends on the chosen valuation
\pi
. This is the reason for
[
160
]
to introduce the theory of
twisted Milnor Witt
K
-theory
. After undergoing certain modifications as explained in
Section

B.0.3
, we can now conveniently set the
Milnor-Witt
K
-theory twisted by
L
as

K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F,L)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[{F^{\times}}]}\mathbb{Z}[L^{\times}]

The twisted homomorphism

\partial_{v}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(F,L)\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*-1}(\kappa(v),(\mathfrak{m}_{v}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}_{v})^{*}\otimes L)

is given by
\partial_{v}(\alpha\otimes l)=\partial_{v}^{\pi}(\alpha)\otimes\overline{\pi}^{*}\otimes l
, for
l\in L
well-defined and is independent of the uniformizer
\pi
(cf.
Lemma

B.0.21
). Here,
L
is any line bundle and
(\mathfrak{m}_{v}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}_{v})^{*}
represents the relative tangent space, which, by definition, is the dual of the relative cotangent space
\mathfrak{m}_{v}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}_{v}
(seen as
\kappa(v)
-vector spaces).

Motivic Brouwer degree

An important invariant from the Milnor-Witt
K
-theory is the motivic version of the Hopf-Brouwer map. It gives a description of the non-trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy sheaf of the motivic sphere
\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
(for
n\geq 2
) as a consequence of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Hurewicz theorem.

Theorem 2.2.2
.

[
160
, Theorem 6.40]

For
n\geq 2
, we have a canonical isomorphism of strictly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant sheaves

\pi^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}_{n-1}(\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\})\cong\pi_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}((\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge n})\cong K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}

The advantage of such a theorem is that it helps us to understand the endomorphism ring
[\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\},\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}]_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
, for any
n\geq 2
.

Corollary 2.2.3
.

[
160
, Corollary 6.43]

Let
(n,i)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and
(m,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
be pairs of integers. For
n\geq 2
, we have a canonical isomorphism:

Hom_{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}(k)}(S^{m}\wedge(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\wedge j},S^{n}\wedge(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\wedge i})=\begin{cases}0&if $m<n$.\\
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{i-j}(k)&if $m=n$ and $i>0$\\
0&if $m=n$, $j>0$ and $i=0$\\
\mathbb{Z}&if $m=n$ and $j=i=0$\end{cases}

Coupling the above results, one obtains that

[\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\},\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}]_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}=K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{0}(k).

If
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
is morphism in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
, then the class
[f]
in
K_{0}^{\operatorname{MW}}(k)\cong\operatorname{GW}(k)
is called the
motivic Brouwer degree of
f
. To compute this degree, we can use the following cohomological technique.

Lemma 2.2.4
.

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
be a morphism in
\mathcal{H}(k)
. Then
f
is an isomorphism if and only if

f^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H^{n-1}(\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\},K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n})\to H^{n-1}(\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\},K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n})

is an isomorphism of Milnor-Witt
K
-theory groups. In other words, the degree of
f
is 1.

Proof.

The reader is redirected to
[
60
, Lemma 2.2]
.
∎

Chapter 3
Affine Algebraic Geometry

Chapter Summary

We dedicate this chapter to appreciating the main results of our script from the perspective of affine algebraic geometry. We will present a survey of some of the fundamental open problems that we will later connect with the context of motivic homotopy theory. This chapter begins with an overview of problems in
Section

3.1
and discusses the quest for the characterization of the polynomial ring in
n
-variables
k[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
or equivalently, the affine
n
-space
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}k[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
in
Section

3.2
. Of particular importance is the Linearization Problem (
Section

3.2.2
) and its connection with motivic homotopy theory, which is illustrated via an illuminative baby example (
Section

3.2.3
). Its grown-up version would be rigorously discussed in
Chapter

5
. We end the section with the famous "Zariski Cancellations" in
Section

3.2.4
in an algebro-geometric context. This chapter solely focuses on the background knowledge from the affine algebraic geometry, with intermediate guiding comments to the chapters further ahead. The reader is also advised to skim the chapter, if need be, and return when the relevant backward references arise.

Affine Fiber Spaces and Local Triviality

Flat morphisms of finite presentation with fibers isomorphic to affine spaces have been intensively studied in Affine Algebraic Geometry ("AAG", in short), in connection to the Zariski Cancellation Problem and and the Dolgačev-Veǐsfeǐler Problem (see e.g.
[
127
]
and the references therein for an overview). The main utility of the following definitions is that, from the motivic perspective, such morphisms provide an abundant source of relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes.

Definition 20
.

An
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space
over a scheme
S
is a smooth morphism of finite presentation
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
whose fibers over all points
s\in S

X_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\times_{S}\mathrm{Spec}(\kappa(s))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\kappa(s)}

are isomorphic to the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{n}
over the corresponding residue field
\kappa(s)
.

Definition 21
.

An
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
as above is called a
locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-bundle
in the Zariski (resp. Nisnevich, resp. étale) topology if every
s\in S
admits a Zariski (resp. Nisnevich, resp. étale) neighborhood
U
in
S
such that

U\times_{S}X\cong U\times\mathbb{A}^{n}
as schemes over
U
.

Remark 3.0.1
.

The above notion of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space corresponds to that of "
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fibration" sometimes called "affine fibration" classically used by some authors in AAG, (see e.g.
[
7
,
178
,
32
,
50
]
). Some other authors also define
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fibrations (
[
118
,
119
]
) in this context by requiring only that the general closed fibers are isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}
over the corresponding residue fields and other fibers are geometrically integral. In the language of Asanuma, morphisms with all closed fibers isomorphisms to
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{R}
are called pseudo-polynomial
R
-algebras over
n
-variables. Our proposed terminology is intended to clarify the notion and to make a clear distinction from the topological or model categorical notion of fibration.

3.1
A Quick Overview

1.

The Dolgačev-Veǐsfeǐler Conjecture:
A general form of the conjecture can be stated as follows:

Question
.

Let
S
be the spectrum of any regular local ring and let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be an
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space. Then does
f
necessarily admit a locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-bundle structure in the Zariski topology?

Anticipating the affirmation of this question, one can show that the following special case admits a positive solution:

Question
.

Is every
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fibre space
X\to\mathbb{A}^{m}
isomorphic to the trivial fibre space
\mathbb{A}^{m}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}\to\mathbb{A}^{m}
?

Indeed, due to the result of Bass-Connell-Wright (
[
28
]
), we have that every
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-bundle over an
affine
variety is isomorphic to a vector bundle. Since the base is an affine space, invoking the Quillen-Suslin theorem
[
171
]
, we get that any vector bundle over the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{m}
is trivial.

This conjecture was originally stated in
[
199
, Conjecture 1]
, where one can additionally find several other implications and connections with the theory of algebraic groups and
k
-forms of affine spaces. The case when
n=m=1
was proven to be true in the same paper (see [
ibid
, Proposition 3.7]). The case when
n=1,m\geq 0
was answered positively in
[
119
]
and the case when
n=2,m=1
was answered positively in
[
115
]
. The case when
n=m=2
was studied in
[
50
,
34
]
. For more background on this conjecture, we redirect the reader to
[
93
, §4]
. In the context of our script, we will formulate an answer to this question in relative settings in low dimensions in the language of motivic homotopy theory in
Section

4.2.2
and
Section

4.3.4
.

2.

The Automorphism Problem:
This problem asks for a complete description of the automorphism ring of the polynomial ring
k^{[n]}
or, equivalently, to determine the group,
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k})
, called the
affine Cremona group
. We have a complete understanding in dimension 1:
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k})\cong(k,+)\rtimes(k^{\times},\times)
and dimension 2: given by the amalgamated product, see
Theorem

3.2.6
. The problem seems to be notoriously difficult in full generality with only partial results known in dimensions
\geq 3
. A related question of interest is to study under what conditions for a variety
X
, does the
\operatorname{Aut}(X)\cong\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{n})
imply that
X\cong\mathbb{A}^{n}
. For example, under the condition that
X
is affine, the group
\operatorname{Aut}(X)
obtains the structure of an
ind-group
and this enables the characterization of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{n})
as follows
[
128
, Theorem 1.1]
:

Theorem
.

If
X
is a connected affine variety, then every isomorphism of ind-groups between
\operatorname{Aut}(X)
and
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{n})
is induced by an isomorphism
X\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{A}^{n}
of varieties.

For a generalization and more interesting characterizations,
[
126
]
and references therein.

3.

The Embedding Problem:
For a field
k
and integers
n\geq m\geq 0
, is every embedding of
\mathbb{A}^{m}_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
necessarily equivalent to the standard embedding of the coordinates? We present a comprehensive picture of this problem in
Section

3.2.1
.

4.

Linearization Conjecture:
This concerns studying if every
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathbb{A}^{n}
equivalent to the linear action. The precise statement and background are given in
Section

3.2.2
.

5.

Zariski Cancellation Problem:
ZCP asks if
\mathbb{A}^{n}
is "cancellative", which we shall discuss more in
Section

3.2.4
.

6.

Jacobian Conjecture:
For
n\geq 0
, if
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
be a polynomial map with
\det f\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, then is
f
invertible? Stated in colloquial terms,

If
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
is a polynomial map such that its derivative is non-singular at every point, then
f
is bijective.

This simple-looking statement has paramount consequences and relations with most problems in AAG. It is listed as one of the foundational problems of this century (
[
187
, Problem 16]
). The conjecture originates due to Keller in dimension 2
[
122
]
and has been widely open ever since. For more background and references, see
[
111
,
175
,
197
]
.

3.2
Characterization of the Affine Space

Fix a field
k
. Recall that by an
affine

n
-space over
k
, we mean the
n
-dimensional affine variety given by the Zariski spectrum of the
n
-dimensional polynomial ring over
k
, i.e.,

\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}k[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}].

As discussed above, several attempts were and are being made to characterize this object in an algebro-geometric way. This section exhibits some of the key directions and results obtained as a result of the tremendous amount of work from the past decades. This will also be an opportunity for us to provide a taste of where our structure results fit into the overall framework in cohesion with motivic homotopy theory.

3.2.1
The AMS Theorem and the Embedding Problem

The story in this direction begins with a purported proof of the following lemma due to Segre
[
181
]
in an attempt to prove the 2-dimensional Jacobian conjecture (See
[
196
, §1]
). The lemma was then independently proven by
[
2
]
and
[
193
]
, which is now famously called the
Abhyankar-Moh-Suzuki theorem
, or simply the AMS theorem:

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
f(t),g(t)\in\mathbb{C}[t]
be non-constant polynomials such that
\mathbb{C}[f,g]=\mathbb{C}[t]
, then either
\text{deg}\ f\mid\text{deg}\ g
(or)
\text{deg}\ g\mid\text{deg}\ f
.

This innocent statement has far-reaching geometric consequences in the world of AAG, including the embedding problem for surfaces
[
2
, §1.6]
. In view of the application to the latter problem, one unanimously refers to the following as the AMS theorem:

Theorem 3.2.2
.

For any algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic zero, every embedding
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
is equivalent
1
1

1

Two embeddings
f,g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
are equivalent if there exists a polynomial automorphism
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
such that
F\circ g=f
to the standard embedding. In other words,
f
is a coordinate in
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
.

Recall that by the standard embedding, we mean
t\mapsto(t,0)
. In the situations as above, we say that the embedding
f
is
rectifiable
and otherwise, call it
non-rectifiable
. A simple example of a rectifiable embedding is as follows:

Example 3.2.3
.

The map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
given by
t\mapsto(t,t^{2})
is rectifiable. It is rectified by the automorphism
F\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
given by
F(X,Y)\mapsto(X,Y-X^{2})
. Note that
F
is indeed an automorphism with the inverse given by
(X,Y)\mapsto(X,Y+X^{2})
.

A zoo of non-rectifiable embeddings into surfaces exists over a field of positive characteristics. In literature, these are also called the
non-trivial lines
and their existence was first demonstrated as early as the 1950s by
[
181
]
and later extensively by
[
162
]
. The one given below is a simple example of a non-rectifiable embedding:

Example 3.2.4
.

Let
k
be any field of characteristic
p>0
. Then consider two polynomials

\displaystyle f(t)=t^{p^{2}}\quad\quad g(t)=t^{p(p+1)}+t.

Observe that we have
k[f(t),g(t)]=k[t]
, but neither of the degrees of
f
or
g
divides the other. In the view of equivalence between
Lemma

3.2.1
and
Theorem

3.2.2
, it amounts to saying that the embedding

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k},\quad\text{defined as}\quad t\mapsto(t^{p^{2}},t^{p(p+1)}+t)

is non-rectifiable.

Remark 3.2.5
.

The embedding problem is also referred to as the
Epimorphism Problem
in the view of correspondence between affine varieties and their coordinate rings. In fact, the epimorphism problem asks the following: Fix a field
k
of characteristic zero, let
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}k[X,Y]\twoheadrightarrow k[X]
be an epimorphism of
k
-algebras with kernel
F=ker(\phi)
, then is
k[F]^{[1]}\cong k[X,Y]
?

Generalizations of the Embedding Problem

There are essentially two ways to generalize
Theorem

3.2.2
.

1.

For any field
k
of characteristic zero, is every embedding
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
rectifiable? When
k=\mathbb{C}
, due to
[
110
,
47
]
, we now have that it is indeed true in dimensions
n\geq 4
. However, over reals
k=\mathbb{R}
, Shastri
[
184
]
constructed an embedding, now called the
Shastri embedding
, of the trefoil knot (which is the simplest non-trivial knot) into
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{R}}
and proved that it is non-rectifiable (see
[
93
, P. 1582]
).

2.

Another type of generalization to this problem is now called the
Abhyankar-Sathaye Conjecture
, which states algebraically asks: for
f\in\mathbb{C}[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
such that
\mathbb{C}[X]/(f)\simeq\mathbb{C}^{[n-1]}
, is
f
a coordinate? i.e.,
\mathbb{C}[X]=\mathbb{C}[f,f_{2},\dots,f_{n}]
, for some
f_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[X]
. In geometric terms, this refers to the rectifiability of
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
. This conjecture is open for all
n\geq 3
. For a recent survey of known special cases and a general overview, we redirect the readers to
[
93
, §5]
and the references therein.

In a vast generality, one is tempted to ask the following question, which a priori seems heavily challenging:

Question
.

For
2\leq m\leq n-1
, is every embedding
\mathbb{A}^{m}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}
rectifiable?

This question, viewed in correspondence with the epimorphism statement, is solved in various cases. Interested readers can find relevant references in
[
74
, §2]
.

3.2.2
Linearization Problem

The Linearization Problem (LP) aims to understand
\mathbb{A}^{n}
via certain algebraic (reductive) group actions. From time immemorial, studying a space (algebraic variety) by means of (an algebraic) group action has proven to be a highly efficient tool. The main class of groups that one usually exploits is the unipotent groups (e.g., vector groups
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{n}
, for
n\geq 1
), the reductive groups (e.g., algebraic tori
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}
, for
n\geq 1
), and the subgroups of the automorphism group of affine spaces
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{n})
. The LP is of paramount interest in AAG as a part of the quest to characterize
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
among various topologically contractible varieties. It can be stated as follows:

Question
.

Fix a reductive (algebraic) group
G
. For every action
G\curvearrowright\mathbb{A}^{n}
, can we find a suitable polynomial change of coordinates such that it becomes equivalent to a linear action?

Recall that for any multiplicative algebraic group
G
with its group of units,
G^{\times}
and a
G
-variety
X
, a group action
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}G\times X\to X
is said to be
linear
if it is conjugate to an action of
GL_{n}
on
X
. Put alternatively, there exists an automorphism
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{n}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}
such that for any
\lambda\in G^{\times}
, we have that
\phi\circ h(\lambda)\circ\phi^{-1}
is linear.

In dimensions 1 and 2

The LP has been settled positively in dimensions 1 and 2, essentially due to the complete understanding we have of the automorphism group
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{n})
for
n\leq 2
. Recall that a polynomial map
\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{n}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}
is an isomorphism if and only if it is bijective. In other words, if
\psi=(\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n})
is given by a set of regular functions, then the above amounts to saying that the set of polynomials
\{\psi_{j}\}
generates the polynomial ring
k[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
. In particular, the LP takes its simplest form in dimension 1, since the group
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{1})
is completely determined by the affine group via
x\mapsto ax+b
, for
a\in k^{\times}
and consequently
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{1})\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}
, for the affine linear group
\mathbb{G}_{a}
. Thus, in particular, every
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
is linearizable. The situation has already become complicated for surfaces: it holds in dimension 2 essentially due to the elegant characterization of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{2})
due to the work of
[
198
,
84
]
that we describe below.

Theorem 3.2.6
.

For any base field
k
, the group
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{2})
is the amalgamated product
\mathcal{A}_{2}*_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}\mathcal{J}_{2}
. Here,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
are the subgroups of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{2})
representing affine transformations and triangular transformations and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
denotes their intersection.

In particular, this answers the LP positively in dimension 2.

Corollary
.

Every algebraic subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{2})
is either conjugate to a subgroup of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
or
\mathcal{J}_{2}
. In particular, every reductive subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{2})
is conjugate to a subgroup of
GL_{2}
.

Corollary
.

Every locally nilpotent derivation on
\mathbb{A}^{2}
is equivalent to the one given by
f(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
, for a polynomial
f(x)
. In other words, every
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action on
\mathbb{A}^{2}
is equivalent to one of the form
t\cdot(x,y)\mapsto(x,y+tf(x))
.

Remark 3.2.7
.

The subgroups of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{n})
that are exclusively generated by
\mathcal{A}_{n}
and
\mathcal{J}_{n}
are called
tame
automorphism. The difficulty in comprehending the
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{n})
is that we do not know if all of its subgroups are tame. Moreover, this characterization using the amalgamated product does not generalize to higher dimensions. For
n\geq 4
, there exists a non-linearizable
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathbb{A}^{n}
over fields of positive characteristics due to Asanuma (
[
8
]
).

Interestingly enough, the LP threw a curveball in dimension 3.

3.2.3
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-actions on the Koras-Russell Threefolds

In dimension
n=3
,
[
125
]
studied a certain smooth affine variety defined by the explicit polynomial equation

\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x^{2}z+x+y^{3}+t^{2}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathbb{C}}

then called the
Russell cubic
, eponymous to Russell who observed its similarities with
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. There is a non-linearizable
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
\mathcal{R}
given by

\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R},\quad(\lambda,(x,y,z,t))\mapsto(\lambda^{6}x,\lambda^{-6}y,\lambda^{3}z,\lambda^{2}t)

(3.1)

Why is this action non-linearizable?

It is a well-known fact that the existence of a fixed point set is a necessary condition for an action to be linearizable. This follows as a fact that the fixed point set is a subset of
\mathbb{A}^{m}
(for some
m
) and whose embedding into
\mathbb{A}^{n}
has to be equivalent to the linear one. Moreover, such a fixed point set also has to be connected. But if the action
\sigma
defined above is linearizable, then so is its restriction to the sixth roots of unity
\mu_{6}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. But we notice that the corresponding fixed point is given by
\{x(1+xz)=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}
, which is disconnected! It is important to note that
\mathcal{R}
is topologically contractible (cf.
[
215
]
) and hence topologically indistinguishable from
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}
. To make things spicier, it was also shown
[
117
, Lemma 5.1]
that
\mathcal{R}
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{6}
as a real manifold, whence
\mathcal{R}
has an incredible property of having the logarithmic Kodaira dimension equal to
\kappa(\mathcal{R})=-\infty
, which is that of
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
.

So, the curveball here are the following: a) if
\mathcal{R}\cong\mathbb{C}^{3}
, then this produces a counter-example to the LP in dimension 3 as
\sigma
gives rise to a non-linearizable
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, b) if
\mathcal{R}\ncong\mathbb{C}^{3}
, then this produces a counter-example to the characterization of
\mathbb{A}^{3}
. Hence, the study of these affine varieties played a pivotal role in AAG. This motivated the authors in
[
124
]
to completely classify all the prototypes similar to that of
\mathcal{R}
that admit hyperbolic
2
2

2

those that have a unique fixed point and the weights of the induced linear actions on the tangent space at this point are all non-zero, and their product is negative.

\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-actions and to construct a family of threefolds that allegedly would produce a counter-example to this conjecture. These are called the
Koras-Russell threefolds
and are broadly divided into three kinds depending on the number of
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-actions that these threefolds admit. The Koras-Russell threefolds of
first kind
admit more than one
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action and are given by the following equation:

\mathcal{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x^{m}z+x+y^{r}+t^{s}=0\}

(3.2)

where
m,r,s\geq 2
integers with gcd
(r,s)=1
. Note that Koras-Russell cubic threefolds
\mathcal{R}
are a particular example of
\mathcal{K}
with
r=3
and
s=2
. Those that are form the Koras-Russell threefolds of
second kind
admit essentially only one
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action (
[
168
, §2]
) and are given by the following equation:

\tilde{\mathcal{K}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x+(x^{n}+z^{k})^{l}y+t^{s}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{4}

(3.3)

where
n,k,s\geq 2,\ s>k,\ l\geq 1,\ \text{gcd}\ (k,s)=1=\text{gcd}\ (k,n)
. Finally, there are other kinds of Koras-Russell threefolds (sometimes called the
third kind
in the literature) that only admit trivial
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action (for more general types, we refer to
[
112
]
). It is important to note that all of these three kinds admit a hyperbolic
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action with a unique fixed point, by design! As far as this script is concerned, we shall only focus on the novel properties of the first kind
Eq.

3.2
. The LP stood open in dimension 3 for some time, as it was difficult to conclude if the Koras-Russell threefolds were actually isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{3}
in the interest of settling the LP. This was finally settled by the work of Makar-Limanov
[
143
]
by inventing a purely algebraic invariant (which he called
AK-invariant
but later became an eponym) that emerged solely for this purpose!

The Makar-Limanov and Derksen invariants

For any commutative ring
R
, the
Makar-Limanov invariant
is defined as follows:

\operatorname{ML}(R)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigg\{\bigcap\operatorname{Ker}(\partial)\mid\partial\in\operatorname{LND}(R)\bigg\}\subseteq R

(3.4)

Here,
\partial
is the locally nilpotent derivation which corresponds to free
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-actions. In particular, for an affine variety
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
, the
\operatorname{ML}(X)\subset\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
corresponds to all those regular functions on
X
that are
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-invariant. As a first instance, we have that
\operatorname{ML}(\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{C}})=\mathbb{C}
as there are no non-constant regular functions on affine spaces. On the other hand, he proved that
\operatorname{ML}(\mathcal{K})=\mathbb{C}[x]
which distinguished it from
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Thus, the LP was finally settled positively for threefold in 1997 (
[
112
]
). Following this, another similar invariant, called the
Derksen invariant
, denoted by
\mathcal{D}(R)
, emerged (
[
57
]
) which for a ring
R
. It is defined as the sub-algebra generated by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(R)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigg\{\bigcap\operatorname{Ker}\partial\mid\partial\in\operatorname{LND}(R)\backslash\{0\}\bigg\}\subseteq R

(3.5)

Alternatively,
\mathcal{D}(R)
is the sub-ring of
R
generated by the rings of invariants of all the non-trivial exponential maps of
R
. Though they seem closely related, neither of them depends on the other (cf.
[
48
]
). The following calculations shows that
\mathcal{K}
is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{k}
:

Example 3.2.8
.

Let
k
be a field and let
R=k[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
. Then
\operatorname{ML}(R)=k
for all
n
. When
n\geq 2
, we have
\mathcal{D}(R)=R
3
3

3

as an exception when
n=1
,
\mathcal{D}(R)=k
. For the Koras-Russell threefolds
\mathcal{K}
with its coordinate ring
\Gamma(\mathcal{K})
, we have that
\operatorname{ML}(\mathcal{K})=k^{[1]}\neq k
(
[
78
, Corollary 9.7]
) and
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{K})=k^{[3]}\neq\Gamma(\mathcal{K})
which distinguishes it from
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{k}
(
[
78
, Theorem 9.6]
).

Later, Kaliman
[
116
]
provided the following elegant argument to show that
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}
is non-isomorphic to
\mathcal{K}
:

Theorem
.

For any field
k
of characteristic zero, if the general fibers of a regular function
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{k}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
, then so is every other fiber.

This was later extended to all fields of characteristic zero by
[
51
, Theorem 4.2]
via Lefschetz principle arguments. For instance, note that the generic fiber
\mathrm{pr}_{x}^{-1}(0)
of
\mathcal{K}
with respect to the projection
\mathrm{pr}_{x}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
is the cylinder on a cuspidal curve
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\{y^{2}+t^{3}\}
and is singular. In particular, it cannot be isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}
. A similar characterization was given by Miyanishi, which was later shown to fail in higher dimensions (see
[
114
, Theorem 1]
and references therein).

Remark 3.2.9
.

In the context of the embedding problem, the question of whether threefolds like
\mathcal{K}
were isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{3}
was then considered a potential threat to Abhyankar-Sathaye conjecture (see
[
74
, §2.2]
).

Where does motivic homotopy theory fit in here?

From the motivic viewpoint, the affine
n
-space
\mathbb{A}^{n}
forms primordial examples of contractible schemes (cf.
Example

2.1.18
). Two varieties that are not isomorphic in
\mathcal{H}(k)
(or non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weakly equivalent in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
) as spaces cannot be isomorphic as
k
-varieties. In particular, if
\mathcal{K}
was not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, then it cannot be isomorphic to the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{3}
. Towards this pursuit, Asok
[
20
]
initiated a program to show that
\mathcal{K}
is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible and hence non-isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The idea was to produce possible obstructions in the
G
-equivariant homotopy theory. But as a key step in the overall program failed (cf.
[
102
, §1]
), it was sadly unable to show that
\mathcal{K}
is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible. In the same paper (
ibid
) Hoyois-Krishna-Østvær, in fact, proved the diametrically opposite result that
\mathcal{K}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in the stable homotopy category
\mathrm{SH}(\mathbb{C})
. Finally, Dubouloz-Fasel managed to prove the unstable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of
\mathcal{K}
over fields of characteristic zero
[
60
]
. We will give a comprehensive overview of these results in
Section

5.1
and prove that these results can further be generalized from fields to a general base scheme. In all, it is an astonishing fact that this family of threefolds is completely invisible to the lens of powerful algebraic tools such as motivic cohomology (or equivalently, higher Chow groups) and carries trivial vector bundles, just like
\mathbb{A}^{3}
(
[
102
, §3]
).

Remark 3.2.10
.

In relation to the Embedding Problem, it has been shown by
[
64
]
that there are inequivalent embeddings of Koras-Russell threefolds into
\mathbb{A}^{4}
.

3.2.4
Cancellations in Algebraic Geometry

The Cancellation Problem, in a broad sense, is a statement about a certain property of affine
k
-algebras in the following sense: Let
A
be any (say, finitely generated) affine algebra over a field
k
. Then for any
k
-algebra
B
,

\text{does}\quad A[x]\cong_{k}B[x]\quad\text{imply}\quad A\cong_{k}B\ ?

If this holds, we say that such a
k
-algebra
A
is
cancellative
. A particularly important version of this problem is when
A
is the
n
-dimensional polynomial ring
A=k[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
and its cancellation is of paramount importance in AAG. This is because, in the light of Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz, one can then transport this problem to the realm of algebraic geometry. In the ring theoretical context, it was posed by Zariski - hence, also referred to as the Zariski Cancellation Problem (ZCP) - at the Paris colloquium in 1949 (
[
161
]
, §5) stated as follows:

Question 3.2.11
.

Let
k
be a (resp. an algebraically closed) field. Then for an
k
-algebra
B
(resp. for an affine
k
-scheme
Y
), does
B\otimes k^{[1]}\cong k^{[n+1]}
(resp.
Y\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\cong\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{k}
) imply
B\cong k^{[n]}
(resp.
Y\cong\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
)?

Here, by
k^{[n]}
, we mean the polynomial ring in
n
-variables. An interesting counter-example when
k=\mathbb{R}
was constructed by
[
99
]
on the projective module defined by the tangent bundle over the real sphere is stably free but not free. The ZCP has been proven true over an arbitrary fields in dimension 1 and 2: For affine curves, it is due to
[
1
]
and for affine surfaces over a field of characteristic zero, it is due to
[
154
,
80
]
and over perfect fields due to
[
177
]
and more generally, due to
[
33
]
. An alternative simplified proof over an arbitrary field was given by
[
47
]
. The problem now remains widely open in dimensions
n\geq 3
over fields of characteristic zero. In dimension 3, the existence of the family of Koras-Russell threefolds of first kind
\mathcal{K}
as discussed above (
Eq.

3.2
) is seen as a potential counter-example to ZCP. Dubouloz
[
68
]
proved that all the cylinders over the Koras-Russell threefolds have trivial Makar-Limanov invariants,

\operatorname{ML}(\mathcal{K}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\cong k\cong\operatorname{ML}(\mathbb{A}^{3})\cong\operatorname{ML}(\mathbb{A}^{n})

If this were proven otherwise, it would have answered that
\mathcal{K}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
cannot be isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{4}
and thus, closing the conjecture; but from the current conclusion, it remains to prove or disprove the isomorphism
\mathcal{K}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\cong\mathbb{A}^{4}
.

ZCP in Positive Characteristics

Due to the works of Gupta
[
91
]
, ZCP admits a negative solution in every dimension
n\geq 3
over fields of positive characteristics. This counterexample essentially comes from another counterexample that was constructed in the context of attacking the Linearization Problem in positive characteristics. In particular,
[
8
, Theorem 2.2]
produced a
k
-algebra
A
that is stably isomorphic
\mathbb{A}^{n}
and carrying a
non-linearizable

\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
A
, for all
n\geq 4
over an infinite field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. The main ingredient towards the construction of such an
k
-algebra
A
emerges from his following theorem:

Theorem
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic
p>0
and

A=k[X,Y,Z,T]/\langle X^{m}Y+Z^{p^{e}}+T+T^{sp}\rangle

where
m,e,s
are positive integers such that
p^{e}\nmid sp
and
sp\nmid p^{e}
. Let
x
denote the image of
X
in
A
. Then
A
satisfies the following two properties:

1.

A^{[1]}\cong_{k[x]}k[x]^{[3]}\cong_{k}k^{[4]}

2.

A\ncong_{k[x]}k[x]^{[2]}
.

Using the techniques developed by Makar-Limanov and Crachiola,
[
91
]
proved that such an
k
-algebra
A
indeed produces a counter-example to ZCP in dimension 3, and soon after also extended this to all dimensions
n\geq 4

[
92
]
and thus completely settled the ZCP over fields of positive characteristics. In eponym, we call these affine varieties the
Asanuma-Gupta varieties
. It is crucial to note that the major tool that was exploited in all these instances is the
existence of the non-trivial line
and its generalization over fields of positive characteristics. Let us recall that a
non-trivial line
is polynomial
f\in k^{[2]}
such that

k^{[2]}/(f)\cong k^{[1]}\quad\text{but}\quad k[f]^{[1]}\ncong k^{[2]}

(that is,
f
cannot be made as a coordinate). For the Gupta’s counter-example, the polynomial equation

f(Z,T)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Z^{p^{e}}+T+T^{sp}

with
sp\nmid p^{e},\ p^{e}\nmid sp
served as a non-trivial line. Such a line is an example of an embedding of
\mathbb{A}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{2}
that is not the standard inclusion of coordinates.

Remark 3.2.12
.

As a result of the structure theorem obtained in
[
63
]
, we will see in
Section

4.2.2
and
Section

4.3.4
that the variant of the Zariski cancellation works over a more general base scheme in relative dimensions up to 2.

3.2.5
Asanuma’s Stable Structure Theorem

Recall that the
Dolgačev-Veǐsfeǐler Conjecture
asks if over a regular local ring
R
, an
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space can be equipped with a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-bundle structure. As noted previously, no complete cases have been known from dimensions
n\geq 3
. However, from dimensions
n\geq 4
, the conjecture fails over fields of positive characteristic, which can be seen as a consequence of either of the following facts:

•

presence of non-trivial lines (equivalently, non-rectifiable embeddings of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
),

•

presence of non-linearizable
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-actions on
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
,

We will state and review this aspect from the motivic viewpoint in
Section

4.3.4
. We shall conclude this chapter by mentioning a crucial output of Asanuma, called the
Stable Structure theorem
(
[
7
, Theorem 3.4]
) stated in the AAG language. For this, we shall quickly recall the following definition:

Definition 22
.

For smooth
k
-schemes
X
and
Y
, we say that
X
is
stably
k
-isomorphic
to
Y
, if there exists
i,j\geq 0
such that

X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{i}\cong Y\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{j}.

In ring-theoretic terms, we say that a
k
-algebra
A
is
stably polynomial
if for some
l,m\geq 0
,
A\otimes_{k}k^{[i]}\cong k^{[j]}
.

When translated to motivic homotopy theory, it provides us with an elegant technique to produce a plenitude of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes relative over a base scheme. The following simple-looking commutative algebra fact expresses a potential limitation of the motivic homotopical techniques towards the classification of problems such as Dolgačev-Veǐsfeǐler Conjecture or equivalently, the triviality of (Zariski) locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-bundles over affine spaces:

Theorem 3.2.13
.

Let
R
be a Noetherian ring and let
A
be a finitely generated flat
R
-algebra such that
A\otimes\kappa(p)\cong\kappa(p)^{[n]}
for any prime ideal
p
of
R
. Then
A
is an
R
-subalgebra (up to an isomorphism) of a polynomial ring
R^{[m]}
(for some
m
) such that

A^{[m]}\cong Sym_{R^{[m]}}(\mathfrak{P}\otimes R^{[m]}),

where
\mathfrak{P}
is a projective
A
-module.

Theorem

3.2.13
asserts that over a Noetherian ring
R
, any finitely generated flat
R
-algebra
A
with all fibers isomorphic to a polynomial ring in
m
-variables is a stably polynomial ring, that is,
A^{[k]}\cong R^{[m]}
, for some integers
k,m\geq 0
. Translated to algebro-geometric language, it asserts the following:

Theorem
.

Over a locally Noetherian scheme
S
, any
affine
morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
that is an
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space is Zariski locally
stably
isomorphic to a locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{m}
-bundle (for some positive integer
m\geq 0
).

This result is critical in the motivic homotopy theory illustrated by the following fact
[
19
, Lemma 3.3.3]
:

Lemma 3.2.14
.

Stably isomorphic smooth varieties are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weakly equivalent.

Corollary
.

If
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is any affine morphism that is also an
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space over a Noetherian scheme
S
, then
X
is stably isomorphic to a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{n+m}
-bundle (for some
m
). In particular, in the view of
Lemma

3.2.14

X
is a
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme over
S
.

Thus,
Theorem

3.2.13
provides us a fruitful way to harness several families of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes. Crucially, observe that from the motivic standpoint, this theorem precisely portrays that the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theoretical techniques alone cannot distinguish
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber spaces from the Zariski locally trivial ones.

Chapter 4
Relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility of Smooth Schemes

The results presented in this chapter are sourced from the article
[
63
]
.

Chapter Summary

Recall that a smooth
k
-scheme
X
is said to be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
if the structure map
X\to\operatorname{Spec}k
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
. It turns out that in relative dimensions
n<3
, the notion of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility is strong enough to characterize
\mathbb{A}^{n}
. In this chapter, we provide a comprehensive explanation of this statement and demonstrate that the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory detects Zariski-local trivial
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-bundles in relative dimensions
n<3
. We begin with the case of étale schemes over an arbitrary base in
Section

4.1
. In
Section

4.2
, we study the relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of smooth curves over a normal scheme and provide a characterization of the affine 1-space relative to this setting. We systematically study the relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of smooth surfaces in
Section

4.3.4
and provide a characterization of the affine 2-space over certain Dedekind schemes. In both these settings, we obtain a positive solution to the relative variant of the Zariski Cancellation Problem as a consequence. Following this, we analyze and review the common knowledge for higher relative dimensions
n\geq 4
in
Section

4.4
and prove that
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber spaces provide abundant examples of relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible spaces.

4.1
Relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility of Étale Schemes

We begin by establishing the structure theorem in relative dimension 0, i.e., for étale schemes, in terms of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility and enhance this to arbitrary base schemes. An important consequence here is the identification of étale schemes with that of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid schemes. The case of zero-dimensional smooth
k
-schemes is equivalent to that of the characterization of étale
k
-algebras. This gives us the following straightforward statement.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

[
63
]

Let
X
be an étale scheme over an arbitrary field
k
. Then the canonical map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\operatorname{Spec}k
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
if and only if
f
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
.

Proof.

One implication is obvious. Assume that
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible. Since
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\operatorname{Spec}k
is étale,
X
is a disjoint union of spectra of finite separable field extensions of
k
. As every
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible space is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected (in particular, connected as a topological space), we have that
X\cong\operatorname{Spec}k^{\prime}
, for some finite separable extension
k^{\prime}
of
k
. But by
Example

2.1.20
, any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme over
k
admits a section, and so
k^{\prime}\cong k
.
∎

Theorem 4.1.2
.

[
63
]

Let
X
be an étale scheme of finite presentation over an arbitrary base scheme
S
. Then the canonical morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
if and only if
f
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
.

Proof.

One direction is obvious. Assume that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. For every point
s\in S
with residue field
\kappa(s)
, it follows from
Corollary

2.1.41
that
f_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X_{s}=X\times_{S}\operatorname{Spec}\kappa(s)\to\operatorname{Spec}\kappa(s)
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\kappa(s)}
, whence, by
Lemma

4.1.1
, that the morphism
f_{s}
is an isomorphism of schemes. Thus,
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a bijective morphism with trivial, hence radicial, residual extensions from which it follows that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is universally injective. Since
f
is on the other hand étale, it is an open immersion (see
[
190
,
Tag 02LC
]
). But as
f
is also surjective
1
1

1

the surjectivity follows from the fact that the fibers cannot be empty for
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Recall from
13
that
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid schemes are the analogs of discrete objects in the motivic homotopy theory. The authors in
[
18
, Example 2.1.10]
observe that any 0-dimensional scheme over a field is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid. This observation admits a generalization over a base scheme as follows:

Proposition 4.1.3
.

[
63
]

An étale scheme of finite presentation
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
over an integral scheme
S
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid over
S
.

Proof.

Let
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\to S
be any smooth
S
-scheme and let
\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\to X
be an
S
-morphism. We can assume without loss of generality that
U
is connected. Let
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\times_{U}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\to U
be the flat morphism defined as the composition of the projections
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\times_{U}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}
and
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\to U
, and consider the following cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z=X\times_{X\times_{S}X}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\times_{U}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\times_{U}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{S}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\times\psi}

where
\Delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to X\times_{S}X
is the diagonal morphism. Since
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is separated,
\Delta
is a closed subscheme of
X\times_{S}X
and hence
j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Z\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\times_{U}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}
is a closed immersion as well. Since
\pi
is flat, whence an open morphism, the image by
\pi
of the Zariski open subset
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\times_{U}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\setminus j(Z)
is a Zariski open subset
U^{\prime}
of
U
consisting by construction of all points
u
for which the morphism
\psi|_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{u}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{u}\to X
is not constant. The fiber
X_{\eta}
of
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
over the generic point
\eta
of
S
being an étale scheme over the field of functions
K
of
S
, it is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid by the observation made above. It follows that there exists a non-empty Zariski open subset
S_{0}\subset S
such that letting
U_{0}=h^{-1}(S_{0})
, the restriction
\psi|_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U_{0}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U_{0}}\to f^{-1}(S_{0})
of
\psi
factors through
U_{0}
. This implies that
U^{\prime}\subset U\setminus U_{0}
, whence that
U^{\prime}=\emptyset
(because
U^{\prime}
is open in
U
,
U\setminus U_{0}
is closed in
U
and
U
is connected). Thus,
\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\to X
factors through the projection
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\to U
, which shows that
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid.
∎

Question 4.1.4
.

Does the
4.1.3
still hold if
X
is not separated?

4.2
Relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility of Smooth Curves

In this section, we show that being affine is a consequence of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility for smooth curves (
Lemma

4.2.1
). We then present an extensive proof of the fact that
\mathbb{A}^{1}
is the only smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible curve over any field (
Theorem

4.2.2
). Finally, we extend this result over a reasonably arbitrary base scheme in relative dimension 1 (
Theorem

4.2.5
). Let us begin by recalling the following definition:

Definition 23
.

Let
Y
be a
k
-variety. We say that
Y
is a
k
-form
of
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
if for some (say, finitely generated) field extension
L/k
the scheme-theoretic base change

Y_{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Y\times_{k}\operatorname{Spec}L\cong\mathbb{A}^{n}_{L}\cong\operatorname{Spec}L[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]

We then say that this
k
-form
Y
is trivial if
L=\operatorname{Spec}k
and non-trivial otherwise.

4.2.1
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility over fields

Let us fix a field
k
. We begin with the following lemma that says that the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of a curve
C
necessarily implies that
C
is affine.

Lemma 4.2.1
.

[
63
]

Any smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible curve
C
over a field is affine.

Proof.

Since
C
is a smooth curve, it is either affine or projective. On the contrary, suppose that
C
is projective. By the classification result of smooth proper (equivalently, projective in dimension 1) curves over a field
k
up to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence
[
18
, Proposition 2.1.12]
, we have that the only
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected curve is the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
. But clearly
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
cannot be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible (for e.g.,
\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{P}^{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}\ncong 0
; cf.
Example

2.1.52
). This uniqueness of
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
among
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected proper curves follows from the observation that other candidates, such as proper curves of positive genus or non-trivial
k
-forms of
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
or
\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
, are all
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid varieties, hence cannot be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible. Thus,
C
has to be affine.
∎

As a consequence of this, one can uniquely characterize the affine line among
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible curves over arbitrary fields (see also
[
11
, Claim 5.7]
).

Theorem 4.2.2
.

A smooth curve
C
over an arbitrary field
k
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in
\mathcal{H}(k)
if and only if
C
is isomorphic to the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
.

Proof.

One implication is obvious: the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over any field. Suppose that
C
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible curve. To begin with, let us assume
k
is algebraically closed. Then one knows that the (regular) compactification
P
of
C\hookrightarrow P
over perfect fields is, in fact, a smooth curve. Moreover, by the classification result and the genus argument mentioned in
Lemma

4.2.1
, one again concludes that
P\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
. By the definition of the compactification, we have that
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash Z
, where
Z\hookrightarrow P
is the closed complement
Z\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash C
. As
C
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible and in particular,
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected (
Example

2.1.54
),
Z
cannot have more than one closed point. On contrary, suppose that
Z=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
, then we see that

C\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash Z\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{p_{1},p_{2}\}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m,k}.

But from the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigidity of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
(
Example

2.1.26
), we have that
\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
cannot be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected; let alone
\mathbb{G}_{m,k}\backslash\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}
. Hence,
Z=\{p_{1}\}
. Thus, one concludes that
C\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}\backslash\{pt\}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
. Now, let
k
be arbitrary. Let
\widetilde{k}
be an algebraic closure of
k
. Then, by
Corollary

2.1.40
,

C_{\widetilde{k}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C\times_{\operatorname{Spec}k}\operatorname{Spec}\widetilde{k}\to\operatorname{Spec}\widetilde{k}

is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible curve over
\widetilde{k}
, which, by the previous case, is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\widetilde{k}}
. Thus,
C
is a
k
-form of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
, which automatically, due to
Example

2.1.43
, implies that it has to be the trivial
k
-form of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2.3
.

For an alternate proof over algebraic closed fields of characteristic zero using
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-actions, see
[
78
, Corollary 1.29]
.

Corollary 4.2.4
.

[
63
]

For any base scheme
S
, if
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
of relative dimension 1, then
X
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber space.

Proof.

For any point
s\in S
, consider the base change of
f
along
\operatorname{Spec}\ \kappa(s)\to S
. By
2.1.39
, each fiber
X_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\times_{S}\operatorname{Spec}\kappa(s)
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible curve in
\mathcal{H}(\kappa(s))
. By
Theorem

4.2.2
, we have that
X_{s}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\kappa(s)}
.
∎

4.2.2
Relative dimension 1

By exploiting the
20
and
Theorem

4.2.2
, we furthermore extend this characterization over a base scheme via the following structure result.

Theorem 4.2.5
.

[
63
]

For a smooth separated morphism of finite presentation
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
and relative dimension
1
over a Noetherian normal scheme
S
, the following are equivalent:

1.

f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\mathrm{Spc}_{S}
,

2.

f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber space,

3.

f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle.

Proof.

The implication
(1)\iff(2)
is a consequence of
2.1.48
and
Theorem

4.2.2
. Indeed assuming (1), we have that for any
s\in S
, the base change morphism
f_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X_{s}\to\operatorname{Spec}\kappa(s)
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\kappa(s)}
, equivalently,
X_{s}
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible curve in
\mathcal{H}(\kappa(s))
, whence by
Theorem

4.2.2
imply that
X_{s}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\kappa(s)}
. The
2.1.48
now glues this fibrewise data to produce that
X
is an
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. The other implication follows directly from
2.1.48
. The implication
(3)\implies(1)
follows from
Example

2.1.18
. The implication
(2)\Rightarrow(3)
is due to the characterization of Zariski-locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundles by Kambayashi-Wright
[
119
]
. For the convenience of the reader, we now briefly recall the scheme of the proof given in
[
119
]
.

We begin with the case where
S
is the spectrum of a discrete valuation ring
R
, with uniformizing parameter
\pi
, we denote by
\eta
the generic point of
S
with corresponding residue
K=\mathrm{Frac}(R)
and by
s
the closed point of
S
, with residue field
\kappa
. Since
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber space, the fiber
X_{\eta}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K}=\mathrm{Spec}(K[x_{0}])
. Viewing
x_{0}
as a rational function
X
, we can assume up to changing
x_{0}
by
\pi^{n}x_{0}
for suitable
n\in\mathbb{Z}
if necessary, that
x_{0}
is actually a regular function on
X
and that its restriction to the closed fiber
X_{s}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\kappa}
of
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is non-zero. The inclusion
R[x_{0}]\hookrightarrow\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
then corresponds to an
S
-morphism
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}=\mathrm{Spec}(R[x_{0}])
which restricts to an isomorphism over the generic point of
S
. If the restriction
h_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{s}
of
h_{0}
over the closed point
s
of
S
is constant, then arguing as in the proof of
[
119
, Proposition 1.4]
, there exists an
S
-morphism
\tau\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
and a quasi-finite
S
-morphism
h^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
both restricting to isomorphisms over the generic point of
S
such that
h=h^{\prime}\circ\tau
, whence an open immersion by Zariski Main Theorem
[
88
, Corollaire 8.12.10]
, from which it follows that
h^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is an étale morphism restricting to an isomorphism over the generic point of
S
. On the other hand, since the restriction
h^{\prime}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X_{s}=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\kappa}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\kappa}=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{s}
is étale, it is an isomorphism. This implies that
h^{\prime}
is an étale bijective morphism with trivial residual extensions, whence an isomorphism.

The case where
\dim S=1
being settled, the proof then proceeds by an induction on the dimension of the base scheme
S
: letting
S
be the spectrum of Noetherian normal local ring of dimension
n\geq 2
and letting
s
be its closed point, we can assume by induction that the restriction
f^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
of
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
over
S^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=S\setminus\{s\}
is a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle. The argument in
[
119
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.2-2.3]
applies verbatim to give the conclusion that
f^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S^{\prime}}\to S^{\prime}
. The diagram in which all the horizontal maps are open immersions determines a rational map of
S
-schemes
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\dashrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

which restricts to an isomorphism over
S^{\prime}
. Since
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
is affine,
h
is uniquely determined by rational function on
X
, which is regular outside
X_{s}
, and since
X
is normal because
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is flat and
S
is normal and
X_{s}
has codimension
\geq 2
in
X
, it follows that the rational map
h
is in fact an everywhere defined
S
- morphism
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
. Moreover, since
h
restricts to an isomorphism over
S^{\prime}
, the canonical homomorphism
h^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}/S}\to\Omega_{X/S}
is an isomorphism outside
X_{s}
, whence is an isomorphism since
h^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}/S}
and
\Omega_{X/S}
are invertible sheaves and
X_{s}
has codimension
\geq 2
in
X
(cf.
[
87
, Theorem 5.10.5]
). In sum,
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
is an étale morphism of
S
-schemes restricting to an isomorphism over
S^{\prime}
. Since
X_{s}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\kappa(s)}
, we conclude as in the previous case that the restriction
h_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X_{s}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\kappa(s)}
is an isomorphism as well and finally that
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
is an isomorphism of
S
-schemes.
∎

Theorem

4.2.5
provides us with an elegant characterization to identify the affine line relative to a base scheme.

Corollary 4.2.6
.

[
63
]

A smooth separated scheme
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
of finite presentation and relative dimension
1
over a Noetherian normal scheme
S
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
if and only if all of the following holds:

1.

X
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible curve in
\mathcal{H}(S)
,

2.

the relative canonical sheaf of differentials
\Omega_{f}
of
f
is trivial, and

3.

f
has a section.

Proof.

One direction is clear: the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over
S
, has trivial sheaf of relative differential
\Omega_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}/S}
and admits a section given by
0_{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
.

Now suppose the converse: since
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, it is a Zariski locally trivial bundle by
Theorem

4.2.5
. Since the automorphism group functor
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{1})
of the affine line is the affine group
\mathbb{G}_{a}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}
, every Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle over
S
is an affine-linear bundle, that is, is isomorphic over
S
to the total space of a torsor
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}P\to S
under a certain line bundle
p\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}L=\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{L})\to S
on
S
for some invertible sheaf
\mathcal{L}
on
S
. The latter invertible sheaf
\mathcal{L}
is uniquely determined up to isomorphism by the property that under the isomorphism
f^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Pic}(S)\to\operatorname{Pic}(X)
induced by
f
(see e.g.
[
142
, Theorem 5]
) the class of
f^{*}\mathcal{L}
equals that of
\omega_{f}
. The assumption that
\omega_{f}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{X}
implies that
\mathcal{L}\cong\mathcal{O}_{S}
, whence that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is isomorphic over
S
to the total space of a
\mathbb{G}_{a,S}
-torsor
P\to S
, and the assumption that
f
has a section implies in turn that
P\to S
is the trivial
\mathbb{G}_{a,S}
-torsor
\mathbb{G}_{a,S}\to S
, whence that
X
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
.
∎

Remark 4.2.7
.

As a consequence of the fact that every vector bundle torsor over an affine scheme is trivial, it follows from the proof of
Corollary

4.2.6
that a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
of finite presentation and relative dimension
1
over an affine Noetherian normal scheme
S
always has a section. So, for a Noetherian normal
affine
scheme
S
, relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility and triviality of the relative canonical sheaf are sufficient to characterize the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
among smooth
S
-schemes of finite presentation and relative dimension
1
over
S
.

On the other hand, if
S
is not affine, the existence of a section in
Corollary

4.2.6
is a necessary condition, as illustrated by the following example.

Example 4.2.8
.

Consider the quasi-projective scheme
Y\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\backslash\Delta(\mathbb{P}^{1})
obtained by deleting the diagonal
\Delta(\mathbb{P}^{1})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\text{Im}(\Delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{P}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}k}\mathbb{P}^{1})
. Let
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a morphism defined by the projection onto the first coordinate. Observe that
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
is a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle and it is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
. Moreover, the scheme
Y
is affine as it is given by the complement of an ample divisor in
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. However, the morphism
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
cannot be a trivial torsor over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
as
Y
does not admit any section, since any section necessarily intersects the diagonal
\Delta(\mathbb{P}^{1})
.

Zariski Cancellation for Relative Curves

As a consequence of
Corollary

4.2.6
, we obtain the affirmation of the following relative variant of the Zariski Cancellation Problem for curves.

Corollary 4.2.9
.

[
63
]

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be a smooth separated scheme of finite presentation and relative dimension
1
over a normal scheme
S
such that
X\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\cong\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
for some
n\geq 1
. Then
X
is
S
-isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
.

Proof.

Since the relative canonical sheaf
\omega_{X\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}/S}
is isomorphic to

\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\omega_{f}\otimes\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\omega_{\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}/S}\cong\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\omega_{f}

we infer from the isomorphism
X\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\cong\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
that
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\omega_{f}
is trivial. However, since
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathrm{Pic}(X)\to\mathrm{Pic}(X\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S})
is an isomorphism, we have that
\omega_{f}
is the trivial sheaf. On the other hand,
f
has a section given by the composition of the zero section
s_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\to\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
with the projection
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}\cong X\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\to X
. Finally, since
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\to X
and
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}\to S
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences in
\mathrm{Spc}_{S}
so is
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
and the conclusion then follows from
Corollary

4.2.6
.
∎

4.2.3
Some Counterexamples over Non-normal Base

We will now present some counterexamples to the
Theorem

4.2.5
, especially when the base scheme is not normal. In particular, these provide examples of relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes which are not Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundles. The first example is due to Madhav Nori (
[
119
, §3]
) that gives us a geometric instance of an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber space that cannot be a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle over a singular (even
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible!) scheme.

Example 4.2.10
.

Let
k
be algebraically closed field and let
C\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{V^{2}-W^{3}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
be the cuspidal curve which is singular at the point
p=(0,0)
. Consider the map

\displaystyle F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\to C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptt\mapsto\bigg((t^{2},t^{3}),[t\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}1]\bigg)

The map
F
is a closed immersion. Let
X=C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}\backslash Z
be the open subscheme of
C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
, where
Z\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\textrm{Im}(F)
. Now the projection
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}\to C
is a (trivial)
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
-fibre space which restricts to the map
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to C
. The map
\phi
is clearly an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber space since for all points
s\in X
, the fiber
\phi^{-1}(s)\cong(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}\backslash[t\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}1])\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
.

We now claim that
\phi
is
not
a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle in any open set around the point
p
. Suppose by contrary,
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to C
has a locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-structure around
p
, then by shrinking
C
about the point
p
, we have an isomorphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\to X
over
C
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Now, considering the composite
C\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\to X\hookrightarrow C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
restricted over the fiber over any point
x\in C
gives us an open immersion
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
. This extends uniquely to an isomorphism (say)
\sigma_{x}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
. To conclude, we observe that the map

\displaystyle C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}\to C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt(x,y)\mapsto\bigg(x,\sigma_{x}(y)\bigg)

carries the singular curve
C\times\{\infty\}
birationally onto the smooth curve
Z
, which is not possible.

The second example is derived from
[
95
]
. The base scheme
S
is not regular, and the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber space over it we consider cannot be stably Zariski locally trivial (for the same reason it is not Zariski locally trivial). Nevertheless, due to
Theorem

4.2.5
, we now know that the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber space considered is indeed relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible!

Example 4.2.11
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic
p>0
, let
S\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}=\operatorname{Spec}k[x,y]
be singular, non semi-normal, curve with equation

x^{p+1}-y^{p}=0

and let
X\subset S\times_{k}\mathrm{Spec}(k[u,v])
be the affine surface with equation

u^{p}+xv^{p}-v=0.

Denote by
A
and
B
the coordinate rings of
S
and
X
respectively. The restriction to
X
of the projection
\mathrm{pr}_{S}
is an affine
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber space
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
whose restriction over the complement
S^{*}\cong\operatorname{Spec}k[t^{\pm 1}]
, where
t=x^{-1}y
, of the unique singular point
o=(0,0)
of
S
is isomorphic to trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle

S^{*}\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}=\operatorname{Spec}k[t^{\pm 1}][\tilde{u}]

over
S^{*}
, where
\tilde{u}=u+tv
. It is known in contrast that there is no Zariski open neighborhood of
o
in
S
over which
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
restricts to a trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle
[
95
, Lemma 1.6]
, in particular,
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is not a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle. Nevertheless, it can be verified by direct computation that the morphism of
S^{*}
-schemes

\displaystyle X\times_{S^{*}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S^{*}}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S^{*}}

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76219pt(\tilde{u},w)\mapsto(z_{1},z_{2})=\bigg(\tilde{u}-t(\tilde{u}+tw)^{p},w+(\tilde{u}+tw)^{p}\bigg)

is an isomorphism (with inverse given by

(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\bigg(z_{1}+t(z_{1}+tz_{2})^{p},z_{2}-(z_{1}+tz_{2})^{p})\bigg)

which further extends over
o
to an isomorphism of
S
-schemes
X\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}
. It then follows due to
Theorem

4.2.5
that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
.

Some other examples of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber spaces
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
over non-normal curves
S
which are not a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle are given
[
119
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.4]
.

Example 4.2.12
.

As a third example, consider
S
to be any rational affine plane cuspidal curve over a field
k
of characteristic zero with equation

\{x^{p}-y^{q}=0\},

where
p,q\geq 2
are relatively prime integers. Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be the restriction of the projection
\mathrm{p}_{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}\to S
to the complement
X
of the Zariski closure
Z\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}
of the graph
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
of the normalization morphism
\nu\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\to S
of
S
. By construction, every such
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber space restricting to a trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle over the complement of the unique singular point
c=(0,0)
of
C
, but whose restriction over any Zariski open neighborhood
U
of
c
in
C
cannot be trivial. Indeed, otherwise if such a trivialization
X|_{U}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}
existed then the inclusion of
U
-schemes

\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}\cong X|_{U}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}_{U}

would extend to a morphism
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{U}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}_{U}
mapping the singular curve
U=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{U}\backslash\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}
birationally onto the smooth curve
Z|_{U}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{U}
, which is not possible. Nevertheless, we see that all of these
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber spaces
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
.

The final example is of a different flavor: the base is a normal scheme, but this example illustrates the importance in
Theorem

4.2.5
of considering
all
fibers of
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
and not only those over closed points of
S
, even when
S
is defined over an algebraically closed field.

Example 4.2.13
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
p>0
. Consider the morphism

\displaystyle f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Spec}k[x,y]\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\longrightarrow S\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}k[t]\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 96.73936pt(x,y)\longmapsto x^{p^{2}}-y-y^{p^{2}+p}.

Then
f
is a smooth morphism between
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
k
-schemes, which has all its closed fibers isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
. Indeed, given
\lambda\in k
, as
k
is algebraically closed we can write
\lambda=-\mu^{2p}
for some
\mu\in k
so that
f^{-1}(\lambda)
equals the smooth curve in
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
with equation

(x+\mu)^{p^{2}}-y-y^{p^{2}+p}=0,

which is the image of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
by the closed immersion
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
defined by

s\mapsto(s+s^{p^{2}+p}-\mu,s^{p^{2}}).

On the other hand, the fiber of
f
over the generic point of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
is the purely inseparable
k(t)
-form
Y
of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k(t)}
with equation

x^{p^{2}}-y-y^{p^{2}+p}-t=0

in
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k(t)}
. So,
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is not an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fiber space. However, on the other hand, since
Y
is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible by
Theorem

4.2.2
, it follows from
2.1.39
that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
is not a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
.

4.3
Relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility of Smooth Surfaces

In this section, we continue to investigate the characterization in dimension 2. We begin with a recent result of Choudhury-Roy on the uniqueness of the affine plane (
Theorem

4.3.1
). As in the case for curves, we systematically establish that being affine is a consequence of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility for smooth surfaces (
Lemma

4.3.3
). This seemingly simple fact allows us to, in fact, extend the aforementioned uniqueness of the affine plane over perfect fields (
Theorem

4.3.4
), leaving the story open for imperfect fields. We will digress a bit over imperfect fields using the theory of non-trivial
k
-forms of
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
. Finally, all these will allow us to state an elegant characterization of Dedekind schemes
D
in relative dimension 2 (
Theorem

4.3.11
). As a consequence, we all characterize the affine plane over
D
(
Corollary

4.3.13
) and present the Zariski Cancellation in this context (
Corollary

4.3.14
).

4.3.1
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility over Fields

Recall from
Section

1.2.1
that the topological contractibility alone is not sufficient to characterize the affine 2-space. On the other hand, tools coming from birational geometry, such as the logarithmic Kodaira dimension (affine version of the usual Kodaira dimension), proved to be very useful for studying the exotic complex structures arising from surfaces such as
Example

1.2.2
,
Example

1.2.3
, and their generalized products (see
[
215
, §1.5]
). The seed for characterizing the affine plane primarily stems from the work of Miyanishi via
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-actions
[
153
]
. However, it was recently proved by Choudhury-Roy (
[
43
, Theorem 1.1]
) that the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility is a much stronger invariant compared to topological contractibility and the log Kodaira invariant when it comes to the characterization of
\mathbb{A}^{2}
. We will now state their theorem and give an overview of the machinery involved in their proof.

Theorem 4.3.1
.

A smooth affine surface
X
over a field
k
of characteristic zero is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in
\mathcal{H}(k)
if and only if it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
.

Strategy of the proof

The work of Miyanshi-Sugie (
[
156
, §4.1]
,
[
154
]
) provides an algebraic characterization for a non-singular affine surface
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
over an algebraically closed field
k
