of characteristic zero. They proved that
X\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}
if and only if

•

A
is a unique factorization domain with
A^{\times}=k^{\times}
,

•

X
has the logarithmic Kodaira dimension
\bar{\kappa}(X)=-\infty
.

Assuming the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of
X
, we get that the Picard group is trivial and the group of units
\mathbb{G}_{m,k}(X)\cong k^{\times}
. It remains to show that
\bar{\kappa}(X)=-\infty
. To achieve this, the authors in
[
43
, §4]
study the notion of a variety being
dominated by the images of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, which in simple terms translates to "being able to cover up the given variety by affine lines".

Definition 24
.

A
k
-variety
X
over a field of characteristic zero is said to be

(a)

dominated by images of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
if there is an open dense subset
U\subset X
such that, for every
p\in U(k)
, there is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
in
X
through
p
, that is, there exists a non-constant morphism from
\mathbb{A}^{1}\to X
whose image contains
p
)

(b)

\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruled
if there is a dominant generically finite morphism
H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\times_{k}Y\to X
for some
k
-variety
Y

In general, we have
(b)\implies(a)
; but if the base field
k
is uncountable, then we have
(a)\iff(b)
. Exploiting such
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connectedness-type results, Choudhury-Roy showed a crucial fact that for any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected surface over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic zero, one of the following occurs (
[
43
, Theorem 4.9]
):

•

there is a non-constant
\mathbb{A}^{1}
through all
x\in X(k)
,

•

there is a non-constant morphism
H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{Y}\to X
for some irreducible
Y\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
such that the dimension of the closure of the image of
H
is at least 2.

Thus, when
k
is uncountable, the above says that
X
has to be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruled, whence by
[
106
]
has negative log Kodaira dimension. Now, given any field
k
of characteristic zero, the idea is to embed
k
as a subfield of an uncountable algebraically closed field
L
and realize the extension
L/k
as the filtered colimit of finitely generated sub-extensions of
k
. Translated to algebro-geometric terms, these extensions correspond to morphisms of (affine) smooth schemes with affine transition maps, whence by
[
158
, Corollary 1.24]
preserves
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility under pullbacks, i.e.,
X_{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\times_{\operatorname{Spec}k}\operatorname{Spec}L\to\operatorname{Spec}L
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible variety. Supported by the previous justifications, we have that
X_{L}\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}_{L}
. In other words,
X
is a
k
-form of
\mathbb{A}^{2}
. To conclude, observe that due to
[
120
, Theorem 3]
this has to be a trivial
k
-form and so
X\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
.

As a result, we have that a complex affine surface
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}
if and only if it is topologically contractible and
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected. Hence, connecting back to our story from
Section

1.2.1
, this result provides us with a proof that the Ramanujam surface and the tom Dieck-Petrie surfaces cannot be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected, which independently implies that they can neither be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected nor be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible. Moreover,
Theorem

4.3.1
also answered the conjecture of
[
66
, Question 2.34]
that any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible surface indeed has negative log Kodaira dimension (as they are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected!). To this end, for a smooth affine surface
X
, we have the following implications:

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}^{1}\text{-contractible}\implies\mathbb{A}^{1}\text{-connected}\implies\mathbb{A}^{1}\text{-uniruled}\implies\bar{\kappa}(X)=-\infty

Having said this, it is natural to ask the following extension.

Question 4.3.2
.

Can we extend
Theorem

4.3.1
over arbitrary fields?

4.3.2
Extension to Perfect Fields

We will first show that the affineness is a consequence of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility for smooth surfaces (
Lemma

4.3.3
) and extend the
Theorem

4.3.1
over perfect fields (
Theorem

4.3.4
).

Lemma 4.3.3
.

(
[
63
]
)
Any smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible surface is necessarily affine.

Proof.

For this proof, we will use arguments from the stable homotopy type of punctured tubular neighborhood (cf.
Section

C.2
). To show that a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible surface
X
is affine, it suffices to show that
X
is
connected at infinity
, in the sense that for every smooth proper completion
X\hookrightarrow\bar{X}
of
X
, the boundary
\partial{X}=\bar{X}\setminus X
is a connected scheme. For now, let us assume that
X
is indeed connected at infinity. By the existence of resolution of singularities for surfaces, we can assume that
\partial{X}
is a simple normal crossing (SNC) divisor on
\bar{X}
. Since
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible,
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{*}=k^{*}
and
\mathrm{Pic}(X)
is trivial, from which it follows that
\mathrm{Pic}(\bar{X})\cong\mathrm{Cl}(\bar{X})
equals the free abelian group generated by the divisor classes in
\mathrm{Cl}(\bar{X})
of the irreducible components of
\partial{X}
. If
\partial{X}
is in addition connected, then by applying the proof due to
[
81
, P. 512, Theorem, item 3]
, one concludes that
\partial{X}
is the support of an effective ample divisor on
\bar{X}
, which implies that
X
is affine.

Now it remains to argue that a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible surface is connected at infinity. For this we use a key property of the stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy type at infinity: the stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy type of
X
at infinity is isomorphic (in the stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
\mathrm{SH}(k)
) to that of
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
(cf.
Example

C.2.4
), in particular, to the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-suspension spectrum
\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\backslash\{0\})_{+}
of the smooth connected scheme
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\setminus\{0\}
. This identification combined with the description in
[
53
]
of the the stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy type of
X
at infinity in terms of the boundary divisor
\partial{X}=\bar{X}\setminus X
in a smooth SNC completion
\bar{X}
of
X
implies that
\partial{X}
must be connected.
∎

Essentially, using this powerful fact that any smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible surface is automatically affine, we can promote
Theorem

4.3.1
to perfect fields.

Theorem 4.3.4
.

[
63
]

A smooth surface
X
over a perfect field
k
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in
\mathcal{H}(k)
if and only if it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
.

Proof.

One direction is obvious:
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible surface over any field
k
. Suppose that
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, then we have that
X
is affine due to
Lemma

4.3.3
. In essence, once we have that
X
is an affine
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible smooth surface, a proof can be obtained by manipulating the arguments from
[
177
,
44
]
. To begin with, recall that due to
[
120
]
,
\mathbb{A}^{2}
does not admit non-trivial forms over a perfect field. Hence, it suffices to show that the base change of
X
to an algebraic closure of
k
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}
. So, we can henceforth assume, without loss of generality, that
k
is an algebraically closed field. Since
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, it is in particular
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected. So,
[
44
, Theorem 2.3]
, implies that there exists an irreducible smooth
k
-scheme
W
and a non-constant homotopy
H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\to X
that
H(0)
is dominant. Now,
[
44
, Corollary 2.4]
provides us that any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected
k
-variety over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero has negative logarithmic Kodaira dimension. However, in view of the existence of resolution of singularities for surfaces, this fact can be extended to perfect fields as well, where the appropriate extension of the theory of logarithmic Kodaira dimension is available (for a detailed account on this, see
[
177
]
). Moreover, the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of
X
implies that
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{*}=k^{*}
and that
\mathrm{Pic}(X)
is trivial and in addition, we have that
X
is also affine (
Lemma

4.3.3
), the conclusion is immediate by appealing to
[
177
, Theorem 2]
.
∎

Corollary 4.3.5
.

[
63
]

Let
X
be any smooth separated scheme over a base scheme
S
with perfect residue fields. Then the canonical morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
of relative dimension 2, then
X
is an
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-fiber space.

Proof.

For any point
s\in S
, consider the base change of
f
along
\operatorname{Spec}\ \kappa(s)\to S
. By
2.1.39
, each fiber
X_{s}\to\operatorname{Spec}\ \kappa(s)
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence. By
Theorem

4.3.4
, we have that
X_{s}\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}_{\kappa(s)}
.
∎

Having said this, we mention that such a characterization is fairly open over fields of positive characteristics, which heavily depends on establishing a suitable notion of being (separably)
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-uniruledness for
k
-varieties.

4.3.3
Non-trivial
k
-forms over
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}

One approach to analyze the proof of
Theorem

4.3.4
over imperfect fields is to study and classify the possible
k
-forms of the affine plane
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
. The following example shows that a non-trivial
k
-form of
\mathbb{A}^{2}
need not necessarily be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible.

Example 4.3.6
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic
p>0
and let
k^{\prime}
be a purely inseparable extension of
k
of degree
p
, so
k^{\prime}=k(t^{\frac{1}{p}})
for some (in fact any) element
t
of
k^{\times}
which is not a
p
-th power. Then the quotient

U=(\mathfrak{R}_{k^{\prime}/k}\mathbb{G}_{m,k^{\prime}})/\mathbb{G}_{m,k}

where
\mathfrak{R}_{k^{\prime}/k}
denote the Weil restriction (cf.
Section

C.1
), is a smooth connected unipotent
k
-group of dimension
p-1
. Explicitly, by Osterlé (
[
165
]
),
U
is isomorphic to the subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{p}
defined by the equation

x_{0}^{p}+tx_{1}^{p}+\cdots+t^{p-1}x_{p-1}^{p}-x_{p-1}=0.

By
[
4
, §4.1]
, we have
\operatorname{Pic}(U)\cong\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
. This, in particular, implies that
U
cannot be an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
k
-variety.

Why is
\operatorname{Pic}(U)
non-trivial?

To see that
\operatorname{Pic}(U)
is non-trivial, we use that
\mathrm{R}_{k^{\prime}/k}\mathbb{G}_{m,k^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to the principal open subset
N_{k^{\prime}/k}\neq 0
of
\mathbb{A}_{k}^{p}
, where
N_{k^{\prime}/k}
is the norm of the field extension, which implies that

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptH^{0}(\mathrm{R}_{k^{\prime}/k}\mathbb{G}_{m,k^{\prime}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{R}_{k^{\prime}/k}\mathbb{G}_{m,k^{\prime}}}^{*})/k^{*}=N_{k^{\prime}/k}

and

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{R}_{k^{\prime}/k}\mathbb{G}_{m,k^{\prime}})=H^{1}(\mathrm{R}_{k^{\prime}/k}\mathbb{G}_{m,k^{\prime}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{R}_{k^{\prime}/k}\mathbb{G}_{m,k^{\prime}}}^{*})=0.

From the exact sequence of
k
-group schemes

0\to\mathbb{G}_{m,k}\to\mathrm{R}_{k^{\prime}/k}\mathbb{G}_{m,k^{\prime}}\to U\to 0,

and
H^{0}(U,\mathcal{O}_{U}^{*})=k^{*}
, we get the short exact sequence

\displaystyle 1\to H^{0}(\mathrm{R}_{k^{\prime}/k}\mathbb{G}_{m,k^{\prime}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{R}_{k^{\prime}/k}\mathbb{G}_{m,k^{\prime}}}^{*})/k^{*}=\mathbb{Z}\langle N_{k^{\prime}/k}\rangle\to H^{0}(\mathbb{G}_{m,k},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{G}_{m,k}}^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}\to\operatorname{Pic}(U)\to 0

in which the image of
N_{k^{\prime}/k}
in
\mathbb{Z}
is simply
p
, which gives
\operatorname{Pic}(U)\cong\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, by Osterlé (
[
165
]
), the group
U
is known to be
k
-wound
: every
k
-morphism
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\to U
is constant with image a
k
-rational point of
U
. The conventional definition of
k
-wound due to Tits is formulated by asking that there is no non-constant
k
-group scheme homomorphism
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}\to U
, but this property is equivalent to the apriori stronger requirement that there is no non-constant morphism
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\to U
(see
[
46
, Proposition B.3.2]
for a justification).

Remark 4.3.7
.

We know that
\mathbb{A}^{2}
does not admit a non-trivial
k
-form over a separably closed extension
[
120
, Theorem 3]
. By observing more closely to the properties of the construction due to Osterlé (
[
165
]
), one see as a natural connection to the approach of
[
44
]
in proving that when
k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected
k
-variety admits non-constant morphism from
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
.

In light of the above example, we pose the following natural question.

Question 4.3.8
.

[
63
]

Does there exist a non-trivial
k
-form of
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
over an imperfect field
k
which is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible?

Any candidate satisfying the question aforementioned would produce a counterexample to the characterization of
\mathbb{A}^{2}
over imperfect fields.

4.3.4
Relative Dimension 2

Recall that a
Dedekind scheme
is a separated integral locally Noetherian scheme
D
whose local rings at its closed points are discrete valuation rings. Notable examples are the Zariski spectrum of fields, the Zariski spectrum of Dedekind domains, such as rings of integers of number fields, and smooth curves over a field.

We begin with a theorem of A. Sathaye that, in algebro-geometric language, conveys that any
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-fiber space over a discrete valuation ring of characteristic zero is necessarily a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-bundle.

Theorem 4.3.9
.

Let
R
be an equicharacteristic zero rank one discrete valuation ring. Let the unique maximal ideal of
R
be
tR
. Let
K=\text{Quot}(tR)
and
k=R/tR
and let
A=R[X_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
be an affine domain over
R
such that

A\otimes_{R}K\cong K^{[2]}\quad\text{and}\quad A\otimes_{R}k\cong k^{[2]}.

Then
A\cong R^{[2]}
.

Proof.

This is due to
[
178
, Theorem 1]
.
∎

Remark 4.3.10
.

In relation to the embedding problem
Section

3.2.1
, this theorem provides the following solution for affine three spaces:
Let
k
be a field of characteristic zero. Let
f\in k^{[3]}
be an element in the polynomial ring
k[X,Y,Z]
. Further, assume that

k[X,Y,Z]\otimes_{k[f]}k[f]\cong k(f)^{[2]}

and all the partial derivatives have no common zeroes in
k^{3}
, i.e.,

\frac{\partial f}{\partial X}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial Y}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial Z}=0.

Then
k[X,Y,Z]/(f-\lambda)\cong k^{[2]}
, for every
\lambda\in k
. In other words, it implies that

k[X,Y,Z]\cong k[f]^{[2]}.

For more background, we redirect the reader to
[
178
,
157
]
.

For our purposes, the remarkable result (
Theorem

4.3.9
) coupled with functoriality in the motivic homotopy theory gives us the following structure theorem (
Theorem

4.3.11
) and consequently, an elegant characterization of
\mathbb{A}^{2}
in relative dimension 2 (
Corollary

4.3.13
):

Theorem 4.3.11
.

[
63
]

Let
X
be a smooth scheme of relative dimension
2
over a Dede-kind scheme
D
with characteristic zero residue fields. Furthermore, let the canonical morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be affine. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
,

2.

f
is a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-bundle.

Proof.

The implication
(2)\implies(1)
is clear: a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-bundle is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence. For the converse, suppose that
X
is relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible of dimension 2 over
D
. Then, by
Corollary

4.3.5
,
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to D
is an
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-fiber space over a discrete valuation ring of characteristic zero. The conclusion is evident from
Theorem

4.3.9
.
∎

The hypothesis that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to D
is an affine morphism is indispensable, as in comparison to the one-dimensional case (where this hypothesis was not needed), which raises the following question:

Question 4.3.12
.

[
63
]

Is there a relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-fiber space
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to D
for which
f
is not an affine morphism?

As a consequence of
Theorem

4.3.11
, we have the unique characterization of the affine plane over a Dedekind scheme.

Corollary 4.3.13
.

[
63
]

A smooth affine scheme
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to D
over an
affine
Dedekind scheme
D
with characteristic zero residue fields is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{D}
if and only if both of the following holds:

1.

X
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible surface in
\mathcal{H}(D)
, and

2.

the relative canonical sheaf of differentials
\omega_{f}
is trivial.

Proof.

One direction is obvious: the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{D}
is smooth,
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, and the relative canonical sheaf
\omega_{\mathbb{A}^{2}_{D}/D}
is trivial.

Suppose the converse: by
Theorem

4.3.11
, we have that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to D
is a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-bundle. Since
D
is affine, by
[
28
, Theorem 4.9]
, the map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to D
is in fact isomorphic over
D
to a vector bundle
p\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}E=\operatorname{Spec}_{D}(\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}\mathcal{E})\to D
for some locally free sheaf
\mathcal{E}
of rank
2
on
D
. Taking relative differentials, we have that
p^{*}\mathcal{E}\cong\Omega_{f}
and consequently, applying the
\det
functor, that
p^{*}(\det\mathcal{E})\cong\omega_{f}
. On the other hand, since
D
is
1
-dimensional, it follows from
[
182
, Theorem 1]
that
\mathcal{E}
is isomorphic to the direct sum of
\mathcal{O}_{D}
and an invertible sheaf
\mathcal{L}
such that
f^{*}\mathcal{L}\cong\omega_{f}
. Since
f^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Pic}(D)\to\operatorname{Pic}(X)
is an isomorphism, the triviality of
\omega_{f}
is equivalent to that of
\mathcal{E}
and the assertion follows.
∎

Zariski Cancellation for Relative Surfaces

An important consequence is the relative variant of Zariski Cancellations over certain Dedekind schemes.

Corollary 4.3.14
.

[
63
]

Let
D
be an affine Dedekind scheme with characteristic zero residue fields. Then for a smooth affine scheme
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to D
of relative dimension 2 over a Dedekind scheme
D
, the isomorphism
X\times_{D}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{D}\cong\mathbb{A}^{3}_{D}
implies that
X\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}_{D}
.

Proof.

Again, the given isomorphism
X\times_{D}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{D}\cong\mathbb{A}^{3}_{D}
implies that
\omega_{\mathbb{A}^{3}_{D}/D}=p_{1}^{*}\omega_{f}\otimes p_{2}^{*}\omega_{\mathbb{A}^{2}_{D}/D}\cong p_{1}^{*}\omega_{f}
and the induced isomorphism of Picard groups
p_{1}^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathrm{Pic}(X)\to\mathrm{Pic}(X\times_{D}\mathbb{A}^{2}_{D})
implies that
\omega_{f}
has to be trivial. But
X
is stably isomorphic to an affine space, which implies that
X
is eventually
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in
\mathcal{H}(D)
(see references from
Section

3.2.5
). The desired conclusion now follows from
Corollary

4.3.13
.
∎

Counter-examples to
Theorem

4.3.11
when char
k=p>0

The following examples show that the analog of
Theorem

4.3.11
does not hold in positive characteristics.

Example 4.3.15
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristics
p>0
. Consider the affine three-fold
V=\operatorname{Spec}R
where

\displaystyle R=\frac{k[X,Y,Z,T]}{\langle X^{m}Y-Z^{p^{e}}-T-T^{sp}\rangle}\quad\text{where}\quad m\geq 2,\quad p^{e}\nmid sp\ \text{and}\ sp\nmid p^{e}.

Define a map
\pi_{x}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
given by the projection onto the
X
-coordinate. This map
\pi_{X}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-fiber space. From
[
7
, Theorem 3.4]
, it follows that the ring
R
is stably a polynomial ring over
k[X]
. In particular, this shows that the affine variety
V
is relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k})
and consequently also
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in
\mathcal{H}(k)
. However,
\pi_{X}
is
not
a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-bundle (ibid, Theorem 5.1).

The following example provides us with an
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-fiber space over a discrete valuation ring with residue fields of positive characteristics, which portrays the obstruction to extending
Corollary

4.3.14
.

Example 4.3.16
.

Let
R
be a Noetherian integral domain and let
p
be any non-invertible prime in
R
. Choose
\pi
to be any non-invertible, non-zero divisor in
R
. Define an
R
-algebra

A=R[X,Y,Z]/\langle-X^{p^{e}}+Y+Y^{sp}+\pi Z\rangle,

where
e,s
are positive integers such that
sp\nmid p^{e}
and
p^{e}\nmid sp
. Then for an affine variety
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}A\to\operatorname{Spec}R
, we have the following due to
[
7
, Corollary 5.3]
:

1.

The map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
is an
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-fiber space (
20
),

2.

The affine variety
X
is stably
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, that is,
X\times_{\operatorname{Spec}R}\mathbb{A}^{1}\cong\mathbb{A}^{3}

3.

The affine variety is not the affine 2-space,
X\ncong\mathbb{A}^{2}_{R}
.

The property (2) and (3) gives the negative solution to the Zariski cancellation problem over
A
.

Vignette

Let
X
be a smooth scheme of dimension
n<3
over a field
k
. Then, due to the characterization results that we have presented in this chapter, we obtain the following beautiful characterization:

•

X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
\iff

X\cong\mathbb{A}^{n}
and so its logarithmic Kodaira is negative,
\bar{\kappa}(X)=-\infty
. When
n=2
with
k
uncountable,
X
being
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected already implies that
\bar{\kappa}(X)=-\infty
(however, the converse is false: take
X=\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
).

This makes one ponder the following questions.

Question 4.3.17
.

To what extent does
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility imply
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connectedness from dimensions
\geq 3
and vice-versa?

Question 4.3.18
.

Do
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible varieties have negative logarithmic Kodaira and vice-versa from dimensions
\geq 3
?

4.4
Relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility in Higher Dimensions

The characterization of Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-bundles up to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility does not hold from relative dimensions
n\geq 3
. In this chapter, we will explain this perspective and present a geometric proof of the fact that
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber bundles produce an abundance of examples of relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes (
Theorem

4.4.3
). This will be explained via a concrete illustration (
Example

4.4.5
).

The characterization of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
in higher dimensions

The characterization of affine spaces up to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility is no longer true starting from dimensions
n\geq 3
, even over the "kindest" base fields. For fields of positive characteristics, this can already be deduced by combining the negative solution of Zariski Cancellations as discussed in
Section

3.2.4
and
Lemma

3.2.14
. Over a field of characteristic zero, the Koras-Russell threefolds give the first examples of a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible variety that is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{k}
as introduced in
Section

3.2.3
(we will study this family in detail in
Chapter

5
).

In higher dimensions, the beautiful work of Asok-Doran
[
11
]
established that from dimensions
n\geq 4
, there exist abundant examples of smooth
n
-schemes that are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible but are not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}
. They demonstrated this by constructing everywhere stable
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-actions on affine spaces with strictly quasi-affine quotients. Towards this, they exploited an
algebraic characterization
(
ibid, Theorem 4.20
) that, in essence, characterizes when a given
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action is
everywhere stable
. Their main result is the following (
[
11
, Theorem 5.1]
):

Theorem
.

For every
m\geq 4
, there exists a denumerably infinite collection of pairwise non-isomorphic
m
-dimensional exotic
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible varieties, each admitting an embedding into a smooth affine variety with pure codimension 2 smooth boundary.

In fact, starting from dimension
\geq 6
, there exists a moduli of exotic smooth varieties arising from the quotient of an action of an unipotent group on an affine space. We include below the statement for the reader’s clarity (
[
11
, Theorem 5.3]
).

Theorem
.

For every
m\geq 6
and every
n\geq 0
, there exists a connected
n
-dimensional scheme
S
and a smooth morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
of relative dimension
m
, whose fibers over
k
-points are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible and strictly quasi-affine and non-isomorphic.

It is important to note that all the varieties obtained as a result of this construction are strictly
2
2

2

quasi-affine but not affine, e.g.,
\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
, for
n\geq 2
quasi-affine smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes which are not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}
. In fact, a simplest prototype of such an occurrence, a smooth (exotic) fourfold, was already studied by Winkelmann in the context of complex geometry.

Theorem 4.4.1
.

The following result has been abridged from several results sourced from
[
214
, §2]
:

1.

There exists a unipotent group
U\cong(\mathbb{C}^{1},+)
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{5}
given by a certain quadratic transformation such that the quotient
X=\mathbb{C}^{5}/U
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{4}
, but not biholomorphic. In particular,
X
is a strictly quasi-affine variety that is a non-Stein manifold (hence
X\ncong\mathbb{C}^{4}
).

2.

There exists a unipotent group
U\cong(\mathbb{C}^{2},+)
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{6}
given by certain affine linear transformation such that the quotient
X=\mathbb{C}^{6}/U
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{4}
, but not biholomorphic. In particular,
X
is a strictly quasi-affine variety that is a non-Stein manifold (hence
X\ncong\mathbb{C}^{4}
).

We will revisit this prototype shortly (cf.
Example

4.4.5
). Intrigued by these results, one can ponder on the following question (
[
11
, Question 5.10]
):

Question 4.4.2
.

Does there exist a 3-dimensional quasi-affine quotient of
\mathbb{A}^{4}
by
\mathbb{G}_{a}
?.

As a special case, it has been shown that any everywhere stable
\mathbb{G}_{a}
action on
\mathbb{A}^{4}
associated to the triangular locally nilpotent derivation always produces an affine space quotient, that is,
\mathbb{A}^{4}/\mathbb{G}_{a}\cong\mathbb{A}^{3}
(
[
58
, Theorem 2.1]
).

Therefore, in coherence with our analysis on low relative dimensions, the program of characterizing the affine
n
-space via
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber spaces fails in higher dimensions. In other words, for every
n\geq 3
, one can now produce relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes that are no longer
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber spaces. We give several examples of this fact in this current section (note that this would already fail in low relative dimensions under certain assumptions: see
Example

4.2.11
and
Example

4.2.12
).

4.4.1
Families of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractible Schemes via
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber Spaces: A Geometric Proof

In
[
10
]
, the authors study the representability of motivic spheres by smooth schemes over a commutative unital ring
k
and they show that a certain family of (split) smooth affine quadric hypersurfaces
Q_{2n}
in
\mathbb{A}^{2n+1}
(defined below) have the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy type of a motivic sphere (see
Section

5.3.1
), hence solving a representability problem for these classes of motivic spheres.

\displaystyle Q_{2n-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}k[x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}]/\langle\sum_{i}x_{i}y_{i}-1\rangle

(4.1)

\displaystyle Q_{2n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}k[x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n},z]/\langle\sum_{i}x_{i}y_{i}-z(1+z)\rangle

In particular,
[
10
, Theorem 2.2.5]
shows that for any
commutative ring
k
,

\displaystyle Q_{2n-1}\simeq(S^{1})^{n-1}\wedge(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge n}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\quad Q_{2n}\simeq(S^{1})^{n}\wedge(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}\quad\text{for all}\ n\geq 0.

(4.2)

These results were already known to be true in the
S^{1}
-stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category due to Morel (See
ibid
, P. 2), and the
Eq.

4.2
shows that it is even true unstably! The proof essentially takes into account that
\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
is equipped with the Nisnevich topology, which brings the motivic purity (
Theorem

2.1.56
) into the act. In the same paper, they also construct the following example: let

E_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x_{1}=\dots=x_{n}=0;z=-1\}\subset Q_{2n}

be the closed subscheme of
\mathbb{A}^{2n+1}
. Then by setting,

X_{2n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Q_{2n}\backslash E_{n}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2n+1}

they show that each of these subvarieties
X_{2n}
are strictly quasi-affine varieties that are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over
\operatorname{Spec}k
. Furthermore, they also proved that
X_{2n}
’s cannot be obtained as the quotient of an unipotent group action on affine spaces. This strikes a stark dichotomous behavior with topology, where every such contractible space is indeed a free quotient of an affine space. Aligned with our context, we show that all these
X_{2n}
’s produce abundant examples of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes
relative to an affine space
base. To make this more comprehensible, let us now prove a fact that the
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber spaces
always
form a class of relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible spaces (
Theorem

4.4.3
). One major consequence of this will be that the family of smooth quasi-affine subvarieties
X_{2n}
’s constructed above even becomes relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible. The following theorem is, in essence, a consequence of Asanuma’s Stable Structure theorem from
Section

3.2.5
.

Theorem 4.4.3
.

[
63
]

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be a separated
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space over a regular scheme
S
. Then
X
is a relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme in
\mathcal{H}(S)
.

Proof.

Observe that the assumptions made on
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
are stable under base change to a cover of
S
by Zariski affine open subsets. In this view, we can assume, without loss of generality, that
S
is affine. Indeed, this follows from the local nature of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility - that being a Nisnevich locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle can be tested on affine open neighborhoods. Since
S
is regular and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a smooth separated morphism,
X
is a separated regular scheme. So, by
Jouanolou–Thomason homotopy lemma
(see e.g.
[
19
, Lemma 3.1.4]
) there exists a Zariski locally trivial torsor
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to X
under a vector bundle
p\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}E\to X
, say of rank
r
, whose total space
V
is an affine scheme. For every point
s\in S
, the induced
E_{s}
-torsor

\rho_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V_{s}\to X_{s}\cong\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\kappa(s)}

is isomorphic to the trivial one
p_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}E_{s}\to X_{s}
because
X_{s}
is affine. Since on the other hand
E_{s}
is isomorphic to the trivial vector bundle on
X_{s}\cong\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\kappa(s)}
by Quillen-Suslin theorem
[
171
]
, we conclude that
\rho_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V_{s}\to X_{s}
is isomorphic to the trivial
\mathbb{A}^{r}
-bundle

\mathrm{pr}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{r}_{\kappa(s)}\times_{\kappa(s)}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\kappa(s)}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\kappa(s)}.

Thus,
g=f\circ\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to S
is an affine
\mathbb{A}^{n+r}
-fiber space over the regular affine scheme
S
. Now it follows from
[
7
, Corollary 3.5]
that there exists
m\geq 0
such that
g\circ\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\times_{S}\mathbb{A}_{S}^{m}\to S
is isomorphic to a vector bundle
q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}F\to S
of rank
n+r+m
over
S
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{m}_{S}=F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{m}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

This implies in particular that

g\circ\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\times_{S}\mathbb{A}_{S}^{m}\to S

is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. Since
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{m}_{S}\to V
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\mathrm{Spc}_{V}
whence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
by
Corollary

2.1.36
(2), it follows from
Corollary

2.1.37
that
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. Since on the other hand
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to X
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{X}
whence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
by
Corollary

2.1.36
(2), we conclude again from
Corollary

2.1.37
that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\mathrm{Spc}_{S}
.
∎

Remark 4.4.4
.

Let us also note that one can now prove
Theorem

4.4.3
using the highly sophisticated algebraic techniques coming from motivic homotopy theory (e.g., via
2.1.48
). Nevertheless, we would like to stress that the proof presented here is useful, as it establishes the geometric mechanism of the aforementioned algebraic fact!

Illustrative Example: Family of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractible Smooth Quadrics

We show that the family of smooth affine quadrics as in
Eq.

4.1
appears as a torsor under a vector bundle projected to an affine space. In particular, these quadrics illustrate
Theorem

4.4.3
for a separated
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-fiber space
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\mathbb{A}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}
with strictly quasi-affine total space
X
. The following example also shows that subvariety considered by
[
214
]
gives us a simplest illustration of the
Theorem

4.4.3
in relative dimension 4:

Example 4.4.5
.

[
63
]

Let
S=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[x,y]=\mathbb{A}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and consider the smooth affine fourfold

V\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{xv-yu+z(z-1)=0\}\subset S\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{Z}}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[x,y][z,u,v].

The projection
\mathrm{pr}_{1}|_{V}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to S
is a smooth morphism whose restriction over
S\backslash\{(0,0)\}
is a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-bundle. On the other hand,
(\mathrm{pr}_{1}|_{V})^{-1}(0,0)
is the disjoint union of two copies

\displaystyle F_{0}=\{x=y=z=0\}\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad F_{1}=\{x=y=0,z=1\}

of
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. The restriction of
\mathrm{pr}_{1}|_{V}
to
X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=V\backslash F_{1}
is then an
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-fiber space
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
whose total space
X
is a strictly quasi-affine
k
-variety. It follows in particular that
f
cannot be a locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-bundle, neither in Zariski topology nor in any of the finer topologies such as Nisnevich, étale, fppf, or fpqc. Nevertheless, as first noticed in
[
11
]
,
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}
. We now observe that for essentially the same reasons,
X
has the stronger property to be relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over
S
. Namely, let

h=\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=S\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}]\cong\mathbb{A}^{5}_{\mathbb{Z}}\to S

be the trivial
\mathbb{A}^{3}
-bundle over
S
and consider the
S
-morphism
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W\to X
defined by

(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\mapsto(u,v,z)=(yt_{3}-(xt_{2}-yt_{1}-1)t_{2},xt_{3}-(xt_{2}-yt_{1}-1)t_{1},xt_{2}-yt_{1})

so that we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W=\mathbb{A}^{5}_{\mathbb{Z}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S=\mathbb{A}^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The morphism
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W\to X
is a
\mathbb{G}_{a,X}
-torsor
[
214
, §3]
, whence is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{X}
and in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
by
Corollary

2.1.36
(2). On the other hand,
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W\to S
being a trivial
\mathbb{A}^{3}
-bundle, it is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. These two properties imply, in turn, by
Corollary

2.1.37
that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is a relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\mathrm{Spc}_{S}
.

Remark 4.4.6
.

Several higher dimensional analogues of the setup in
Example

4.4.5
have been successively constructed and studied in
[
11
,
10
,
14
]
leading to many new examples of smooth strictly quasi-affine
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes
X
over an affine base scheme
B
having the structure of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber spaces
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\mathbb{A}^{n}_{B}
over the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{B}
. This holds, for instance, for the family of smooth quasi-affine schemes

f_{n,\mathbf{m}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathrm{pr}_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X_{n,\mathbf{m}}=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\big\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{m_{i}}y_{i}+z(z-1)=0\}\backslash\{x_{1}=\ldots=x_{n}=z-1=0\big\}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}_{B},

where
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
is multi-index of positive integers. In the case where
B
is regular,
Theorem

4.4.3
implies that for such schemes, the
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space
f_{n,\mathbf{m}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X_{n,\mathbf{m}}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}_{B}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{B}}
, providing in turn, since
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{B}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over
B
, a different proof that all these schemes are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over
B
.

To conclude, recall that the Dolgačev-Veǐsfeǐler Conjecture admits a negative solution over fields of positive characteristics due to the counter-example of Asanuma (
Example

4.3.15
) and thus, leaving it wide open over fields of characteristic zero. The only known cases are due to
[
118
,
119
]
in dimension 1 and
[
178
]
in dimension 2, whenever the base schemes contain a field of characteristic zero. On the other hand, starting from dimension
n\geq 3
, we have seen in this chapter that there are numerous sources of relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes non-isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}
. Hence, the relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence of the morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
does not, in general, allow us to conclude that
X
is an
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space whenever
n\geq 3
. Moreover, even if we do know that
X
is an
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-fiber space, one has no clue whether it will be a locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-bundle in Zariski (or even in finer topologies)!

Chapter 5
Koras-Russell Prototypes and Exotic Motivic Spheres

The results presented in this chapter are sourced from the article
[
141
]
.

Chapter Summary

We establish the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of the classical Koras-Russell threefolds over perfect fields (
5.1.7
). This will allow us to extend its relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility over integers (
Theorem

5.1.8
) and, further, over Noetherian schemes (
Theorem

5.1.9
) by exploiting the intricate relationship between the relative and fiberwise data (
2.1.48
) in the unstable setting. Following this, we study the generalized prototype of Koras-Russell threefolds in higher dimensions and extend their
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility over perfect fields (
Theorem

5.2.1
), and systematically relatively over Noetherian schemes (
Corollary

5.2.2
). As a major application of these results, we will prove the existence of exotic spheres in motivic homotopy theory in every dimension
\geq 4
(
Theorem

5.3.8
) over infinite perfect fields.

5.1
The Family of Koras-Russell Threefolds

Recall that a smooth affine scheme
X\to S
of dimension
n
is said to be
exotic
if it is relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible but not isomorphic, as
S
-schemes, to the affine
n
-space
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
. Moreover, we have seen that even under the assumption that
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over any "nicest" base fields, the characterization of
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
breaks away in higher dimensions. The same is true in dimension 3, and this is due to the
Koras-Russell threefolds
, whose sibling was already introduced in
Section

3.2.3
. In what follows, we will discuss the family of Koras-Russell threefolds of the first kind in detail, which by now has become an independent and vibrant area of study in its own right, interpolating between motivic homotopy theory and affine algebraic geometry. Building on this, we study the higher-dimensional prototypes of these Koras-Russell threefolds, called
generalized Koras-Russell varieties
in arbitrary dimensions
n\geq 4
.

5.1.1
Extension to Perfect Fields

We begin with the definition of the classical Koras-Russell threefolds.

Definition 25
.

Let
k
be any perfect field. Then we define the
Koras-Russell threefold of first kind

\mathcal{K}
as the smooth affine variety described by the explicit polynomial equation

\mathcal{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x^{m}z=x+y^{r}+t^{s}\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{4}_{k}

(5.1)

where the integers
m\geq 2
and
r,s\geq 1
such that
\text{gcd}\ (r,s)=1
.

Figure 5.1
:
Fibers of
\mathcal{K}
when projected to
x
-axis; the generic fiber is shown in red

Traditionally, Koras-Russell threefolds have been studied extensively only over a field of characteristic zero. But in the light of coprimality of the integers
r,s
and the usefulness of the coordinate "
x
" to make the Jacobian criterion for smoothness work, it is also well-defined over any perfect field. In particular, this means
\mathcal{K}
is defined over the finite fields
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and over arithmetic curves (e.g., the integers
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}
). The affine variety
\mathcal{K}
exhibits the following incredible properties:

•

\mathcal{K}
is topologically contractible (
[
124
]
), whenever
k
admits an embedding into
\mathbb{C}
,

•

The cylinders
\mathcal{K}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
have trivial Makar-Limanov value over char
k=0
,
\operatorname{ML}(\mathcal{K}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\cong\operatorname{ML}(\operatorname{Spec}k)
(
[
68
]
)

•

\mathcal{K}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible both in the stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
[
102
]
and in the unstable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
[
60
]
when the base field is of characteristic zero,

•

\mathcal{K}
is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}
as witnessed by the Makar-Limanov invariant:
\operatorname{ML}(\mathcal{K})\ncong\operatorname{ML}(\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{C}})
.

All these properties make
\mathcal{K}
an algebro-geometric analog of the Whitehead manifold
\mathcal{W}
from
Section

1.2.1
, and hence make it difficult to construct algebraic invariants to distinguish it among
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible smooth affine threefolds. Closely associated with this first kind, there are certain threefolds of the second kind
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
as illustrated in
Eq.

3.3
, whose stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility has been proven in
[
102
]
. As we will shortly see that both of these threefolds contain a special affine line
L\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x=y=t=0\}
. But the crucial step is to conclude that
\mathcal{K}\backslash L\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
, which, as the authors in
[
60
]
remark, is not apparent for the second kind
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}
. The main tool that makes this possible for the first kind is
Lemma

5.1.3
whose analog remains open for the second kind!

In
[
60
, Theorem 1.1]
, the authors prove the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of the Koras-Russell threefold of first kind over a field of characteristic zero. In the following sections, we prove that this, in fact, can be extended over any perfect field and over certain base schemes. To give the context of the strategy, we shall now begin by explaining the elements from
[
60
]
.

5.1.2
The Unstable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility of
\mathcal{K}

The basic ingredient towards the proof is the categorical tool known as the
very weak five lemma
(cf.
Lemma

A.2.26
). To adapt this to our situation, we first note that the threefold
\mathcal{K}
contains a hypersurface given by
\{z=0\}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=P\subset\mathcal{K}
. It can be shown that it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}
with coordinates
y
and
t
, that is,
P\cong\operatorname{Spec}k[y,t]
. More crucially,
\mathcal{K}
also contains an affine line given by

L\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x=y=t=0\}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}\quad\text{such that}\quad L\cong\operatorname{Spec}(k[z]).

Now, considering the complement, we have that
P\backslash L\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
. By the very definitions, the varieties
L
and
P
intersect transversally in
\mathbb{A}^{4}
at the point
(0,0,0,0)
which gives us a pullback diagram as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\backslash L\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}\backslash L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

(5.2)

Consider the associated cofiber sequence given by the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{2}/\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}\backslash L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{K}\backslash{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

(5.3)

In the light of
Lemma

A.2.26
, we only need to show that the morphisms
i
and
j
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
.

5.1.3
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak Equivalence of
j

The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence of the morphism of schemes
j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{2}/\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\to\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{K}\backslash L
follows from the purity isomorphism in motivic homotopy theory. The defining equation of
\mathcal{K}
gives us a description of the normal cone of
L
inside
\mathcal{K}
, which can be seen by rewriting

\mathcal{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x(x^{m-1}z-1)=y^{r}+t^{s}\}

and the fact that the regular function (
x^{m-1}z-1
) is invertible in
L
(in fact, it is equal to -1 on
L)
, we have that the normal cone
N_{\mathcal{K}}(L)
is generated by
y
and
t
. Coupling this data with the homotopy purity (
Theorem

2.1.56
), we have that

\mathcal{K}/(\mathcal{K}\backslash{L})\simeq\operatorname{Th}(N_{\mathcal{K}}(L))\simeq L_{+}\wedge(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge 2}.

Again via the purity isomorphism, the quotient space
\mathbb{A}^{2}/\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
can be identified with
(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge 2}
(see for e.g.,
[
6
, §4.6]
or
[
10
, §2.1]
). The inclusion
\operatorname{Spec}k\simeq\{0\}\hookrightarrow L\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}
induces a the composite

\operatorname{Spec}k\wedge(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge 2}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}/\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\xrightarrow{j}\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{K}\backslash L\simeq L_{+}\wedge(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge 2}

which is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence (cf.
[
207
, Lemma 2.1]
) and so is
j
.

5.1.4
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak Equivalence of
i

To show that the morphism
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\to\mathcal{K}\backslash L
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence, we first produce an explicit
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\backslash L\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
in
Section

5.1.4
. Then using the functoriality of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Brouwer degree (
Lemma

2.2.4
), we show that the composite
g\circ i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
.

The explicit
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence via Zariski local triviality

The upshot is to produce an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\backslash L\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
by constructing a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle over
\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
. Consider the affine scheme
\mathcal{K}(s)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x^{m}z=x+y^{r}+t^{s}\}
and the associated strictly quasi-affine scheme
\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash\{0\}
. Observe that when
s=1
, we have that
\mathcal{K}(1)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x^{m}z=x+y^{r}+t\}\cong\{x^{m}z-x-y^{r}=t\}
which by the change of coordinates is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{k}
. As a result, we have

\mathcal{K}(1)\backslash L\cong\mathbb{A}^{3}\backslash L\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}.

The following result is proven in
[
60
, Proposition 3.1]
for fields of characteristic zero. Here, we show that this trick can be extended to perfect fields as well. In particular, we prove the existence of a quasi-affine fourfold
W
that is the total space of a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle over
\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L
, for all
s\geq 1
. This fourfold
W
is produced by exploiting the Danielewski fiber product trick (
[
52
]
) that was initially constructed to produce a counterexample to the Zariski Cancellation Problem for surfaces (for more background, see
[
67
]
).

Proposition 5.1.1
.

[
141
]

Let
k
be any perfect field. Then for every
s\geq 2
, there exists a smooth strictly quasi-affine fourfold
W
which is simultaneously the total space of a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundles over
\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L
, for all
s\geq 1
.

Proof.

The desired fourfold
W
is constructed as the fiber product of strictly quasi-affine schemes over an algebraic space
\mathfrak{S}
whose existence we prove in
Lemma

5.1.3
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}(1)\backslash L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}

Lemma

5.1.3
shows that for all
s\geq 1
, the quasi-affine threefolds
\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L
all have a structure of an étale locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}(s)\to\mathfrak{S}
fibered over the same algebraic space
\mathfrak{S}
. A priori, by taking the fiber product

W\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mathcal{K}(1)\backslash L)\times_{\mathfrak{S}}(\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L),

We see that
W
only has the structure of an algebraic space. But notice that the natural projections
\mathrm{pr}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W\to\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L
(for all
s\geq 1
) are an affine morphism and
\mathcal{K}\backslash L
is a strictly quasi-affine scheme, which consequently implies that
W
also has to be a strictly quasi-affine scheme. The fact that this étale equivalence descends also to the Zariski topology is because the transition group, which is
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{1})=\mathbb{G}_{m}\ltimes\mathbb{G}_{a}
, is special
[
90
, §5]
.
∎

As an immediate consequence of
5.1.1
, we get our desired result.

Corollary 5.1.2
.

The map
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence for all
s\geq 1
.

Proof.

This follows from the observation that Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{n}
-bundles preserve
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence (see e.g.,
[
19
, Lemma 3.1.3]
).
∎

5.1.5
Strategy to Construct
\mathfrak{S}
over Perfect Fields

Briefly speaking, the existence of the claimed algebraic space follows due to a fundamental fact in moduli theory that if an algebraic group acts freely on a scheme, then the quotient space exists and is an algebraic space (see
[
123
]
or
[
190
,
Tag 071R
]
). When the char
k=0
, producing such a
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action on the affine variety
\mathcal{K}
is equivalent to giving a locally nilpotent derivation
D
on its coordinate ring
\Gamma(\mathcal{K})
defined via the exponential map (cf.
Section

C.1
). However, if the char
k=p>0
, this simple correspondence does not a priori apply, for example,
1/p!
in the exponential function is not well-defined. Nevertheless, one still gets a correspondence by considering a locally finite iterative higher derivation instead of a single locally nilpotent derivation. The former is obtained by considering the truncated version of
\exp(tD)
(see for e.g.,
[
155
, Part I, §1]
,
[
166
, §5]
). Therefore, assuming the existence of such a family of iterative higher derivations, we have that for a
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action on
\mathcal{K}(s)
its algebraic quotient coincides with the projection
\mathrm{pr}_{x,t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{4}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}
restricted to
q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}(s)\to\mathbb{A}^{2}
with the fixed point locus
L
. Hence, we obtain an induced fixed point free
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action on
\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L)/\mathbb{G}_{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

it factors through an étale
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-torsor over its geometric quotient
(\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L)/\mathbb{G}_{a}
as above. The desired algebraic space
\mathfrak{S}
can be constructed by an étale equivalence relation. Let us consider the fibers of the morphism
q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\backslash L\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
. Observe that for the fibers over points
x\neq 0
, the Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle is actually a trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle because if
x\neq 0
, one has an element of the form
a=f(y)/x^{m}
, for some suitable polynomial
f(y)\in k[y]
such that
D(a)=1
, (i.e.,
a
is a slice). In other words, for a principal affine open set
U_{x}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x\neq 0\}
and a point
b\in U_{x}
, we have that
q^{-1}(b)\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}k[x^{\pm 1},t]\times\operatorname{Spec}k[y]
.

On the other hand, the fiber over the punctured affine line
C_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x=0\}=\operatorname{Spec}k[t^{\pm 1}]
is given by
C\times\operatorname{Spec}k[z]
where

C\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}k[t^{\pm 1},y]/\langle y^{r}+t^{s}\rangle.

The curve
C\to C_{0}
can be identified with a finite étale cover of degree
r
with respect to the projection
(y,t)\mapsto t
. This is the exact crossroad of the fields of positive characteristics and that of characteristic zero. If char
k=p\mid r
, then one cannot form a finite étale cover as claimed above. But since we have that gcd
(r,s)=1
, one can rework the proof as follows. If
p\mid r
, then one can then consider the finite étale cover

\displaystyle\operatorname{Spec}k[t^{\pm 1},y]/\langle y^{r}+t^{s}\rangle=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C\to C_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}(k[t^{\pm 1}])

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt(y,t)\mapsto y

of degree
s
with respect to the other coordinate, where we can still extract the roots (i.e.,
(y^{r})^{1/s}
exists in
k
) since in this case
p\nmid s
by the coprimality.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

[
141
]

For any perfect field
k
, there exists a smooth algebraic space
\delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
such that for every
s\geq 1
, the map
q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L\to\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
factors through an étale-locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L\to\mathfrak{S}
.

The working mechanism of the proof follows from a more general principle involved in constructing an algebraic space from a surface by replacing a desired curve by its finite étale covering (
[
61
, §1.0.1]
). For our context, we will now explain the strategy and the earlier-promised étale equivalence following
[
60
]
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let us assume that char
k=p\nmid r
. Observe that the strictly quasi-affine threefolds
\mathcal{K}\backslash L
are covered by two principal affine opens, (say)
V_{x}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x\neq 0\}
where it is already a trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle as observed above, and
V_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{t\neq 0\}
where the suitable modification has to be implemented. It is now enough to build an algebraic space
\mathfrak{S}_{t}
locally on
V_{t}
and prove that it compatibly extends to the desired algebraic space globally.

We first prove that there (locally) exists an algebraic space
\delta_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathfrak{S}_{t}\to U_{t}
such that
q|_{{V_{t}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V_{t}\to U_{t}
factors through an étale locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle
\rho_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V_{t}\to\mathfrak{S}_{t}
. Moreover, such a factorization via
\delta_{t}
should be an isomorphism when restricted to the overlap
U_{xt}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=U_{x}\cap U_{t}
. Here,
U_{x}=\operatorname{Spec}k[x^{\pm 1},t]
and
U_{t}=\operatorname{Spec}k[x,t^{\pm 1}]
are corresponding principal affine open subsets in
\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
. The desired (global) algebraic space
\delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
is then obtained by gluing
U_{x}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{t}
by the identity along
U_{xt}
and such that
\delta^{-1}_{t}(U_{xt})\cong U_{xt}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}(s)\backslash L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{t}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{t}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{S}_{t}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{|_{V_{t}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}}

Let
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Spec}R=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C\to C_{0}
be the Galois closure of the finite étale map
h_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{1}\to C_{0}
with

C_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}\big(k[t^{\pm 1}][y]\big)/\big(y^{r}+t^{s}\big).

In other words,
C
gives the normalization of
C_{1}
in the Galois closure
\kappa
with respect to the field extension

k(t)\hookrightarrow k(t)[y]/\big(y^{r}+t^{s}\big).

The above-mentioned Galois closure
\kappa
is precisely obtained by adjoining the
r
th-roots to
t^{s}
, which is valid since
p\nmid r
by assumption. By the coprimality of
r
and
s
, this is equivalent to just adjoining the
r
th-roots to
t
along with all the
r
th-roots of
-1
. Thus, also note that neither
\kappa
nor
C_{1}
or
C
depend on
s
. By construction, this gives us an étale
G
-torsor
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C\to C_{0}
with the Galois group
G=\text{Gal}(\kappa/k(t))
and a factorization

h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C\xrightarrow{h_{1}}C_{1}\xrightarrow{h_{0}}C_{0}

where
h_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C\to C_{1}
is an étale torsor under some subgroup
H\leq G
of index
r
equal to the degree of
h_{1}
. This corresponds to the fact that we have made an
r
-fold Galois cover of
C_{0}
. To complete the proof, we show that there exists a scheme
S
obtained by gluing
r
copies of
S_{\bar{g}}
, where
\bar{g}\in G/H
, by the identity along curves
C_{0}\times C
such that the group
G
acts freely on
S_{\bar{g}}
giving rise to a geometric quotient
\mathfrak{S}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=S/G
in the category of algebraic spaces in the form of an étale
G
-torsor over
\mathfrak{S}
. Moreover, this local morphism glues to a global
G
-invariant morphism which then descends to the corresponding (global) quotient. By definition, the polynomial

R[y]\ni y^{r}+t^{s}=\prod_{\bar{g}\in G/H}\big(y-\lambda_{\bar{g}}\big),

splits over
\kappa
for some elements
\lambda_{\bar{g}}\in R
and
\bar{g}\in G/H
. The Galois group
G
then acts transitively on the quotient
G/H
via

g^{\prime}\cdot\lambda_{\bar{g}}=\lambda_{\overline{{(g^{\prime})^{-1}}\cdot g}}.

Since
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{1}\to C_{0}
is étale, we have that for distinct elements
\bar{g},\bar{g}^{\prime}\in G/H
, the element
\lambda_{\bar{g}}-\lambda_{\bar{g}^{\prime}}
is an invertible regular function on
C
. Consequently, we can find a collection of elements
\sigma_{\bar{g}(x)}\in B=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}R[x]
with corresponding residue classes
\lambda_{\bar{g}}\in R=B/xB
modulo
x
on which
G
acts via

g^{\prime}\cdot\sigma_{\bar{g}}(x)\mapsto\sigma_{\overline{(g^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot g}}(x)

along with a
G
-invariant polynomial
s(x,y)\in B[y]
such that in the ring
B[y]
, we have

y^{r}+t^{s}+x=\prod_{\bar{g}\in G/G}(y-\sigma_{\bar{g}}(x))+x^{m}s(x,y).

(5.4)

Now observe that the principal open subset
V_{t}
under the base change along
B
, given by
\hat{V}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=V_{t}\times_{U_{t}}\operatorname{Spec}(B)
, is isomorphic to the closed subvariety of
\operatorname{Spec}(B[y,z_{1}])
defined by the equation

\hat{V}_{t}\cong\bigg\{x^{m}z_{1}=\prod_{\bar{g}\in G/H}(y-\sigma_{\bar{g}}(x))\bigg\}\subset\operatorname{Spec}(B[y,z_{1}])

(5.5)

where
z_{1}=z-s(x,y)
. As mentioned previously, the regular function
\lambda_{\bar{g}}-\lambda_{\bar{g}^{\prime}}
is invertible whenever
g\neq g^{\prime}
, the closed subscheme

\{x=0\}\subset\hat{V}_{t}
is the disjoint union of
r
-closed subschemes
D_{\bar{g}}\cong\operatorname{Spec}R[z_{1}]
with corresponding defining ideals
(x,y-\sigma_{\bar{g}}(x))\in\Gamma(\hat{V}_{t},\mathcal{O}_{\hat{V}_{t}})
on which
G
acts by permutation. Furthermore, the affine variety
\hat{V}_{t}
is covered by affine open subsets of the form
\hat{V}_{t,\bar{g}}
, where

\hat{V}_{t,\bar{g}}=\hat{V}_{t}\backslash\bigcup_{\bar{g}^{\prime}\in(G/H)\backslash\{\bar{g}\}}D_{\bar{g}}

for
\bar{g}\in G/H
. Now, due to the splitting of roots as in
Eq.

5.4
, we have a well-defined rational map

\hat{V}_{t,\bar{g}}\dashrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(B[u_{\bar{g}}]),\quad\big(x,y,z_{1}\big)\mapsto\left(x,\frac{y-\sigma_{\bar{g}}(x)}{x^{m}}=\frac{z}{\prod_{\bar{g}^{\prime}\in(G/H)\backslash\{\bar{g}^{\prime}\}}(y-\sigma_{\bar{g}^{\prime}}(x))}\right)

which is in fact an isomorphism of schemes over
\operatorname{Spec}B
. As a consequence, one verifies that the morphism
\hat{q}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\hat{V}_{t}=V_{t}\times_{U_{t}}\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}B
is faithfully flat and that
\hat{q}
factors through a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle
\hat{\rho}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\hat{V}_{t}\to S
over the scheme
\hat{\delta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\to\operatorname{Spec}B
. The latter scheme
S
is obtained by gluing
r
copies of
S_{\bar{g}}
, for
\bar{g}\in G/H
, of
\operatorname{Spec}B\cong C\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
by identity along the principal open subsets

\operatorname{Spec}B_{x}\cong C\times\operatorname{Spec}k[x^{\pm 1}]\subset S_{\bar{g}}.

Put differently, the morphism
\hat{\rho}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\hat{V}_{t}\to S
is a Zariski-locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle with local trivializations
\hat{V}_{t}\ |_{S_{\bar{g}}}\cong\operatorname{Spec}B[u_{\bar{g}}]
and transition functions over
S_{\bar{g}}\cap S_{\bar{g}^{\prime}}\cong\operatorname{Spec}B_{x}
of the form

u_{\bar{g}}\mapsto u_{\bar{g}^{\prime}}+x^{-m}(\sigma_{\bar{g}}(x)-\sigma_{\bar{g}^{\prime}}(x)).

The action of
G
on
\hat{V}_{t}
descends to a fixed point-free action on
S
defined locally by

S_{\bar{g}}\ni(c,x)\mapsto(g^{\prime}\cdot c,x)\in S_{\overline{(g^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot g}}.

This
G
-action produces a quotient which is a scheme only if
h_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{1}\to C_{0}
is an isomorphism; nevertheless always exists as an algebraic space in the form of an étale
G
-torsor
S\to\mathfrak{S}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=S/G
. By construction of
\mathfrak{S}_{t}
, we get the following cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{V}_{t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{t}\cong{\hat{V}_{t}}/G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{S}_{t}=S/G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\rho}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{t}}

with the horizontal maps being étale
G
-torsors. Consequently, the induced morphism
\rho_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V_{t}\to\mathfrak{S}_{t}
is an étale locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle. It remains to observe that the
G
-invariant morphism

\mathrm{pr}_{1}\circ\hat{\delta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\to\operatorname{Spec}B\cong U_{t}\times_{C_{0}}C\to U_{t}

descends to a morphism
\delta_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathfrak{S}_{t}\to U_{t}
and
\hat{\delta}
restricts to an isomorphism outside
\{x=0\}\subset U_{t}
. In contrast, over the punctured fiber, we have that the
\delta^{-1}(\{x=0\})
is isomorphic to the quotient of
C\times G/H
by the diagonal action of
G
, and hence to
C/H\cong C_{1}
. Thus, the algebraic space
\mathfrak{S}_{t}
extends to globally
\delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
.
∎

Remark 5.1.4
.

The perfectness assumption is not strict in
Lemma

5.1.3
. In fact, any base field of char
k=p\mid r
(resp.
p\mid s
) where one can extract the
s
th root of
y
(resp.
r
th root of
t
) has the desired properties furnished in
Lemma

5.1.3
.

5.1.6
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Brouwer Degree

Let
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\backslash L\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
be the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence from
Corollary

5.1.2
. Recall that our aim is to show that the morphism
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\to\mathcal{K}\backslash L
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
. To this end, it is enough to show that the composite
i\circ g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}

\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}\xrightarrow{i}\mathcal{K}\backslash L\xrightarrow{g}\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}

is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
. This follows due to the two-out-of-three property of model categories (
34
). To show that
i\circ g
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence, we use the associated
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Brouwer degree map from the motivic homotopy theory (
Corollary

2.2.3
). The morphism
g
being an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence, we can identify
\mathcal{K}\backslash L
in the Milnor-Witt
K
-theory as
H^{1}(\mathcal{K}\backslash L,K_{2}^{\operatorname{MW}})=K_{0}^{\operatorname{MW}}(k)\cdot\mu
, where
\mu=g^{*}(\xi)
is the pullback of the explicit generator. In the view of
Lemma

2.2.4
, it is enough to show that the induced map on the Milnor-Witt
K
-theory sheaves by
i

i^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H^{1}(\mathcal{K}\backslash L,K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\},K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})

is an isomorphism of groups. The diagram
Eq.

5.3
induces the following commutative diagram in Milnor-Witt
K
-theory:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(\mathcal{K}\backslash L,K_{2}^{\operatorname{MW}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(\mathcal{K}/(\mathcal{K}\backslash L),K_{2}^{\operatorname{MW}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(\mathcal{K},K_{2}^{\operatorname{MW}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(\mathcal{K}\backslash L,K_{2}^{\operatorname{MW}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\},K_{2}^{\operatorname{MW}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}((\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge 2},K_{2}^{\operatorname{MW}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(\mathbb{A}^{2},K_{2}^{\operatorname{MW}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{2}(\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\},K_{2}^{\operatorname{MW}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial^{\prime}}

(5.6)

Since
j
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence, this implies that
j^{*}
is an isomorphism. By
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy invariance of Milnor-Witt
K
-theory, we have that it vanishes for affine spaces, in particular,
H^{*}(\mathbb{A}^{2},K_{2}^{\operatorname{MW}})=0
, which, due to the exactness, renders that
\partial^{\prime}
is also an isomorphism. Observe that the groups
H^{1}(\mathcal{K}\backslash L,K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})
and
H^{2}(\mathcal{K}/(\mathcal{K}\backslash L),K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})
are actually free
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{0}
-modules of rank 1 (
[
16
, Lemma 4.5]
). This implies that
\partial
is
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{0}(k)
-linear and, in particular, an injective group homomorphism. Thus, it remains to only show that
\partial
is surjective. This is proven below in
5.1.5
by showing that every cocycle of
H^{2}(\mathcal{K}/(\mathcal{K}\backslash L),K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})
is a boundary of some cocycle of
H^{1}(\mathcal{K}\backslash L,K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})
. In other words, for every such
\beta\in H^{2}(\mathcal{K}/(\mathcal{K}\backslash L),K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})
, there exists a cocycle
\alpha\in H^{1}(\mathcal{K}\backslash L,K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})
such that
\partial(\alpha)=\beta
.

Proposition 5.1.5
.

[
141
]

For any perfect field
k
, the connecting homomorphism

\partial\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H^{1}(\mathcal{K}\backslash L,K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})\to H^{2}(\mathcal{K}/(\mathcal{K}\backslash L),K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})

is surjective.

Except for our style of presentation, the contents of the proof are essentially the same as in
[
60
, Proposition 3.3]
. We will include it here for concreteness.

Proof.

Due to
[
77
]
, we have the relation (cf.
Remark

B.0.23
)

H^{n}(\mathcal{K}/(\mathcal{K}\backslash L),K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*})\cong H_{L}^{n}(\mathcal{K},K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*})

for all
\forall\ n\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, we have that
H^{2}(\mathcal{K}/(\mathcal{K}\backslash L),K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})\cong H_{L}^{2}(\mathcal{K},K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})
. Recall from
Section

5.1.3
that the normal bundle of
L
in
\mathcal{K}
is explicitly generated by the sections
y
and
t
. Using the localization exact sequence of Chow-Witt theory (
[
76
, §10.4]
), this provides us with a long exact sequence and, as a result, also the generators for
H^{2}_{L}(\mathcal{K},K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})
explicitly as the following class of cocycle:

\beta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\langle 1\rangle\otimes\bar{t}\otimes\bar{y}\in K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{0}(\kappa(L),\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{m}_{L}/\mathfrak{}m^{2}_{L}).

(5.7)

Now, to prove our claim, it is enough to show that this cocycle is indeed a boundary of a cocycle
\alpha\in H^{1}(\mathcal{K}\backslash L,K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})
. The proof exploits several machinery developed in
[
160
, §5.1]
. Consider the integral subvarieties of
\mathcal{K}
as follows:
M\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{y=0\}\subset\mathcal{K}
and
N\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{t=0\}\subset\mathcal{K}
and
L^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{y=t=0,\ x^{m-1}z=1\}\subset\mathcal{K}
. By the construction, we have
M\cap N=L\bigsqcup L^{\prime}
. By the definition of
M
and
N
, we have that the element
\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=[y]\otimes\bar{t}\in K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{1}(\kappa(N),\mathfrak{}{m}_{N}/\mathfrak{}{m}^{2}_{N})
has a non-trivial boundary only on
L
and
L^{\prime}
with same values of the boundaries in respective residue fields, that is, the boundary of
\gamma
on
L
is given by
\langle 1\rangle\otimes\bar{t}\wedge\bar{y}\in K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{0}(\kappa(L),\wedge^{2}(\mathfrak{}{m}_{L}/\mathfrak{}{m}^{2}_{L}))
and on
L^{\prime}
given by
\langle 1\rangle\otimes\bar{t}\wedge\bar{y}\in K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{0}(\kappa(L^{\prime}),\wedge^{2}(\mathfrak{}{m}_{L^{\prime}}/\mathfrak{}{m}^{2}_{L^{\prime}}))
. Eventually, it turns out that
\gamma\in K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{1}(\kappa(N),\mathfrak{}{m}_{N}/\mathfrak{}{m}_{N}^{2})
is not a cocycle on
\mathcal{K}\backslash L
, but we can produce one by doing the following modification.

Note that the symbol
\alpha^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=[x^{m-1}z-1]\otimes\bar{y}\in K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{1}(\kappa(M),\mathfrak{}{m}_{M}/\mathfrak{}{m}^{2}_{M})
has non-trivial boundary only on
L^{\prime}
. Indeed, from the equation of
\mathcal{K}
, we have that
x(x^{m-1}z-1)=y^{r}+t^{s}
and again since
r,s
are coprime, we have that any prime containing
x^{m-1}z-1
and
y
must also contain the
t
and in turn also the prime ideal corresponding to
L^{\prime}
by the definition of
L^{\prime}
. We now compute the boundary of
\alpha^{\prime}
on
L^{\prime}
, taking advantage of the above-mentioned primality condition of the ideals. Since
x
is invertible in
L^{\prime}
, we can write
(x^{m-1}z-1)=(y^{r}+t^{s})/x=x^{-1}y^{r}+x^{-1}t^{s}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{}{m}_{L^{\prime}}}
. Hence, on
L^{\prime}
, the boundary of
\alpha^{\prime}
is the same as the boundary of
[x^{-1}t^{s}]\otimes\bar{y}
. The latter can be computed using the explicit relations given in
[
160
, Lemma 3.5]
. In particular, we have that

[x^{-1}t^{s}]=[x^{-1}]+\langle x^{-1}\rangle[t^{s}]=[x^{-1}]+\langle x\rangle[t^{s}].

Now due to
Lemma

B.0.13
, we have that
[t^{s}]=s_{\epsilon}[t]
. Finally, using the boundary formulas as given in
[
160
, Proposition 3.17]
, we obtain the boundary of
\alpha^{\prime}
as

d(\alpha^{\prime})=\langle x\rangle s_{\epsilon}\otimes\bar{y}\wedge\bar{t}=\langle-x\rangle s_{\epsilon}\otimes\bar{t}\wedge\bar{y}.

Set
S\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x^{m-1}z=1\}\subset\mathcal{K}
as a codimension 1 subvariety of
\mathcal{K}
. Since
M
and
S
are different codimension 1 subvarieties, we compute the boundary as follows:

d([y,x^{m-1}z-1])=[x^{m-1}z-1]\otimes\bar{y}+\epsilon[y]\otimes\overline{x^{m-1}z-1}

As
d^{2}=0
and
\epsilon=-\langle-1\rangle
, we have

d([y]\otimes\overline{x^{m-1}z-1})=\langle-1\rangle\ d([x^{m-1}z-1]\otimes\bar{y})=\langle x\rangle s_{\epsilon}\otimes\bar{t}\wedge\bar{y}.

Now
x
is an unit in
S
, we have that
d(\langle x\rangle[y]\otimes\overline{x^{m-1}z-1})=s_{\epsilon}\otimes\bar{t}\wedge\bar{y}
. Similarly,
d([x^{m-1}z-1]\otimes\bar{t})=\langle x\rangle r_{\epsilon}\otimes\bar{t}\wedge\bar{y}
and as a consequence, we have

d([t]\otimes\overline{x^{m-1}z-1})=\langle-1\rangle d([x^{m-1}z-1]\otimes\bar{t})=\langle-x\rangle r_{\epsilon}\otimes\bar{t}\otimes\bar{y}.

Thus,
d(\langle-x\rangle[t]\otimes x^{m-1}z-1)=r_{\epsilon}\otimes\bar{t}\wedge\bar{y}
. Now, choose
g,h\in\mathbb{N}
such that
gr-hs=1
, which is possible as
(r,s)=1
. For any integers
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
(pq)_{\epsilon}=p_{\epsilon}q_{\epsilon}
, and so we have
g_{\epsilon}r_{\epsilon}-h_{\epsilon}s_{\epsilon}=\langle\pm 1\rangle

1
1

1

it is odd if
gr
is odd and
\langle-1\rangle
otherwise
. In the end, we have obtained that
d(g_{\epsilon}\langle-x\rangle[t]\otimes\overline{x^{m-1}z-1})-h_{\epsilon}\langle x\rangle[y]\otimes(\overline{x^{m-1}z-1}))=\langle\pm 1\rangle\otimes\bar{t}\wedge\bar{y}
. This gives us the desired cocycle obtained by modifying
\alpha^{\prime}
by setting

\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=[y]\otimes\bar{t}-\langle\pm 1\rangle(g_{\epsilon}\langle-x\rangle[t]\otimes\overline{(x^{m-1}z-1)}-h_{\epsilon}\langle x\rangle[y]\otimes\overline{(x^{m-1}z-1)})

that maps to the generator of
H^{2}_{L}(X,K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{2})
under
\partial
.
∎

A note on the
lazy
proof

In the case when char
k=0
, the authors in
[
60
]
present another proof, which they call the
lazy proof
, exploiting the stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of
\mathcal{K}
(
[
102
, Theorem 4.2]
) over a field of characteristic zero. The proof strongly in the stable context relies on the deep fact that every smooth quasi-projective scheme is dualizable in the stable homotopy category
\mathrm{SH}(k)
. This is, in turn, proven by exploiting the existence of resolution of singularities in characteristic zero. Hence, assuming the existence of resolution of singularities over perfect fields, the lazy proof can very well be adapted to our situation.

Remark 5.1.6
.

[
141
]

The family of smooth affine threefolds
\mathcal{K}
produces the first example of exotic varieties in mixed characteristics. For instance, this has the consequence that the affine variety
\mathcal{K}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible threefold which is not isomorphic to the Asanuma-Gupta threefolds (
Section

3.2.4
). Thereby, all these threefold add to the list of "potential" counter-examples to the Zariski Cancellation in positive characteristics. In view of this distinction, we can propose the following action plans:

1.

Construct an invariant to distinguish between a given smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible affine threefold and Koras-Russell threefolds.

and in analogy with
Lemma

4.2.1
and
Lemma

4.3.3
:

2.

Prove or disprove the fact that every smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible threefold is necessarily affine. To disprove, it suffices to find a strictly quasi-affine smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible threefold.

5.1.7
Relative
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Contractibility of
\mathcal{K}

We can now extend the unstable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of
\mathcal{K}
over integers and over an arbitrary Noetherian scheme with perfect residue fields.

Proposition 5.1.7
.

[
141
]

For any perfect field
k
, the canonical morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\to\operatorname{Spec}k
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
.

Proof.

From
Section

5.1.3
and
Section

5.1.4
, we have that the morphisms
i
and
j
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences.
Consequently,
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{2}\to\mathcal{K}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
Eq.

5.3
and
\mathbb{A}^{2}
is clearly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over
\operatorname{Spec}k
.
∎

Theorem 5.1.8
.

[
141
]

The Koras-Russell three fold of first kind
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}
is relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. In particular,
\mathcal{K}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\mathbb{Z}}
that is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{3}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

The fibers of
f
over the generic point
0\in\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, as the quotient field
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Q}
has characteristic zero, for which the proof follows due to
[
60
]
. Now, for all closed points
q\in\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}
, we have that the corresponding residue field is
\mathbb{F}_{q}
(which in particular is perfect), the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}_{q}
now follows from
5.1.7
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}_{o}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}_{q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Q}\cong\operatorname{Spec}\kappa_{o}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}}}
