{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spec}\kappa_{q}\cong\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}_{q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{o}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{q}}}

Now,
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}
is a morphism of motivic spaces whose fibers are all
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over the corresponding residue fields. The proof now follows as a consequence of
2.1.48
.
∎

In fact, the above theorem can be generalized as follows.

Theorem 5.1.9
.

[
141
]

Let
S
be any Noetherian scheme with perfect residue fields. The Koras-Russell threefold of first kind
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\to S
is relatively
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
.

Proof.

Let us first note that the canonical map
\mathcal{K}\to S
is well-defined and smooth due to
Lemma

C.1.4
as all the fibres of
f
over either the quotient field or corresponding residue fields are smooth schemes. Now, choose a point
s\in S
with residue fields
\kappa_{s}
. Now, if
\kappa_{s}
has characteristic zero, then the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of
\mathcal{K}\to\operatorname{Spec}\kappa_{s}
follows from
[
60
]
. If char
\kappa_{s}=p>0
, it follows from
Theorem

5.1.8
that
\mathcal{K}\to\operatorname{Spec}\kappa_{s}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible since by assumption we have that
\kappa_{s}
is a perfect field. Now, we have that all the fibres of the morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}\to S
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible, and the proof follows due to
2.1.48
.
∎

A Note on the Deformed Version of
\mathcal{K}

Let us also mention a closely related family of affine threefolds called the
deformed Koras-Russell threefolds
of the first kind studied by the authors in
[
66
]
. They are defined by the equation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(m,r,s,\phi)=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{K}(\phi)=\{x^{m}z=y^{r}+t^{s}+x\phi(x,y,t)\}

where
m,r,s\geq 2
are integers with
r
and
s
are coprime and
\phi(x,y,t)\in k[x,y,t]
such that
\phi(0,0,0)\in k^{\times}
. Observe that when
\phi(x,y,t)=a\in k^{\times}
, then
\mathcal{K}(\phi)=\mathcal{K}
(
25
). And when
\phi(x,y,t)=q(x)\in k[x]
with
q(0)\in k^{\times}
, then the corresponding affine threefolds
\mathcal{K}(q)
has been shown to be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over a field of characteristic zero (
[
60
, Theorem 1.2]
). Now, for a general polynomial
\phi(x,y,t)
satisfying
\phi(0,0,0)\in k^{\times}
, it is known that the corresponding affine varieties
\mathcal{K}(\phi)
form examples of the affine modification, in the sense of Kaliman-Zaidenberg (
[
113
]
). In this direction, it has been shown that
\mathcal{K}(\phi)
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible in the stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
\mathrm{SH}^{S^{1}}(k)
(
[
66
, Theorem 4.7]
) after simplicial suspension over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. In addition, the authors in [
ibid
] also study the notion of Koras-Russell fiber bundles, whose definition we include below.

Definition 26
.

Suppose
s(x)\in k[x]
has positive degree and let
R(x,y,t)\in k[x,y,t]
. Define the closed subscheme
\mathcal{X}(s,R)
of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{x}\times\mathbb{A}^{3}=\operatorname{Spec}(k[x][y,z,t])
by the equation:

\{s(x)z=R(x,y,t)\}.

We say that the projection map
\mathrm{pr}_{x}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{X}(s,R)\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{x}
defines a Koras-Russell fiber bundle if

1.

\mathcal{X}(s,R)
is a smooth scheme, and

2.

For every zero
x_{0}
of
s(x)
, the zero locus in
\mathbb{A}^{2}=\operatorname{Spec}(k[y,t])
of the polynomial
R(x_{0},y,t)
is an integral rational plane curve with a unique place at infinity and at most unibranch singularities.

The simplicial stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of
\mathcal{X}(s,R)
has been established over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero (
[
66
, Theorem 4.13]
). In the light of
5.1.7
, we can now essentially lift these results over perfect fields as well
2
2

2

this is a work in progress
.

5.2
Generalized Koras-Russell Prototypes

In this section, we turn towards studying the higher-dimensional analog of the Koras-Russell threefolds, named as the
generalized Koras-Russell varieties
. The authors in
[
62
]
recently proved its unstable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility over a field of characteristic zero. In this spirit, we will now lift the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility from characteristic zero to arbitrary perfect fields and consequently over some Noetherian scheme. As a sequel, we shall see in
Section

5.3
that these varieties are the crucial ingredients to produce exotic motivic spheres.

Let us begin with the definition of these varieties. As for the notation,
k
will denote a perfect field and
k^{[n]}
will denote the polynomial ring in
n
-variables.

Definition 27
.

Let
r,s\geq 2
be any coprime integers. Consider the triple
(m,\underline{n},\psi)
with integers
m\geq 0
and a
(m+1)
-tuple
\underline{n}=(n_{0},n_{1},\dots,n_{m})
of multi-index with
n_{i}>1
and a polynomial
\psi\in k^{[1]}
such that
\psi(0)\neq 0
. Then consider the coordinate ring

R_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{k[x_{0},x_{1}\dots,x_{m},y,z,t]}{\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}z+y^{r}+t^{s}+x_{0}\ \psi(\underline{x})}

where
\underline{x}^{\underline{n}}=\prod_{i=0}^{m}x_{i}^{n_{i}}
and
\underline{x}=\prod_{i=0}^{m}x_{i}
. The
generalized Koras-Russell varieties
are defined as the affine variety associated with this coordinate ring

\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Spec}R_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)

(5.8)

For every
m\geq 0
, the so-obtained affine variety
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)
is of dimension
(m+3)
and is smooth by the Jacobian criterion, by the assumption that
\psi(0)\neq 0
. Let us now look at a concise example of one such variety in relative dimension
m+3
constructed over the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{n-3}_{k}
. Putting
\psi(\underline{x})=1+\underline{x}+\sum_{i=2}^{n-2}a_{i}\underline{x}^{i}
which is clearly non-vanishing at the origin (as
\psi(0)=1\neq 0
), we get that

\mathcal{X}_{m}(n,\psi)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigg\{\underline{x}^{n}z=y^{r}+t^{s}+x_{0}(1+\underline{x}+\sum_{i=2}^{n-2}a_{i}\underline{x}^{i})\bigg\}\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{n-3}_{k}\times\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}

(5.9)

with coordinates given by
\operatorname{Spec}(k[a_{2},\dots,a_{n-2}][x_{0},\dots,x_{m},y,z,t])
. In particular,
\mathcal{X}_{m}(n,\psi)
is a smooth affine scheme under the projection map
\mathcal{X}_{m}(n,\psi)\to\mathbb{A}^{n-3}_{k}
given by the projection onto the
a_{i}
’s.

Theorem 5.2.1
.

[
141
]

Let
k
be any perfect field. Then under the notations and assumptions as in
27
, the canonical morphism
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)\to\operatorname{Spec}k
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
.

Proof.

The authors in
[
62
]
have proven the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of these varieties over fields of characteristic zero. We can now promote this result over perfect fields in the light of
5.1.7
following the original proof. Let us first note that all these varieties are all stably
k
-isomorphic (
[
62
, Proposition 3]
), that is,
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\cong\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},1)\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
, for any polynomial
\psi\in k^{[1]}
as above. Hence, in the light of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy invariance (
7
), it is enough to show that
\mathcal{X}_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},1)
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible. Also, note that when
m=0
and
\psi(\underline{x})=1
,
\mathcal{X}_{0}(n_{0},1)
is precisely the family of Koras-Russell threefolds studied in the previous section, which we proved to be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible (
5.1.7
).

Fix
A_{m-1}=k[x_{1},\dots,x_{m-1},y,t]
,
R_{m}=A_{m-1}[x_{0},x_{m},z]/\big(\prod_{i=0}^{m}x_{i}^{n_{i}}z+y^{r}+t^{s}+x_{0}\big)
. Then let us consider the following closed subschemes in
\mathcal{X}_{m}

\displaystyle W_{m}=\{x_{m}=0\}\cong\operatorname{Spec}(R_{m}/x_{m}R_{m})\cong\operatorname{Spec}(A_{m-1}[z])\cong\mathbb{A}^{m+2}_{k}

(5.10)

\displaystyle H_{m}=\{z=0\}\cong\operatorname{Spec}(R_{m}/zR_{m})\cong\operatorname{Spec}(A_{m-1}[x_{m}])\cong\mathbb{A}^{m+2}_{k}

(5.11)

\displaystyle P_{m}=W_{m}\cap H_{m}\cong\operatorname{Spec}(R_{m}/(x_{m},z)R_{m})\cong\operatorname{Spec}(A_{m-1})\cong\mathbb{A}_{k}^{m+1}

(5.12)

Now consider the following canonical closed immersions
j_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H_{m}\to\mathcal{X}_{m}
and
i_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H_{m}\backslash P_{m}\to\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash W_{m}
. By setting
Z\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=x_{m}z
, we have an isomorphism

\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash W_{m}\cong\operatorname{Spec}(A_{m-2}[x_{0},x_{m-1},Z][x^{-1}_{m}]/(x_{0}^{n_{0}}\prod_{i\neq m}x_{i}^{n_{i}}Z+y^{r}+t^{s}+x_{0}))\cong\mathcal{X}_{m-1}\times\mathbb{G}_{m,k}

for which
i_{m}
equals the product

j_{m-1}\times id_{\mathbb{G}_{m.k}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H_{m}\backslash P_{m}\cong H_{m-1}\times_{k}\mathbb{G}_{m.k}=\operatorname{Spec}(A_{m-2}[x_{m-1}][x_{m}^{\pm 1}])\to\mathcal{X}_{m-1}\times_{k}\mathbb{G}_{m.k}

Now, by setting up the cofiber sequence as in
Eq.

5.3
, we get the following commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{m}\backslash P_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{m}/(H_{m}\backslash P_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash W_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}_{m}/(\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash W_{m})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{m}}

(5.13)

associated to the open immersions
H_{m}\backslash P_{m}\to H_{m}
and
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash W_{m}\to W_{m}
. Observe that
H_{m}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible by
Eq.

5.11
and so
\mathcal{X}_{m}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible provided
j_{m}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence. For
m=0
, the morphism
j_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H_{0}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence due to
5.1.7
. We now establish this property for
m\geq 1
by induction on
m
.

To show that
j_{m}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence, it is enough to show that
i_{m}
and
l_{m}
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
by the
Lemma

A.2.26
. The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence of
i_{m}
follows from the fact that
i_{m}\simeq j_{m-1}\times id_{\mathbb{G}_{m,k}}
and by the induction hypothesis,
j_{m-1}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence. On the other hand,
l_{m}
is also an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence. Indeed, as
P_{m}
is obtained as the transversal intersection of two smooth subvarieties inside
\mathcal{X}_{m}
, the normal cone
N_{P_{m}}H_{m}
of
P_{m}
in
H_{m}
is in fact the restriction of the normal cone
N_{W_{m}}\mathcal{X}_{m}
of
W_{m}
in
\mathcal{X}_{m}
to
P_{m}
. But the normal cone of
N_{W_{m}}X_{m}
is trivial, attributed to the analogous reason as in
Section

5.1.3
, whence the triviality of
N_{P_{m}}H_{m}
. The proof now follows from the homotopy purity
Theorem

2.1.56
as all these varieties are smooth. In particular,

H_{m}/(H_{m}\backslash P_{m})\simeq\operatorname{Th}(N_{P_{m}}H_{m})\simeq P_{m}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}

and similarly,

\mathcal{X}_{m}/(\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash W_{m})\simeq\operatorname{Th}(N_{W_{m}}\mathcal{X}_{m})\simeq W_{m}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}.

The map
l_{m}
coincides with the following map observed under the isomorphisms mentioned above

\operatorname{Th}(N_{P_{m}}H_{m})\simeq P_{m}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\xrightarrow{\iota\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}}W_{m}\wedge\mathbb{P}^{1}\simeq\operatorname{Th}(N_{W_{m}}\mathcal{X}_{m}).

obtained as the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-suspension of the closed immersion
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}P_{m}\hookrightarrow W_{m}
. Hence, the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence of
\iota
implies that of
l_{m}
.
∎

Corollary 5.2.2
.

[
141
]

Let
S
be any Noetherian scheme with perfect residue fields. Then under the assumptions as in
Eq.

5.8
, the canonical morphism
\varphi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
.

Proof.

The strategy of proof is precisely the same as that of
Theorem

5.1.9
. Choose any point
s\in S
and consider the induced morphism on the corresponding fiber
\varphi_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)\times_{\kappa_{s}}\operatorname{Spec}\kappa_{s}=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}(\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi))_{s}\to\operatorname{Spec}\kappa_{s}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spec}\kappa_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

Since by the hypothesis such an
\kappa_{s}
is perfect,
\varphi^{-1}(s)
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\mathcal{H}(\kappa_{s})
owing to
Theorem

5.2.1
. The proof now follows from
2.1.48
.
∎

Remark 5.2.3
.

The authors in
[
62
]
remark that
\mathcal{X}_{m}
are "potential" counterexamples to the Zariski Cancellation over fields of characteristic zero in all dimensions
\geq 3
. Conforming to their fact, our extension over perfect fields
Theorem

5.2.1
opens up a path to view these varieties
\mathcal{X}_{m}
as "potential" counterexamples to the Zariski cancellations even over fields of positive characteristics. Moreover, none of these varieties can be isomorphic to Asanuma-Gupta varieties (the latter have smooth fibers while the generic fiber of
\mathcal{X}_{m}
is singular), making it a familiar, yet novel candidate in disguise.

Moduli of Arbitrary Smooth Exotic Affine Varieties

Recall from
Section

4.4
that the authors in
[
11
, Theorem 5.1]
constructed an infinite collection of pairwise non-isomorphic exotic
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible strictly quasi-affine varieties of relative dimensions
\geq 4
. The picture in dimension 1 (
Theorem

4.2.2
) and dimension 2 (
Theorem

4.3.1
) being filled, the missing gaps were

1.

Does there exist an analogous picture for threefolds?

This gap was answered by the authors in
[
60
]
, exploiting the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility of Koras-Russell threefolds. In particular, they
[
60
, Corollary 1.3]
devised a general method to produce a moduli of arbitrary positive dimension of pairwise non-isomorphic, stably isomorphic,
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible smooth affine threefolds using techniques from
[
65
]
that were initially established over
\mathbb{C}
.

2.

If such families consistently arose out of only strictly quasi-affine schemes in dimension
\geq 4
, is there a (purely) affine family with such a property in dimensions
\geq 4
?

The generalized Koras-Russell varieties
\mathcal{X}_{m}(n,\psi)
answer the question above.

As a favorable addition, our results (
Theorem

5.2.1
) and
Corollary

5.2.2
) garnish this overall picture by producing a moduli of arbitrary positive dimension of pairwise non-isomorphic, stably isomorphic,
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible smooth affine schemes over a generalized base scheme in relative dimensions
\geq 3
.

5.3
Exotic Motivic Spheres

In this section, we introduce the
motivic spheres
, one of the most important objects of study in the motivic homotopy theory, and study the existence of exotic motivic spheres, a study that can be seen in contrast with that of exotic affine varieties from
Chapter

4
. To establish this, we first prove that the affine variety
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{\bullet\}
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected (
Lemma

5.3.7
) and the quasi-affine varieties
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{\bullet\}
provides a model for an exotic motivic spheres in all higher dimensions
\geq 4
(
Theorem

5.3.8
).

Recall from
Section

2.1.1
, the category of pointed
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}
is symmetric monoidal with respect to the smash product. This can be exploited to obtain many more objects in
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}
.

Proposition 5.3.1
.

For
X\in\operatorname{Spc}_{S\bullet}
, we have a canonical
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence

\Sigma X\simeq S^{1}\wedge X

Proof.

Observe that the category of presheaves
\operatorname{Psh}(\mathrm{Sm}_{S})
is cocomplete and
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
preserves coproducts. So, it suffices to compute the pushout at the level of simplicial presheaves, where the coproduct is computed levelwise. In the category of simplicial presheaves, it is clear that
\Sigma X(U)=S^{1}\wedge X(U)
, for any
U\in\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
. Hence, as pointed presheaves, we have that
\Sigma X=S^{1}\wedge X
. Now to see this in fact holds in
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}
, note that
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
preserves the smash product operation as it preserves finite products, as well as coproducts and cofibers (
2.1.15
).
∎

5.3.1
Spheres in Motivic Homotopy Theory

An important application of the smash product structure is to obtain sphere objects in
\mathcal{H}(S)_{\bullet}
. In motivic homotopy theory, we have two types of spheres:

1.

The
simplicial circle

S^{1}
comes from topology, and as a simplex it can be seen as
S^{1}=\Delta^{1}/\partial\Delta
pointed by the image
\partial\Delta\hookrightarrow\Delta
. In analogy with topology, if
S^{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{0\}\sqcup\{1\}
, then
S^{1}
is the pushout of the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{1}}}

In stark contrast with algebraic topology,
S^{1}
exists as a nodal scheme. In particular,
S^{1}
is not smooth as an algebraic sphere! The algebraic model is given by
S^{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{A}^{1}/\{0\sim 1\}
.

2.

The
Tate circle

\mathbb{G}_{m,k}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{G}_{m}=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
pointed by 1 is an algebro-geometric sphere. It exists as a smooth scheme. Over a base scheme
S
, we define
\mathbb{G}_{m,S}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}}S
.

One forms a bi-graded (mixed) motivic sphere by smashing copies of both of these spheres (cf.
Section

2.1.1
). There are several varying notations for these motivic spheres. We will adopt the following convention that aligns with the grading of motivic cohomology (which is also widely in use):

\mathbb{S}^{p,q}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(S^{1})^{\wedge(p-q)}\wedge\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge q}

for all
p\geq q\geq 0
. For notational convenience, we will adapt to write
S^{p-q}
for
(S^{1})^{\wedge(p-q)}
and
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{q}
for
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge q}
.

Example 5.3.2
.

The following are some typical examples of motivic spheres.

1.

By definition, we have that
\mathbb{S}^{1,0}=S^{1}
and
\mathbb{S}^{1,1}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
. More generally, for every
n\geq 1
, we have
\mathbb{S}^{2n-1,n}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
(
[
6
, Proposition 4.40]
),

2.

For every
n\geq 1
, we have a pushout diagram (
[
6
, Corollary 4.41]
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{n}/\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}=\mathbb{S}^{2n,n}}}

which gives us the identifications

\mathbb{S}^{2n,n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Sigma_{S^{1}}\mathbb{S}^{2n-1,n}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{n}/({\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}})\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n}/(\mathbb{P}^{n}\backslash\{0\})\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n}/\mathbb{P}^{n-1}.

In particular, for
n=1
, we get

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{G}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}^{1}}}

and so
\mathbb{S}^{2,1}\simeq\Sigma_{s}\mathbb{G}_{m}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
,

3.

The smooth affine quadrics
Q_{2n}
’s discussed in
Section

4.4.1
are motivic spheres. We have that

\displaystyle Q_{2n-1}\simeq\mathbb{S}^{2n-1,n}\quad\text{and}\quad Q_{2n}\simeq\mathbb{S}^{2n,n}\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge n}.

In combination with the (2) above, we obtain:

\Sigma_{S^{1}}\mathbb{A}^{n}\backslash\{0\}\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge n}

for all
n\geq 1
.

4.

Whenever
p>2q
, it is known that the motivic spheres
\mathbb{S}^{p,q}
cannot be represented by a smooth scheme
[
10
, Proposition 2.3.1]
.

5.

The special linear group
SL_{2}
is a motivic sphere
\mathbb{S}^{3,2}
of dimension 3. Indeed, the projection
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}SL_{2}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
is a Zariski locally trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-bundle, whence an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence,

Following the study of exotic affine varieties in
Section

5.1
, we are now interested in the compact
3
3

3

though the terminology might be misleading as a motivic sphere need not always be proper: e.g.,
\mathbb{G}_{m}
analog.

Definition 28
.

A (smooth) scheme
X\to S
of finite type and of dimension
n
will be called an
exotic motivic sphere
if it is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to the smooth scheme
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\backslash\{0\}
without being isomorphic as
S
-schemes, i.e.,

X\simeq\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\backslash\{0\}\quad\text{and}\quad X\ncong\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\backslash\{0\}.

Remark 5.3.3
.

In differential geometry, one talks about two kinds of spheres: the topological sphere
\mathbb{S}^{m}
, which inherits the canonical subspace topology from
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
, and the smooth sphere
\mathbb{S}^{m}
with the standard differential structure. The distinction became evident as Milnor
[
150
]
discovered that one can equip
\mathbb{S}^{7}
with more than one smooth structure (in fact, 28 of them!), leading to the discovery of
smooth exotic spheres
in differential geometry. He further proved that such a phenomenon consistently occurred in higher dimensions as well. Though our study of
exotic motivic spheres
is purely algebraic, it would be worthwhile to explore whether there are plausible, intricate connections when one equips a smooth structure on these varieties.

A prompt question to ponder is the existence of such exotic schemes over fields and subsequently over a base scheme. In what follows, we shall first give an overview of this problem and establish a counterexample for the following question:

Question 5.3.4
.

Let
S
be a reasonably arbitrary base scheme. Then for a smooth scheme
X\to S
of dimension
n
, does
X\simeq_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\backslash\{0\}
imply the isomorphism
X\cong\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\backslash\{0\}
of
S
-schemes?

5.3.2
Low Dimensional Motivic Spheres

In dimension 0,
\mathbb{S}^{1,0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=S^{0}\cong\{0\}\cup\{1\}
is the unique (singular) motivic sphere. In dimension 1,
\mathbb{S}^{0,1}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
is the unique smooth motivic sphere up to isomorphism. This follows as a consequence of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigidity of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
as described below.

Proposition 5.3.5
.

[
141
]

Over a reduced scheme
S
, if a smooth scheme
X\to S
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{G}_{m,S}
, then it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{m,S}
.

Proof.

We shall prove this over fields
4
4

4

The author thanks Biman Roy for his generous explanation of the proof over fields.
with a note that the same scheme of proof essentially would work over any reduced scheme due to
Example

2.1.26
. Let
X
be a smooth curve that is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weakly equivalent to
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Then due to
Example

2.1.26
, we have that
\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)\simeq\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathbb{G}_{m})\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Now we want to show that
X
is also
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid. Suppose the contrary, then recall that we have a canonical surjection of Nisnevich sheaves
X\to\pi_{0}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)
(
Theorem

2.1.21
) which gives us a dominant morphism
\varphi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Since by supposition
X
is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid, then there is a finite separable extension
L/k
and a
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy
H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{L}\to X
such that the induced maps on the 0-section and 1-section of
\operatorname{Spec}L\to X
do not agree
H(0)\neq H(1)
(see
Lemma

2.1.25
). Hence,
H
is a dominant map which makes the composition
\varphi\circ H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{L}\to X\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
constant (since
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-rigid). In particular,
\varphi
is constant, which contradicts the fact that
\varphi
is dominant. So,
X\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
∎

In dimension 2 over fields of characteristic zero, it has been recently proven (
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
) that if an open subscheme
U
of an affine surface
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weakly equivalent to
\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
, then
U\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
as
k
-schemes. In view of the extension of the characterization of
\mathbb{A}^{2}
over perfect fields (
Theorem

4.3.4
), it is likely that this result can be lifted to perfect fields as well. In dimension 3, the family of Koras-Russell threefolds of the first kind
\mathcal{K}
provides the necessary obstruction to this question (cf.
[
44
, §3]
). However, the question remains widely open in dimensions
\geq 4
over fields. We close this question in
[
141
]
in all higher dimensions by providing an obstruction using the results from
Section

5.1
, which we shall now discuss.

5.3.3
Existence of Exotic Spheres in Higher Dimensions

Any member of the family of
generalized Koras-Russell varieties
provides us a way to create a counter-example to the
5.3.4
in every dimension
\geq 3
. However, accounting for the fact that all of these varieties
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\underline{n},\psi)
are stably isomorphic, it is enough to produce a counter-example using
\mathcal{X}_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{X}_{m}(n,1)
. The proof that we present below is inspired by the case of threefolds (
[
44
, §3]
).

For simplicity, let us consider the family of smooth affine varieties defined as in
Eq.

5.9
:

\mathcal{X}_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\underline{x}^{n}z=y^{r}+t^{s}+x_{0}\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{m+4}_{k}\cong\operatorname{Spec}(k[x_{0},\dots,x_{m},y,z,t]).

(5.14)

The canonical morphism
\mathcal{X}_{m}\to\operatorname{Spec}k
makes it into a smooth affine scheme of dimension
m+3
for all
m\geq 0
and
n\geq 2
and
\underline{x}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\prod_{i=0}^{m}x_{i}
. This variety is obtained by taking
\psi(\underline{x})=1
and a single index
\underline{n}=n
in the original equation
Eq.

5.8
. Observe that
p=(1,\dots,1,0,1,0)\in\mathcal{X}_{m}
is a rational point. Define the map

\displaystyle\hskip 59.75078pt\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{X}_{m}\to\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}

\displaystyle(x_{0},\dots,x_{m},y,z,t)\mapsto(x_{0},\dots,x_{m},y,t)

(forgetting ’
z
’). Then denote the image
\phi(p)=(1,\dots,1,0,0)=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}q\in\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}
.

Lemma 5.3.6
.

[
141
]

Let
k
be a field. For all
m\geq 0
, the induced map
d\phi_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T_{p}\mathcal{X}_{m}\to T_{q}\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}
is an isomorphism of tangent spaces. As a consequence, we have that the normal bundle of
\mathcal{X}_{m}
at the point
p
is trivial.

Proof.

Set the equation
f(x_{0},\dots,x_{m},y,z,t)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\underline{x}^{n}z=y^{r}+t^{s}+x_{0}\}
. We compute the partial derivatives of
f

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{0}}=nx_{0}^{n-1}x_{1}^{n}\dots x_{m}^{n}z-1

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{j}}=nx_{0}^{n}\dots x_{j-1}^{n}x_{j}^{n-1}x_{j+1}^{n}\dots x_{m}^{n}z,\quad\text{for all}\ 0<j\leq m,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}=ry^{r-1}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial z}=\underline{x}^{n}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}=st^{s-1}

The total derivative
\nabla(f)
of
f
can be expressed as

\nabla f=\bigg((nx_{0}^{n-1}x_{1}^{n}\dots x_{m}^{n}z-1),\dots,(nx_{0}^{n}x_{1}^{n}\dots x_{j}^{n-1},\dots,x_{m}^{n}z),(ry^{r-1}),(\underline{x}^{n}),(st^{s-1})\bigg)

which at the point
p
is given by
\nabla f|_{p}=(n-1,n,\dots,n,0,1,0)
. Recall that the tangent space of
\mathcal{X}_{m}
at
p
is the kernel of the total derivative at
p
. And hence, depending on the parity of
m
, the tangent space at
p
can be given as follows: If
m
is even, we have

T_{p}\mathcal{X}_{m}=\{(a,b,-b,\dots,b,-b,c,-a(n-1),d)\mid a,b,c,d\in k\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{m+4}_{k}

If
m
is odd, we have

T_{p}\mathcal{X}_{m}=\{(a,b,-b,\dots,0,c,-a(n-1),d)\mid a,b,c,d\in k\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{m+4}_{k}

In the case when
m
is odd (respectively even), the induced map
d\phi_{p}
is given by the

(a,b,-b,\dots,b,-b,c,-a(n-1),d)\mapsto(a,b,-b,\dots,b,-b,c,d)

and (respectively)

(a,b,-b,\dots,0,c,-a(n-1),d)\mapsto(a,b,-b,\dots,0,c,d)

which are clearly isomorphic. This, in particular, implies that the normal bundle of
\mathcal{X}_{m}
at
p
is isomorphic to that of
N_{q}\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}
and hence trivial.
∎

We next show that for the chosen rational point
p\in\mathcal{X}_{m}
, the open subscheme
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected. In fact to prove our main result (
Theorem

5.3.8
), we will only need that
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected. In general, such a question that for a smooth
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected scheme
X
if a certain open subscheme
U\subset X
such that
\text{codim}X\backslash U\geq 2
is again
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected, is open in general (
[
13
, Conjecture 2.18]
). However, this has been proven to be true for coordinate subspaces, i.e., when
X=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
,
k
is any infinite field (
[
13
, Lemma 2.15]
).

Lemma 5.3.7
.

[
141
]

Let
k
be any infinite perfect field. Then the strictly quasi-affine variety
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected.

Proof.

The strategy of the proof takes into account the fact that the classical Koras-Russell threefolds
\mathcal{K}
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected (
[
66
, Example 2.28]
). Let
L/k
be any finitely generated field extension. Recall from
15
that we need to show that all the fibers and any points between fibers can be connected by elementary
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopies. Consider the projection

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\mathrm{pr}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{X}_{m}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\quad\text{given by}

\displaystyle(x_{0},\dots,x_{m},y,z,t)\mapsto x_{0}.

First, let us observe that the fibers over points of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected. Indeed, choose a point
\alpha\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
. If
\alpha\neq 1
, then
\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(\alpha)\simeq\mathbb{A}_{k}^{m+2}
which is clearly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected and if
\alpha=1
, we have
\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(\alpha)\simeq\mathbb{A}^{m+2}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
which is again
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected. In all, for every point
\alpha\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
, any two points
L
-points in
\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(\alpha)
can be connected via chains of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{L}
’s. The fiber over the point
\{x_{0}=0\}
is given by
\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(0)\simeq\mathbb{A}_{k}^{m+1}\times_{k}\Gamma_{r,s}
, where
\Gamma_{r,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{y^{r}+t^{s}=0\}
is the cuspidal curve. The cuspidal curve is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected as witnessed by
Example

2.1.18
(2). Hence, the fiber
\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(0)
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected given by the naïve
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}^{1}_{L}\to\mathbb{A}^{m+1}\times\Gamma_{r,s}

\displaystyle\gamma\mapsto(a_{0}\gamma,\dots,a_{m}\gamma,b\gamma^{s},c\gamma^{r})

joining
(0,\dots,0)
with the point
(a_{0},\dots,a_{m},b,c)
. Now, to conclude, we only have to show that points between different fibers can be joined via chains of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{L}
’s. For this, we can cook up an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy as follows: let
f(w)
and
g(w)
be two polynomials in
k[w]
such that
w^{n}
divides
f(w)^{r}+g(w)^{s}+w
. The existence of such polynomials can be demonstrated by lifting the polynomials
\text{mod}\ w^{n}
by inducting on
n\geq 0
. The case
n=0
is trivial and for the case
n=1
, choose
f(\alpha w)=1
and
g(\alpha w)=-1
. For the case
n=2
, choose
f(\alpha w)=1+w
and
g(\alpha w)=w-1
. By proceeding in the same fashion, we can obtain a general strategy by setting:

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptf(\alpha w)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1+a_{0}w+a_{1}w^{2}+\dots+a_{n-2}w^{n-1}

and

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptg(\alpha w)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1-w-\dots-w^{n-1}

for some coefficients
a_{i}\in k
. One then has to choose
\{a_{i}\}
s such that the coefficients of
w_{i}
vanish for all
i\leq n-1
. Now, define a map
\theta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{L}\to\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
by

w\mapsto\left(\alpha w,1,\dots,1,\frac{f(\alpha w)^{r}+g(\alpha w)^{s}+\alpha w-1}{(\alpha w)^{n}},f(\alpha w),g(\alpha w)\right).

This connects points from the fibre
\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(0)
with points of the fiber
\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(\alpha)
, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Crucially, note that our chosen point
p=(1,\dots,1,0,1,0)
does not lie in the image of
\theta
. Suppose on contrary, if
p\in\text{Im}(\theta)
, then we have that
\theta(a)=p=(1,\dots,1,0,1,0)
, for some
a\in\mathbb{A}^{1}_{L}
. This gives us
\alpha w=1
,
f(\alpha w)=1
, and
g(\alpha w)=0
. Upon directly substituting these values and comparing with
p=(1,\dots,1,0,1,0)
, we see that

\frac{f(\alpha w)^{r}+g(\alpha w)^{s}+\alpha w}{(\alpha w)^{n}}=1\neq 0.

This show that the quasi-affine variety
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected.
∎

We now have all the tools to prove our key result that answers
5.3.4
.

Theorem 5.3.8
.

[
141
]

Let
k
be an infinite perfect field. Then for every
m\geq 0
, the strictly quasi-affine variety
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
of dimension
m+3
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
but is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}\backslash\{q\}
as a
k
-scheme.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{m}
be the smooth affine variety as described in
Eq.

5.14
with the rational point
p=(1,\dots,1,0,1,0)\in\mathcal{X}_{m}
and with its image
q=(1,\dots,1,0,0)\in\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}
. We first show that
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{A}^{m+3}\backslash\{q\}
. For this, let us set up the following commutative diagram whose rows are cofiber sequences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}_{m}/(\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}\backslash\{q\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}/(\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}\backslash\{q\})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

(5.15)

Due to
Theorem

5.2.1
, we have that
\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{X}_{m}\to\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
\operatorname{Spc}_{k}
. Next, we observe that
\rho
is also an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence. Indeed, since the normal bundle associated to the closed immersion
\{q\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}
is trivial, by the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy purity (
Theorem

2.1.56
), we have that

\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}/(\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}\backslash\{q\})\simeq\operatorname{Spec}k\ \wedge(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge({m+3})}\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge({m+3})}.

Since
\{p\}\in\mathcal{X}_{m}
and
\mathcal{X}_{m}
are smooth subvarieties, due to the aforementioned purity, we have

\mathcal{X}_{m}/(\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\})\simeq\operatorname{Th}(N_{p}\mathcal{X}_{m}).

By
Lemma

5.3.6
, we have that the normal bundle
N_{p}\mathcal{X}_{m}
is isomorphic to a trivial bundle and hence that

\operatorname{Th}(N_{p}\mathcal{X}_{m})\simeq\{p\}\wedge(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge{(m+3)}}\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge{(m+3)}}.

Hence, we have that

(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge{(m+3)}}\simeq\mathcal{X}_{m}/(\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\})\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathbb{A}_{k}^{m+3}/(\mathbb{A}_{k}^{m+3}\backslash\{q\})\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\wedge({m+3})}

which due to the fact that the map
\rho
is induced from the map
d\phi_{p}
becomes an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in the light of
Lemma

2.1.57
. The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence of
\rho
implies, by definition, that the simplicial suspension

\Sigma_{S^{1}}\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Sigma_{S^{1}}\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}\to\Sigma_{S^{1}}\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}\backslash\{q\}

is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence of motivic spaces. Now observe that
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected from
Lemma

5.3.7
and whence by
[
18
, Proposition 2.2.7]
is thereby
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected as well. Moreover, it is of codimension
d=m+3
in
\mathcal{X}_{m}
, for
m\geq 0
. Thus, due to
[
13
, Theorem 4.1]
, this implies that the open subschemes
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{m}
have the same
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy sheaves in a range:

\pi_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\})\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{X}_{m})\quad\text{for all}\quad 0\leq i\leq d-2

and in particular,
\pi^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\})
is trivial (since
\mathcal{X}_{m}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible - see
Example

2.1.54
), which by the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Hurewicz theorem
[
160
, Theorem 6.35, Theorem 6.37]
imply that
\phi
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homology equivalence. To conclude, observe that due to
[
185
, Theorem 1.1]
any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homology equivalence is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence and that
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}\simeq\mathbb{A}_{k}^{m+3}\backslash\{q\}
.

Now, we show that these quasi-affine varieties cannot be isomorphic as schemes over
k
. Suppose, on the contrary, that there is an isomorphism

\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}\to\mathbb{A}_{k}^{m+3}\backslash\{q\}

with an inverse

\tau\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}_{k}^{m+3}\backslash\{q\}\to\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}.

Now since the point
\{p\}
(respectively,
\{q\}
) is of codimesion at least 2 in
\mathcal{X}_{m}
(respectively, in
\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}
), the morphism
\sigma
and
\tau
can be continuously extended to a smooth morphism
{\sigma^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{X}_{m}\to\mathbb{A}_{k}^{m+3}
and
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}\to\mathcal{X}_{m}
. Both the maps
\sigma^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
take the same values with the identity maps away from the complement of a rational point, which implies that both
\sigma^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
are isomorphisms. But due to the fact that
\operatorname{ML}(\mathcal{X}_{m})\ncong\operatorname{ML}(\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k})
(cf.
[
83
, Proposition 3.4]
), this is absurd. Thus, the quasi-affine varieties
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
and
\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{k}\backslash\{q\}
cannot be isomorphic as
k
-schemes.
∎

Remark 5.3.9

(Update!)
.

The assumption on the base field to be infinite in
Theorem

5.3.8
is due to the limitation as expressed in
[
13
, Remark 2.16]
. In essence, the limitation is as follows: for a smooth scheme
X
with an open immersion
U\hookrightarrow X
and its closed complement
Z\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\backslash U
, if
X(k)\subseteq Z
, then clearly
U
has no rational point, whence it cannot be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connected. However, in the light of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-simply connectedness of
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
, the infinite assumption in
Theorem

5.3.8
can be lifted due to
[
185
, Corollary 3.6]
.

5.3.4
Base Change of Motivic Spheres

In this short section, we will comment on the base change properties of motivic spheres. In anticipation of the existence of exotic motivic spheres over fields (
Theorem

5.3.8
), it is natural to systematically ask for the verity over a base scheme
S
. The question of interest can be framed as follows:

Question 5.3.10
.

Let
S
be any "reasonable" base scheme. Then for every
m\geq 0
, is
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}

\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{S}\backslash\{0\}
and if so, are they isomorphic as
S
-schemes?

We anticipate that they should not be isomorphic as
S
-schemes, intuitively, as a result of the failure of relative Makar-Limanov invariants as in the case of fields. To show that they are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic, we propose to study the following approaches:

Gluing motivic spheres

One may choose to work fiberwise as in the instance of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility. For this, let us fix the base scheme
S
to be a Noetherian scheme with infinite perfect residue fields. Let us now decipher the obstruction to taking up this approach. We know that
\mathcal{X}_{m}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible over
S
due to
Corollary

5.2.2
, hence we have that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{X}_{m}\to S
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence of motivic
S
-spaces. For every point
s\in S
, let us consider the base change
f_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}(\mathcal{X}_{m})_{s}\to\operatorname{Spec}(\kappa_{s})
, where
\kappa_{s}
is the corresponding residue field at
s\in S
, which by hypothesis is (infinite) perfect. Thus, by
Theorem

5.3.8
, we have that for every
s\in S
, the quasi-affine schemes
(\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\})_{s}
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{\kappa_{s}}\backslash\{q\}
in
\operatorname{Spc}_{\kappa_{s}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}_{\kappa_{s}}^{m+3}\backslash\{0\}\simeq(\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\})_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spec}\kappa_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

But since, in general, the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy classes of motivic spheres are not trivial (see
Corollary

2.2.3
). And so, knowing that all the fibers
(\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\})_{s}
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{\kappa_{s}}\backslash\{q\}
does not allow us conclude that the original space
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{A}^{m+3}_{S}\backslash\{q\}
. This can be explained by the lack of an analogous
gluing lemma
for motivic spheres as we had for contractible objects (
2.1.48
). Anticipating such a gluing lemma for motivic spheres, it is straightforward to verify that there are exotic motivic spheres in relative dimensions
\geq 3
over a such base scheme
S
.

The relative approach

Another approach to show that the varieties in
5.3.10
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic is to directly work over a base scheme
S
and establish similar results following the footpaths of
Theorem

5.3.8
. To begin with, the purity isomorphism (
Theorem

2.1.56
) is available over any Noetherian scheme
S
. We believe that by suitably choosing base scheme
S
, one should retrieve back that
\mathcal{X}_{m}\backslash\{p\}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain connected over
S
by cooking up an explicit
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-chain homotopy with the coefficients
\{a_{i}\}\in S
, as we did in
Lemma

5.3.7
. It only remains to check to what extent we can extend
S
to retrieve the following facts:

•

"relative"
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-excision style result over
S
(cf.
[
13
, Theorem 4.1]
),

•

\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homology is the same as that of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy in a range (cf.
[
185
, Theorem 1.1]
).

Uniqueness of motivic spheres in relative dimension 2

In anticipation of the analogous gluing lemma, one could attempt to establish that there are no exotic motivic spheres in relative dimensions 2.

Conjecture 1
.

Let
S
be any Noetherian scheme with characteristic zero residue fields. Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be a smooth scheme of relative dimension 2. Then if
X
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}\backslash\{0\}
, then
X
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}\backslash\{0\}
as a
S
-scheme.

The proposed strategy is as follows: first, due
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
, we have that over a field of characteristic zero, a smooth scheme
X\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
implies that
X\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
. Now, if
S
is any Noetherian scheme with characteristic zero residue fields, then by the "anticipated" gluing lemma, we have that
X\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
implies that
X\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\backslash\{0\}
as
S
-schemes due to
Theorem

4.3.1
.

Yay, you’ve reached the beginning of the end!

Appendix A
All About Categories!

A.1
Grothendieck Topologies

In this section, we will develop the notion of a Grothendieck topology. We will also illustrate the usefulness of the Nisnevich topology in comparison with the Zariski and the étale topologies. We shall be brief in our definitions and formulations, and we redirect readers to
[
140
]
or
[
200
]
for more pedantic details.

A.1.1
Sheaves on topological spaces

For the readers’ convenience, we shall recollect some basic notions of presheaves and sheaves from the topological setting. Let
X
be a topological space and denote by
\mathcal{O}_{X}
its collection of open subsets.

Definition 29
.

A
presheaf

F
on
X
is a contravariant functor

F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{op}\to\operatorname{Set}

which assigns for every open subset
U\subset X
, a set
F(U)
. A
sheaf

\mathcal{F}
on
X
is a presheaf satisfying the following axiom: for every open covering
U=\bigcup_{i}U_{i}
of an open subset
U
of
X
, we have an equalizer diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i,j}\mathcal{F}(U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

where for
t\in\mathcal{F}(U)
,
e(t)=\{t{|_{U_{i}}}\mid i\in I\}
and for a family
t_{i}\in\mathcal{F}(U_{i})
, and set
U_{ij}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}

p\{t_{i}\}=\{t_{i}|_{U_{ij}}\},\quad q\{t_{j}\}=\{t_{j}|_{U_{ij}}\}.

A morphism
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
of (pre-)sheaves is a natural transformation of functors.

The above definition of a sheaf is equivalent to that of a presheaf satisfying the sheaf axiom (
[
190
,
Section 006S
]
). Of course, one can (and will) also talk about (pre)-sheaves of groups, rings, Abelian groups, modules, and so on by replacing the category of sets by the corresponding category in the above definition. All of these variants help one to talk about a notion of (pre-)sheaf in the concerned category.

Example A.1.1
.

There are numerous examples of sheaves in nature:

•

Let
X
be a topological space and
k
be a field (e.g.,
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{C}
,…). Then for every open
U\subseteq X
, the association

U\mapsto\{f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\to k\mid f\ \text{is a continuous/smooth/differentiable $k$-valued function}\}

It is, in fact, a sheaf of rings.

•

The constant function on any topological space
X
is a presheaf, while the locally constant function is a sheaf.

•

Let
X
be a topological space and choose
x\in X
and any set
S
. For any open set
U\subseteq X
, the presheaf

\mathcal{F}(U)=\begin{cases}S&x\in U\\
\emptyset&x\notin U\\
\end{cases}

is, in fact, a sheaf, called the
sky scrapper sheaf
.

This notion of sheaves on a topological space can be generalized to any category
\mathscr{C}
. For any
X\in\mathscr{C}
, one considers maps of the form
\{U\to X\}
that "cover
X
" (in a sense that will be made precise below). Then one can construct a topology with respect to these (open) coverings and define a sheaf as the corresponding contravariant functor that satisfies some analogous locality and gluing properties. This notion was established by Grothendieck and his collaborators in SGA IV for studying the cohomology theories of generalized spaces and is popularly known as the
Grothendieck topology
. In a sense, it provides a way to "categorify" the notion of a sheaf from topological spaces to that of any small category
\mathscr{C}
. The collection of presheaves (resp. sheaves) on
\mathscr{C}
forms an example of a
functor category
. We denote the category of presheaf (resp. sheaf) on
\mathscr{C}
by
\operatorname{Psh}(\mathscr{C})
(resp.
\operatorname{Shv}(\mathscr{C})
).

To define the notion of a Grothendieck topology, we shall formulate it via the notion of sieves. Let
\mathscr{C}
be a (small) category.

Definition 30
.

Given an object
C\in\mathscr{C}
, a
sieve

S
is a family of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
, all with codomain
C
, such that

f\in S\Rightarrow f\circ g\in S

Whenever this composition makes sense. If
S
is a sieve on
C
and
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}D\to C
is any arrow to
C
, then

h^{*}(S)=\{g\mid\text{cod}(g)=D,\quad h\circ g\in S\}

is a sieve on
D
.

There is a convenient alternative definition of a sieve: Let
U
be an object of a category
\mathscr{C}
. A
sieve
on
U
is a sub functor of
h_{U}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{Set}
, where
h_{U}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Hom}(-,U)
is the representable functor on
U
.

Definition 31
.

A
Grothendieck topology
on a category
\mathscr{C}
is a function
J
which assigns to each object
C\in\mathscr{C}
, a collection
J(C)
of sieves on
C
, in such a way that the following three axioms are satisfied:

1.

(Maximality): The maximal sieve
t_{C}=\{f\mid\text{codomain}(f)=C\}
is in
J(C)
,

2.

(Stability): If
S\in J(C)
, then
h^{*}(S)\in J(D)
, for any arrow
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C\to D
,

3.

(Transitivity): If
S\in J(C)
and
R
is any sieve on
C
such that
h^{*}(R)\in J(D)
, for all
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}D\to C
in
S
, then
R\in J(C)
.

Definition 32
.

A
site
is a pair
(\mathscr{C},J)
of a small category
\mathscr{C}
equipped with a Grothendieck topology
J
. If
S\in J(C)
, one says that
S
is a covering sieve, or that "
S
covers
C
".

Example A.1.2
.

We shall now give some examples of sites.

•

Let
X
be a topological space; denote by
X_{op}
the category in which the objects are the open subsets of
X
, and the arrows are given by inclusions. Then we get a Grothendieck topology on
X_{op}
by associating with each open subset
U\subseteq X
, the set of open coverings of
U
. This defines a site of a topological space.

•

Let
\mathscr{C}=\mathrm{Top}
is the category of topological spaces. If
U
is a topological space, then a covering of
U
will be a jointly surjective
1
1

1

that is, the set-theoretic union of their images equals
U
collection of open embeddings
2
2

2

an open continuous injective map
V\to U

\{U_{i}\to U
}
. This is also called the
classical topology
on
X
.

•

In the example above, if in addition, the covering families
\{U_{i}\to U
}
are also local homeomorphisms, then it is called the
étale site
on
X
.

A.1.2
Sheaves on Schemes

We shall now develop the notion of sheaves on a site with a view towards application to algebraic geometry and eventually to the category of motivic spaces
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. Sheaves on a site can be defined in much the same way as sheaves on a topological space. Recall that a
ringed space
is a pair
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
, where
X
is a topological space and
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a sheaf of rings on
X
. It is
locally ringed
if, in addition, each stalk is a local ring. A scheme
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
is a canonical example of a locally ringed space which is locally isomorphic to an affine scheme in the Zariski topology. In other words, for every point
p\in X
, there exists an open neighborhood
U
such that
(U,\mathcal{O}_{X}|_{U})
is isomorphic to an affine scheme
(\operatorname{Spec}R,\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Spec}R})
. One equips the category of schemes with a different Grothendieck topology that varies depending on the nature of the open covering defined.

Example A.1.3
.

Let
T
be a scheme with a family of coverings
\{f_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T_{i}\to T\}_{i\in I}
such that
T=\bigcup_{i}f_{i}(T_{i})
, then we say that such a covering is called

•

Zariski
if each
f_{i}
is an open immersion,

•

étale
if each
f_{i}
is étale,

•

smooth
if each
f_{i}
is smooth,

•

fppf
3
3

3

in French for “fidèlement plat de présentation finie”.
(faithfully flat and finitely presented) if each
f_{i}
is flat, locally of finite presentation,

•

fpqc
4
4

4

in French for ”fidèlement plat” and quasi-compact.
(faithfully flat and and quasi-compact) if each
f_{i}
is flat and for each affine open
U\subseteq T
there exists a finite set
K
, a map
\textbf{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K\to I
and affine opens
U_{\textbf{i}(k)}\subset T_{\textbf{i}(k)}
such that
U=\bigcup_{k\in K}f_{\textbf{i}(k)}(U_{\textbf{i}(k)})
.

We will now describe another important topology called the
Nisnevich topology
that is well-suited for performing
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory.

A.1.3
The Nisnevich Site

We begin with an alternative definition of the Nisnevich topology from the one presented in
Section

2.1
. This is due to
[
137
, Definition 3.7.1.1]
(see also
[
158
, Lemma 1.5]
):

Definition 33
.

The
Nisnevich topology
on
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
is the topology generated by those finite families of étale morphisms
\{p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U_{i}\to X\}_{i\in I}
such that there is a finite sequence

\emptyset\subseteq Z_{n}\subseteq Z_{n-1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq Z_{1}\subseteq Z_{0}=X

of finitely presented closed subschemes of
X
such that

\bigsqcup_{i\in I}p_{i}^{-1}(Z_{m}\backslash Z_{m+1})\to Z_{m}\backslash Z_{m+1}

admits a section for
0\leq m\leq n-1
.

For a justification that this definition is indeed equivalent to that of being surjective on
k
-points, one can refer to
[
103
]
. Yet another equivalent definition of the Nisnevich topology is the following: it is a Grothendieck topology that is generated by the elementary distinguished squares (
[
19
, Remark 2.3.3]
). In terms of covering sieves, we have that the Nisnevich topology is the coarsest such that the empty sieve covers
\emptyset
, and for EDS as above, the sieve on
X
generated by
U\to X
and
V\to X
is a covering sieve. For any chosen definition, we have that the Nisnevich topology is finer than the Zariski topology but coarser than the étale topology:

\displaystyle\text{\'{e}tale}>>\text{Nisnevich}>\text{Zariski}

This dual nature makes the Nisnevich topology more suitable for doing motivic homotopy theory.

Advantages of Nisnevich topology

1.

The Nisnevich cohomological dimension (and even the homotopy dimension) of a scheme with Krull dimension
d
is equal to
d
(this is similar to the Zariski topology):
H^{i}_{Nis}(S,\mathcal{F})=0
for all
i>d
and any sheaf
\mathcal{F}
of Abelian groups (
[
158
, Proposition 1.8]
).

2.

For a field
k
, we have that any field has no non-trivial Nisnevich cohomology (similar to the Zariski topology):
H^{i}_{Nis}(\operatorname{Spec}k,\mathcal{F})=0
, for any sheaf
\mathcal{F}
of Abelian groups.

3.

Algebraic
K
-theory has Nisnevich descent (similar to the Zariski topology),

4.

Pushforward along finite morphisms is an exact functor on Nisnevich sheaves of abelian groups (this behaves like the étale topology).

5.

Nisnevich cohomology can be computed using Čech cohomology (similar to the étale topology): For any sheaf of abelian groups
\mathcal{F}
on
Sm_{S}
and any
n\geq 0
, we have an isomorphism of groups
\check{H}_{Nis}^{n}(S,\mathcal{F})\cong H_{Nis}^{n}(S,\mathcal{F})
(
[
158
, Proposition 1.9]
), where the left hand side is the Čech cohomology of a space
X
. This fails for Zariski topology (see
[
158
, Example 1.10]
).

6.

For closed immersions, Nisnevich sheaves exhibit purity isomorphism (like étale topology). As a consequence, we have that any smooth pair
(Z,X)
is locally equivalent in the Nisnevich topology to a pair of the form
(\mathbb{A}^{m},\mathbb{A}^{n})
: See
Theorem

2.1.56
.

7.

Since the Nisnevich topology is coarser than the étale topology, it inherits the property of being subcanonical from étale topology. This has the consequence that any representable presheaf in the Nisnevich topology is automatically a representable sheaf (see e.g.,
[
18
, §2.4]
or
[
190
,
Remark 03O4
]
).

A.2
Model Categories

The language of model categories was established by Quillen (
[
170
]
) as a suitable framework to deal with homotopy theoretic formulations. This framework has nowadays become almost indispensable to study any category up to some homotopical context; the first step one does is to garnish the desired category with a model structure and re-frame the original question in this setup. The far-reaching adaptability of model categories is due to their concise description that allows one to study morphisms in a given category by just studying two specified classes of maps: weak equivalences and either fibrations or cofibrations. This is furthermore enhanced by a certain adjoint pair of (Quillen) functors that are defined at the level of the homotopy categories. The formalism of localization from the commutative algebra allows one to formally invert a chosen multiplicative system. In application to algebraic geometry, localizations help us to formally invert any chosen class of maps, producing a new, larger category where these chosen maps become isomorphisms. In this section, we will give a brief introduction and background to this machinery with a focus on the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory. For most parts, we will follow
[
136
]
.

Definition 34
.

A
model structure
on a category
\mathscr{C}
consists of three distinguished classes of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}

•

cofibrations

\mathcal{C}
of

•

fibrations

\mathcal{F}
ib

•

weak equivalences

\mathcal{W}
eq

satisfying the following axioms:

1.

The category
\mathscr{C}
admits (small) limits and colimits.

2.

Given a composable pair of maps
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
, if any two of
g\circ f
,
f
, and
g
are weak equivalences, then so is the third (this is called the
two-out-of-three axiom
),

3.

Suppose
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
is a retract of
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
: that is, suppose there exists a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}

where
r\circ i=id_{X}
and
r^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime}=id_{Y}
. Then

(i)

If
g
is a fibration, so is
f
,

(ii)

If
g
is a cofibration, then so is
f
,

(iii)

If
g
is a weak equivalence, then so is
f
.

4.

Given a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

A dotted arrow can be found rendering the diagram commutative if either

(i)

The map
i
is a cofibration, and the map
p
is both a fibration and a weak equivalence.

(ii)

The map
i
is both a cofibration and a weak equivalence, and the map
p
is a fibration.

5.

Any map
X\to Z
in
\mathscr{C}
admits a functorial factorization as follows:

\displaystyle X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\

\displaystyle X\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}}Z

where
f
is a cofibration,
g
is a trivial fibration,
f^{\prime}
is a trivial cofibration, and
g^{\prime}
is a fibration.

A
model category
is a category
\mathscr{C}
equipped with a model structure.

We will encounter plenty of examples of model categories in due course. Here is the simplest of the kind:

Example A.2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be any category that admits small limits and colimits. Then
\mathscr{C}
can be endowed with the trivial model structure:

•

Weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
eq are isomorphisms of
\mathscr{C}
.

•

every morphism is a fibration
\mathcal{F}
ib.

•

every morphism is a cofibration
\mathcal{C}
of.

Definition 35
.

A morphism in a model category
\mathscr{C}
is called a
trivial cofibration
if it is both a cofibration and a weak equivalence. Similarly,
f
is called a
trivial fibration
if it is both a fibration and a weak equivalence.

By axiom (1), any model category
\mathscr{C}
has an initial object
\emptyset
and a final object
*
. This gives us the following description:

Definition 36
.

An object
X
of
M
is fibrant if
X\to*
is a fibration, and
X
is cofibrant if
\emptyset\to X
is a cofibration. Given an object
X
of
M
, an acyclic fibration
QX\to X
such that
QX
is cofibrant is called a
cofibrant replacement
. Similarly, if
X\to RX
is an acyclic fibration with
RX
fibrant, then
RX
is called a
fibrant replacement
of
X
.

These replacements always exist by the axioms of model categories.

A.2.1
Homotopy Category of a Model Category

Broadly speaking, one has two different (yet equivalent) ways to construct a homotopy category associated with a model category. We will now briefly explain both of the viewpoints.

Cylinder-Path Decomposition

Definition 37
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a model category containing an object
X
. A
cylinder object
for
X
is an object
C
together with a diagram
X\sqcup X\xrightarrow{i}C\xrightarrow{j}X
where
i
is a cofibration,
j
is a weak equivalence, and the composition
j\circ i
is the fold map
X\sqcup X\to X
. Dually, a
path object
for
Y\in\mathscr{C}
is an object
P
together with a diagram
Y\xrightarrow{q}P\xrightarrow{p}Y\times Y
such that
q
is a weak equivalence,
p
is a fibration, and
p\circ q
is the diagonal map
Y\to Y\times Y
.

The existence of cylinder and path objects follows from the factorization Axiom (5) of
34
. The following fact establishes an important tool for producing the homotopy category:

Proposition A.2.2
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a model category. Let
X
be a cofibrant object of
\mathscr{C}
,
Y
a fibrant object of
\mathscr{C}
, and
f,g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
two maps. The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

For every cylinder object
X\sqcup X\xrightarrow{j}C
there exists a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\sqcup X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g)}

2.

There exists a cylinder object
X\sqcup X\xrightarrow{j}C
and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\sqcup X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g)}

3.

For every path object
P\xrightarrow{p}Y\times Y
, there exists a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

4.

There exists a path object
P\xrightarrow{p}Y\times Y
and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

If
\mathscr{C}
is a model category containing a cofibrant object
X
and a fibrant object
Y
, we say two maps
f,g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
are
homotopic
if the hypotheses of
A.2.2
are satisfied and write
f\simeq g
. The relation
\simeq
is an equivalence relation on
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(X,Y)
.

Definition 38
.

The homotopy category
h\mathscr{C}
may be defined as follows:

•

The objects of
h\mathscr{C}
are the fibrant-cofibrant objects of
\mathscr{C}
.

•

The set
\operatorname{Hom}_{h\mathscr{C}}(X,Y)
is the set of
\simeq
-equivalence classes of
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(X,Y)
, for all
X,Y\in h\mathscr{C}
.

Note that the composition is well-defined in
h\mathscr{C}
: if
f\simeq g
, then
f\circ h\simeq g\circ h
(this is clear from characterization (4) of
A.2.2
) and
h^{\prime}\circ f\simeq h^{\prime}\circ g
(this is clear from characterization (2) of
A.2.2
) for any maps
h,h^{\prime}
such that the compositions are defined in
\mathscr{C}
.

Formal Inverses

The other viewpoint involves formally adding a class of (inverse) maps to enlarge the class of weak equivalence
\mathcal{W}eq
of
\mathscr{C}
. Let
H(\mathscr{C})
denote the category so obtained, then

H(\mathscr{C})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathscr{C}[\mathcal{W}eq]^{-1}.

If
X\in\mathscr{C}
is cofibrant and
Y\in\mathscr{C}
is fibrant, then homotopic maps
f,g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
have the same image in
H(\mathscr{C})
. Consequently, we obtain a functor
h\mathscr{C}\to H(\mathscr{C})
which can be shown to be an equivalence. Thus, both of these methods produce similar homotopy theories at least up to an equivalence. One more important technique to point out in this direction is that there is a universal way of adjoining all the homotopy colimits to
\mathscr{C}
to produce a homotopy category. This is called the
universal homotopy theory

U\mathscr{C}
constructed by Dugger
[
71
]
, which, though being a highly formal tool, has proven useful in the verification of certain generic properties of
\mathscr{C}
by looking at certain universal cases. For our context, it can be shown that the universal homotopy associated to
\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
,
U(\mathrm{Sm}_{k})_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
is Quillen equivalent to that of the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory of Morel-Voevodsky
\mathscr{MV}_{k}
(see
ibid
, §8) for more details.

A.2.2
Quillen Functors and Quillen Adjunctions

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be model categories and suppose we are given a pair of adjoint functors

\mathscr{C}\xrightleftharpoons[G]{F}\mathscr{D}

(here
F
is the left adjoint and
G
is the right adjoint). The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

The functor
F
preserves cofibrations and trivial cofibrations.

2.

The functor
G
preserves fibrations and trivial fibrations.

3.

The functor
F
preserves cofibrations, and the functor
G
preserves fibrations.

4.

The functor
F
preserves trivial cofibrations, and the functor
G
preserves trivial fibrations.

Definition 39
.

If any of this equivalent condition is satisfied, we say that the pair
(F,G)
is a
Quillen adjunction
between the model categories
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
and say that
F
is a
left Quillen functor
and that
G
is a
right Quillen functor
. In such a situation, one can show that
F
preserves weak equivalences between cofibrant objects and
G
preserves weak equivalences between fibrant objects.

For a Quillen adjunction as above, one views the homotopy category
h\mathscr{C}
as obtained from
\mathscr{C}
by first passing to the full subcategory consisting of cofibrant objects and then inverting all weak equivalences. Applying a similar procedure with
\mathscr{D}
, we see that because
F
preserves weak equivalence between cofibrant objects, it induces a functor
LF\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}h\mathscr{C}\to h\mathscr{D}
. This functor is called the
left derived functor
of
F
. Dually, one defines the
right derived functor
of
G
as
RG\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}h\mathscr{D}\to h\mathscr{C}
. One can furthermore show that the derived functors
LF
and
RG
determine an adjunction between the homotopy categories
h\mathscr{C}
and
h\mathscr{D}
:

Proposition A.2.3
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be model categories and let

\mathscr{C}\xrightleftharpoons[G]{F}\mathscr{D}

be a Quillen adjunction. The following are equivalent:

1.

The left derived functor
LF\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}h\mathscr{C}\to h\mathscr{D}
is an equivalence of categories.

2.

The right derived functor
RG\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}h\mathscr{D}\to h\mathscr{C}
is an equivalence of categories.

3.

For every cofibrant object
C\in\mathscr{C}
and every fibrant object
D\in\mathscr{D}
, a map
C\to G(D)
is a weak equivalence in
\mathscr{C}
if and only if the adjoint map
F(C)\to D
is a weak equivalence in
\mathscr{D}
.

If the equivalent conditions listed above are satisfied, then we say that the adjunction
(F,G)
gives a Quillen equivalence between the model categories
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
.

Proof.

See
[
136
, Proposition A.2.5.1.]
.
∎

Bousfield Localizations

Given a model category
\mathscr{C}
, we will now discuss a procedure to formally invert a certain class of maps to enlarge
\mathscr{C}
to include more weak equivalences via a process called
localization
. The reader should take note that this process of localizing maps does not turn the maps themselves into an isomorphism, but rather, it makes the images of those maps in the homotopy category into isomorphisms. As the image of a map in the homotopy category is an isomorphism if and only if the map is a weak equivalence, localizing a model category with respect to a class of maps amounts to making maps into weak equivalences rather than isomorphisms.

Definition 40
.

Let
M=(\mathcal{W}eq,\mathcal{C}of,\mathcal{F}ib)
be a simplicial model category with class of weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}eq
. Suppose that
I
is a set of maps in
M
.

•

An object
X
of
M
is
I
-local
if it is fibrant and if for all
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A\to B
with
i\in I
, the induced morphism on mapping spaces
i^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Map}_{M}(B,X)\to\operatorname{Map}_{M}(A,X)
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets.

•

A morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A\to B
is an
I
-local weak equivalence
if for every
I
-local object
X
, the induced morphism on mapping spaces

f^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Map}_{M}(B,X)\to\operatorname{Map}_{M}(A,X)
is a weak equivalence.

Let
\mathcal{F}ib_{I}
denote the class of maps satisfying the right lifting property with respect to
W_{I}
-acyclic cofibrations, denoted as
\{\mathcal{W}eq_{I}\cap\mathcal{C}of\}
. Then the model category structure on
M
given by
(\mathcal{W}eq_{I},\mathcal{C}of,\mathcal{F}ib_{I})
, if it exists, is called the
left Bousfield localization
of
M
with respect to
I
. Whenever it forms a model category, we denote this localization by
\mathbb{L}_{I}M
.

Remark A.2.4
.

When it exists, the Bousfield localization of
M
with respect to
I
is universal with respect to Quillen pairs
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}M\rightleftharpoons N\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}G
such that
LF(i)
is a weak equivalence in
N
, for all
i\in I
.

The following theorem portrays the existence of such localization in good cases and an elegant characterization of fibrant objects in those cases:

Theorem A.2.5
.

If
M
is a left proper and combinatorial simplicial model category and
I
is a set of morphisms in
M
, then the left Bousfield localization
\mathbb{L}_{I}M
exists and inherits a simplicial model category structure from
M
. Moreover, the fibrant objects of
\mathbb{L}_{I}M
are precisely the
I
-local objects of
M
.

Proof.

See
[
136
, Proposition A.3.7.3]
.
∎

For various other characterizations and existence of Bousfield localization for other model structures, one can refer to
[
136
, §A.3.7]
or
[
98
, Chapter 4]
. Dually for a right proper cellular model category, we have a theory of right Bousfield localization obtained by inverting the class of
\mathcal{W}eq_{I}
-acyclic fibrations
\mathcal{W}eq_{I}\cap\mathcal{F}ib
. This is called
cellularization
(see
[
98
, Chapter 5]
). The reader may also wish to refer to the classical reference
[
39
]
or a modern exposition
[
6
, §3.3]
.

We now turn to one of the most important model categories that has an elegant description and which has been well-studied in the literature.

A.2.3
Combinatorial Model structures

We will now briefly describe a special kind of model structure. A
combinatorial model category
is a tractable model structure with a very strong control over the cofibrations in this model structure. It is a structure that is generated from small data = from a small set of (acyclic) cofibrations between small objects.

Definition 41
.

A model category
\mathscr{C}
is
left proper
if, for any pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

in which
i
is a cofibration and
j
is a weak equivalence, the map
j
is also a weak equivalence. Dually,
\mathscr{C}
is
right proper
if, for any pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

in which
p
is a fibration and
q
is a weak equivalence, the map
q
is also a weak equivalence. If every object in
\mathscr{C}
is cofibrant, then
\mathscr{C}
is left proper.

Definition 42
.

[
136
, Definition 5.5.0.1]

A category
\mathscr{C}
is
presentable
if it satisfies the following conditions:

1.

The category
\mathscr{C}
admits all (small) colimits,

2.

There exists a (small) set
S
of objects of
\mathscr{C}
which generates
\mathscr{C}
under colimits; in other words, every object of
\mathscr{C}
may be obtained as the colimit of a (small) diagram taking values in
S
,

3.

Every object in
\mathscr{C}
is small. (Assuming (2), this is equivalent to the assertion that every object which belongs to
S
is small.)

4.

For any pair of objects
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
, the set
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(X,Y)
is small.

Example A.2.6
.

For any model category
\mathscr{C}
, the category of presheaves
\operatorname{Psh}(\mathscr{C})
is presentable. This follows from the fact that any presheaf is a colimit of representable ones (
A.2.15
).

Definition 43
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a presentable category and
\kappa
a regular cardinal. We will say that a full subcategory
\mathscr{C}_{0}\subset\mathscr{C}
is a
\kappa
-accessible subcategory
of
\mathscr{C}
if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

The full subcategory
\mathscr{C}_{0}\subset\mathscr{C}
is stable under
\kappa
-filtered colimits.

2.

There exists a (small) set of objects of
\mathscr{C}_{0}
which generates
\mathscr{C}_{0}
under
\kappa
-filtered colimits.

We will say that
\mathscr{C}_{0}\subset\mathscr{C}
is an accessible subcategory if
\mathscr{C}_{0}
is a
\kappa
-accessible subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
for some regular cardinal
\kappa
.

Remark A.2.7
.

An
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathscr{C}
is
presentable
if
\mathscr{C}
is accessible and admits small colimits.

Example A.2.8
.

The category of motivic spaces
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}\subset\operatorname{Psh}(Sm_{S})
is an accessible subcategory of
\operatorname{Psh}(Sm_{S})
. This follows from
[
136
, Proposition 5.5.1.2]
and the fact that
\operatorname{Psh}(Sm_{S})
is an accessible category.

Definition 44
.

A model category
\mathscr{C}
is
cofibrantly generated
if there are small sets of morphisms
I,J\subset Mor(\mathscr{C})
such that

•

\mathcal{C}of(I)
is precisely the collection of cofibrations of
\mathscr{C}
,

•

\mathcal{C}of(J)
is precisely the collection of acyclic cofibrations in
\mathscr{C}
,

•

I
and
J
permit the small object argument.

The key takeaway from the above definition is that in a cofibrantly generated model category, the collection of cofibrations and acyclic cofibrations can be characterized in a much simpler way:

(a)

\mathcal{C}of(I)=llp(rlp(I)),

(b)

\mathcal{C}of(J)=llp(rlp(J)).

And therefore the fibrations form precisely
rlp(J)
and the acyclic fibrations precisely
rlp(I)
. Here,
llp=\text{left lifting property}
and
rlp=\text{right lifting property}
. For equivalent definitions and various discussions of cofibrantly generated categories, one can refer to
[
164
,
Cofib-gen
]
. Since in practice these generators might be difficult to find,
[
136
, §A.2.6]
provides an alternative definition which puts more emphasis on the collection of weak equivalences in
\mathscr{C}
.

Definition 45
.

A model category
\mathscr{C}
is
combinatorial
if it is presentable as a category and cofibrantly generated as a model category.

There are at least two brilliant ways of identifying combinatorial structure on
\mathscr{C}
:

1.

A theorem due to Smith (
[
31
, Theorem 1.7]
) characterizes combinatorial model categories in terms of weak equivalences and generating cofibrations, hence using only two-thirds of the input data explicitly required, which facilitates identifying combinatorial structures. For example, one can show that the left Bousfield localization of certain combinatorial model categories is again a combinatorial model category.

2.

Dugger (
Theorem

A.2.9
) identifies the combinatorial structures with those model categories that have a presentation in that they are Bousfield localizations of global model structures on simplicial presheaves, up to Quillen equivalence (
[
70
, Corollary 1.2]
). This was an important tool in Lurie’s proof to show that simplicial combinatorial model categories are precisely the models for locally presentable
(\infty,1)
-categories.

Theorem A.2.9
.

Every combinatorial model category
\mathscr{C}
is Quillen equivalent to a left Bousfield localization
\mathbb{L}_{S}\operatorname{sPsh}(K)
f the global projective model structure on simplicial presheaves
\operatorname{sPsh}(K)_{proj}
on a small category
K

\mathbb{L}_{S}\operatorname{sPsh}(K)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathscr{C}

where
S\subset\operatorname{sPsh}(K)
is a well-defined subset.

Proof.

This is the main result of
[
70
]
.
∎

A.2.4
Homotopy Theory on Simplicial Presheaves

In this section, we will briefly review the category of simplicial sets and the simplicial presheaves and equip it with a model categorical structure following
[
101
,
86
,
136
]
.

The Category of Simplicial Sets

Definition 46
.

For each
n\geq 0
, we let
[n]\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{0<1<\dots n-1<n\}
denote the linearly ordered set. The category of (combinatorial) simplices, denoted
\Delta
, is the category with:

•

Objects: linearly ordered sets
[n]
(a.k.a. string of relations
1\to 2\to\dots\to n
).

•

Morphisms:
\theta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}[m]\to[n]
is an order-preserving set function.

The category
\Delta
is also called the
ordinal number category
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is any category, a simplicial object of
\mathscr{C}
is a functor
\Delta^{op}\to\mathscr{C}
. Dually, a
cosimplicial object
of
\mathscr{C}
is a functor
\Delta\to\mathscr{C}
.

Definition 47
.

The category of
simplicial sets
, denoted as
\operatorname{sSet}
, is a simplicial object in the category of sets, that is,
\operatorname{sSet}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Fun}(\Delta^{op},\operatorname{Set})
.

Since the category of sets has all (small) limits and colimits, it follows that the category of simplicial sets also has all (small) limits and colimits.

Example A.2.10
.

There is a standard covariant functor

\displaystyle\Delta\to\mathrm{Top}

\displaystyle n\mapsto|\Delta^{n}|

The
topological
n
-simplex

\mid\Delta^{n}\mid\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is the space

|\Delta^{n}|\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\sum_{t=0}^{n}t_{i}=1,t_{i}\geq 0\}

with the subspace topology. The map
\theta^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}|\Delta^{n}|\to|\Delta^{m}|
induced by
\theta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}[m]\to[n]
is defined by

\theta_{*}(t_{0},\dots,t_{m})=(s_{0},\dots,s_{n})

where

s_{i}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\quad\theta^{-1}=\emptyset\\
\sum_{j\in\theta^{-1}(i)}&\text{if}\quad\text{else}\end{cases}

For any
T\in\operatorname{sSet}
, the
singular set

\operatorname{Sing}(T)
is the simplicial set given by

[n]\mapsto\hom(|\Delta^{n},T|)

By convention, we extend this construction by setting
\Delta^{-1}=\emptyset
. The standard 0-simplex
\Delta^{0}
