is a final object of the category of simplicial sets, that is, it carries each
[n]\in\Delta^{op}
to a set having a single element.

Remark A.2.11
.

There is an equivalent definition of a simplicial set that is useful for computations. A simplicial set
S_{\bullet}
can be viewed as a collection of sets
\{S_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
endowed with two additional structures:

\displaystyle d^{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}[n-1]\to[n],\quad 0\leq i\leq n\quad(\text{cofaces})

\displaystyle s^{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}[n+1]\to[n],\quad 0\leq j\leq n\quad(\text{codegeneracies})

Both of these maps
d^{i}
and
s^{j}
are expected to satisfy a bunch of equalities, called the
simplicial identities
that can be found in any of the standard references (e.g.,
[
86
, Chapter 1, §1]
). Every order-preserving map from
[n]
to
[m]
can be factored as a composition of face and degeneracy maps, and so the structure of a simplicial set
S
is completely determined by the sets
S_{n}
for
n\geq 0
together with these face and degeneracy maps.

Example A.2.12
.

The
standard
n
-simplex
, simplicial
\Delta^{n}
in the simplicial set category
\operatorname{sSet}
is defined by

\Delta^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\hom_{\Delta}(-,[n]).

Put alternatively,
\Delta^{n}
is the contravariant functor on
\Delta
which is represented by
[n]
.

A morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
of simplicial sets, called the
simplicial map
, is a natural transformation of contravariant set-valued functors defined on
\Delta
. The Yoneda lemma implies that simplicial maps
\Delta^{n}\to Y
classify
n
-simplices of
Y
in the sense that there is a natural bijection

\hom_{\operatorname{sSet}}(\Delta^{n},Y)\cong Y_{n}

between the set
Y_{n}
of
n
-simplices of
Y
and the set
\hom_{S}(\Delta^{n},Y)
. There is a standard simplex

\iota_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\textbf{1}_{n}\in\hom_{\Delta}([n],[n])

Example A.2.13
.

The category
\Delta^{n}
contains two important sub-complexes: The
k
-th horn
\Lambda^{n}_{k}

0\leq k\leq n
which is a sub-complex of
\Delta^{n}
generated by all faces
d_{j}(\iota_{n})
except the
k
th face
d_{k}(\iota_{n})
and the boundary
\partial\Delta^{n}
, which is the smallest sub-complex of
\Delta^{n}
containing the faces
d_{j}(\iota_{n})
,
0\leq j\leq n
of the standard simplex
\iota_{n}
.

Model structure on Simplicial Sets

The category
\operatorname{sSet}
of simplicial sets has a combinatorial, left proper, and right proper model structure, which is usually referred to as the
Kan model structure
defined as follows:

1.

A map of simplicial sets
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
is a
cofibration
if it is a monomorphism: if the induced map
X_{n}\to Y_{n}
is injective for all
n\geq 0
,

2.

A map of simplicial sets
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
is a
fibration
if it is a Kan fibration: if for any diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Lambda^{n}_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

It is possible to supply the dotted arrow rendering the diagram commutative, where
\Lambda^{n}_{i}
is the
i
-th horn of
\Delta^{n}
.

3.

A map of simplicial sets
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
is a
weak equivalence
if the induced map of geometric realizations
|X|\to|Y|
is a homotopy equivalence of topological spaces.

Studying model structures on the category of simplicial sets is the fundamental step towards building model structures for the category of motivic
S
-spaces
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
. This is essentially since the category of simplicial presheaves on which the category of spaces
\operatorname{Spc}_{S}
is built is, in fact, enriched over
\operatorname{sSet}
(such that the enrichment is compatible with the model structure). Such enriched categories are called
Simplicial Categories
. Briefly, speaking a model category
\mathscr{C}
is
simplicial
if the following are satisfied:

1.

There exists mapping space functor
\operatorname{Map}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}^{op}\times\mathscr{C}\to\operatorname{sSet}

2.

There exists a product functor
\otimes\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{sSet}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}

with a compatibility that the product is associative, that is,

•

(L\times K)\otimes X\simeq L\times(K\otimes X)
, natural in
X\in\mathscr{C}
,
K,L\in\operatorname{sSet}

•

\Delta^{0}
is a unit object, i.e.
\Delta^{0}\otimes X\cong X
.

•

for a
X\in\mathscr{C}
and
K\in\operatorname{sSet}
, there are adjoint functor pairs

\displaystyle\otimes X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{sSet}\leftrightharpoons\mathscr{C}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Map(-,X)

\displaystyle K\otimes-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}\leftrightharpoons\operatorname{sSet}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}(-)^{K}

Example A.2.14
.

The category
\operatorname{sSet}
together with the model structure introduced above is a simplicial model category if we take

K\otimes X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=K\times X
(
[
86
, Proposition 11.5]
).

A.2.5
Category of Simplicial Presheaves

The category of simplicial presheaves
\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathscr{C})
on a model category
\mathscr{C}
is the contravariant functor

\operatorname{sPsh}(\mathscr{C})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Fun}(\mathscr{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{sSet})

We shall now briefly discuss some of the relevant model structures on the category of simplicial presheaves. Several model structures on the category of presheaves have been studied throughout history (See
[
108
,
97
,
86
,
98
,
71
,
73
,
107
,
101
,
174
]
). Classically speaking, one equips this category with four possible model structures:

•

(Global) injective and local injective model structure

•

(Global) projective and local projective model structure

The injective model structure is the one where cofibrations are detected pointwise
5
5

5

also called as sectionwise or objectwise
, and fibrations are defined as those that satisfy the left lifting property with respect to trivial (also called as acyclic) cofibrations. On the other hand, the projective model structure is the one where weak equivalences stay the same, but fibrations are detected pointwise, and cofibrations are those morphisms that satisfy the left lifting property with respect to the acyclic fibrations. Hence, the injective model structure is more suited to describe cofibrations and the projective model structure to describe fibrations. The local analogs are then obtained by (Bousfield) localizing with respect to a suitable class of morphisms to produce certain additional weak equivalences that allow one to study the category locally.

For the current exposition below, we will follow
[
183
,
6
]
. We will only build this theory on the level of presheaves, noting that all of these can also be lifted to the category of simplicial sheaves via the adjoint functor pair

a\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{sPsh}(T)\rightleftarrows\operatorname{sShv}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\iota_{*}

where
\iota
is the inclusion functor and
a
is the associated sheaf functor that is defined objectwise.

Definition 48
.

Let
T=(\mathcal{C},J)
denote a small Grothendieck site. Then the simplicial objects denoted by

\operatorname{sPsh}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Fun}(\Delta^{op},\operatorname{Psh}(T))

are called the
simplicial presheaves
on
T
. That is,
\operatorname{sPsh}(T)=\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\operatorname{sSet})
which are
\operatorname{sSet}
-valued presheaves on
T
. Furthermore, we have that the pointed simplicial presheaves are the same as
\operatorname{sSet}
-valued presheaves on
T
, i.e.,
\operatorname{sPsh}_{*}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\operatorname{sSet}_{*})
.

Proposition A.2.15
.

Every simplicial presheaf is canonically isomorphic to a colimit of representable simplicial presheaves
R_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,X)
.

Proof.

This is due to
[
139
, Chapter III, §7, Theorem 1]
.
∎

Corollary A.2.16
.

Every motivic space is a colimit of representable ones.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\mathbb{L}_{mot}
preserves colimits (See
2.1.15
). See also
[
73
, Lemma 2.4]
.
∎

Definition 49
.

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
be a map of simplicial presheaves. Then
f
is called a

•

local weak equivalence
, if
f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\pi_{0}(X)\to\pi_{0}(Y)
is a bijection and for all
n>0
and
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the induced map
\pi_{n}(X|_{U},x)\to\pi_{n}(Y|_{U},f(x)
is an isomorphism of sheaves for all base points
x\in X(U)_{0}
.

•

objectwise weak equivalence
(resp. (co-)fibrations if for all
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the induced map
f(U)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X(U)\to Y(U)
is a weak equivalence (resp. (co-)fibration) in
\operatorname{sSet}
.

There is a closed symmetric monoidal structure on simplicial presheaves (
[
139
, Chapter XI, §1]
). Let
X,Y
be simplicial presheaves. Define the simplicial function complex

\operatorname{Map}(X,Y)_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{sPsh}(T)}(X\times\Delta^{n},Y)

where
\Delta^{n}
is taken as the constant simplicial presheaf. The internal function complex is given by

\underline{\operatorname{Map}}(X,Y)(U)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Map}(X|_{U},Y|_{U})

on simplicial sheaves, and one shows that this complex is again a simplicial presheaf. The category of simplicial presheaf, together with the categorical product

-\times-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{sPsh}(T)\times\operatorname{sPsh}(t)\to\operatorname{sPsh}(T)

is symmetric monoidal, where
X\times Y
is denoted by
X\otimes Y
. Moreover, they are closed since

-\otimes X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{sPsh}(T)\rightleftarrows\operatorname{sPsh}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\underline{\operatorname{Map}}(X,-)

The following is a version of the Yoneda lemma over
\operatorname{sSet}
.

Lemma A.2.17
.

Let
X\in\mathcal{C}
and
F\in\operatorname{sPsh}(T)
. Then we have that

\operatorname{Map}(R_{X},F)\cong F(X)\in\operatorname{sSet}.

Lemma A.2.18
.

Let
F,G
be simplicial presheaves and
X\in\mathcal{C}
. Then

\underline{\operatorname{Map}}(R_{X},G)=G(-\times X)\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{\operatorname{Map}}(F,G)(X)=\operatorname{Map}(F,G(-\times X))

for presheaves.

All these can also be formulated for pointed simplicial presheaves. Furthermore, it is a closed symmetric monoidal category with respect to the smash product

-\wedge X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{sPsh}_{*}(T)\rightleftarrows\operatorname{sPsh}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\underline{\operatorname{Map}}_{*}(X,-)

Model Structure on Simplicial Presheaves

We are now ready to define the (local) model structures on simplicial presheaves. We begin with the following simplicial structure.

Definition 50
.

Let
X,Y
be simplicial presheaves and
K
be a simplicial set considered as a constant presheaf. Then define

K\otimes X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=K\times X

and
\operatorname{Map}(X,Y)
is the simplicial function complex declares as
X^{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\underline{\operatorname{Map}}(K,X)
and
\underline{\operatorname{Map}}(K,X)(U)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Map}(K,X(U))
. Then

-\otimes X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{sSet}\rightleftarrows\operatorname{sPsh}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Map}(X,-)

and

K\otimes-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{sPsh}(T)\rightleftarrows\operatorname{sPsh}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}(-)^{K}

are an adjoint pair of functors.

We now define its homotopy category.

Definition 51
.

Let
X
be a simplicial presheaf. Define
\pi_{0}(X)
as the sheafified presheaf

U\mapsto\pi_{0}(X(U))

for
U\in\mathcal{C}
, where
\pi_{0}(X(U))
is just the connected components of the simplicial set
X(U)
. Let
U\in\mathcal{C},x\in X(U)_{0},
and
n>0
. Define
\pi_{n}(X|_{U},x)
as the sheafified presheaf

(V\to U)\mapsto\pi_{n}(X(V),x_{V})

of homotopy groups of simplicial sets on the site
T\downarrow U
and
x_{V}\in X(V)_{0}
is just the image of
x
under
X(U)_{0}\to X(V)_{0}
. Here,
X\downarrow U
is the restricted pointed simplicial presheaf, which is defined via the composition

X|_{U}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mathcal{C}\downarrow U)^{op}\xrightarrow{(\iota_{U})^{op}}\mathcal{C}^{op}\xrightarrow{X}\operatorname{sSet}_{*}

Definition 52
.

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
be a map of simplicial presheaves. Then
f
is called a

•

local weak equivalence
if
f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\pi_{0}(X)\to\pi_{0}(Y)
is an isomorphism of sheaves and for all
n>0
and
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the induced map
f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\pi_{n}(X|_{U},x)\to\pi_{n}(Y|_{U},f(x)
is an isomorphism of sheaves for all base points
x\in X(U)_{0}
,

•

objectwise weak equivalence
if for all
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
f(U)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X(U)\to Y(U)
is a weak equivalence in
\operatorname{sSet}
,

•

objectwise (co-)fibration
if for all
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
f(U)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X(U)\to Y(U)
is a (co)-fibration in
\operatorname{sSet}
.

The Four Model Structures on Simplicial Presheaves

The following theorems establish the (local) injective model structure on simplicial presheaves due to Jardine (
[
108
]
):

Theorem A.2.19
.

The category of simplicial presheaves on a small Grothendieck site together with the distinguished classes of maps

•

(local) objectwise weak equivalences

•

objectwise cofibrations

•

injective fibrations, those maps having the right lifting property with respect to acyclic (trivial) objectwise cofibrations.

form a proper simplicial cofibrantly generated model category and is called the
(local) injective model structure
(cf.
[
35
, Theorem 1.1]
).

For instance, the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory as established by
[
158
]
uses this model structure. There is an analogous model structure obtained by swapping the cofibrations with fibrations due to Bousfield and Kan (
[
38
]
):

Theorem A.2.20
.

The category of simplicial presheaves on a small Grothendieck site together with the distinguished classes of maps

•

(local) objectwise weak equivalences

•

objectwise fibrations

•

projective cofibrations, those maps having the left lifting property with respect to acyclic (trivial) objectwise fibrations.

form a proper simplicial cellular/combinatorial model category and is called the
(local) projective model structure
(cf.
[
35
, Theorem 1.4]
,
[
6
, Proposition 3.32]
).

The projective model category structure has a special universal property highlighted by Dugger
[
71
]
: it is the initial model category into which
C
embeds. It is essential to note that both of these model structures are Quillen equivalent (see e.g.,
[
183
, Theorem 6.2.11]
) and hence produce the same homotopy theory.

We will end this chapter with a collection of useful notions from model categories.

Kan Extensions

Let us now define the Kan extensions. The Kan extension of a functor
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
, if it exists, is one of the best approximations to finding an extension from another category
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
to that of
\mathscr{C}
. In other words, the Kan extension of
F
with respect to a functor
p\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C^{\prime}}\to\mathscr{D}
is the functor that makes the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces?}

commutative. In this way, it extends the domain of
F
through
p
from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
. Dually, there is also a notion of Kan lift that is analogously defined to lift any functor. Here we give an official definition of this fact (
[
138
,
Tag 02Y1
]
):

Definition 53
.

Let
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be a functor between categories and let
\mathscr{C}^{0}\subset\mathscr{C}
be a full subcategory. We say that
F
is
Left Kan extended from
\mathscr{C}^{0}
if, for every object
C\in\mathscr{C}
, the collection of morphisms
\{F(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}F(C_{0})\to F(C)\}_{u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{0}\to C}
exhibits
F(C)
as a colimit of the diagram

(\mathscr{C}^{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{C}_{/C})\to\mathscr{C}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{F}\mathscr{D}.

Such a functor, if it exists, is unique. In the above,
\mathscr{C}_{/C}
we mean the
overcategory
whose objects are maps
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to C
and morphisms between
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to C
and
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to C
is a morphism
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
such that the obvious commutative triangle commutes.

Proposition A.2.21
.

Let
F,G\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be functors between categories, and suppose that
F
is left Kan extended from a full subcategory
\mathscr{C}^{0}\subset\mathscr{C}
. Then the restriction map

\{\text{Natural transformation from $F$ to $G$}\to{\text{Natural transformation from $F_{|{\mathscr{C}^{0}}}$ to $G_{|{\mathscr{C}^{0}}}$}}\}

is bijective. In particular, the functor
F
can be recovered (up to canonical isomorphism) from the restriction
F_{|{\mathscr{C}^{0}}}

The existence of such an extension can be tested by looking at a certain colimit in
\mathscr{D}
.

Proposition A.2.22
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a category, let
\mathscr{C}^{0}\subset\mathscr{C}
be a full subcategory, and let
F_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}^{0}\to\mathscr{D}
be a functor between categories. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

There exists a functor
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C\to D
which is left Kan extended from
\mathscr{C}^{0}
and satisfies
F_{|_{\mathscr{C}^{0}}}\to F_{0}

2.

For every object
C\in\mathscr{C}
, the diagram

(\mathscr{C}^{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{C}_{/C})\to\mathscr{C}^{0}\xrightarrow{F_{0}}\mathscr{D}.

Among all the mathematical formulations, Kan extensions are one of the most ubiquitous occurrences in nature. Here are a few of them.

Example A.2.23
.

One may think of left Kan extensions as a generalization of colimits and dually. Let
\mathscr{C}
be any category that is both complete and cocomplete (eg,
\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{Set}
). Let
\mathscr{D}
be any small category, then all functors
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
have both right and left Kan extensions along all functors
K\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
. In particular, any functor
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
has both right and left Kan extensions along
1_{\mathscr{C}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
. By the universal property of Kan extensions, this in turn implies that both left and right Kan extensions are
F
itself.

It is a general fact that the left adjoint to any pre-composition functor is a left Kan extension, and the right adjoint to any pre-composition functor is a right Kan extension. This follows since adjoints are unique up to unique isomorphism. The following illustrates this principle:

Example A.2.24
.

Fix a group
G
with a subgroup
H\leq G
along with the inclusion functor
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H\to G
. The category
[G,Vect_{k}]
of functors from the group
G
to the
k
-vector spaces has
G
-representations as objects and
G
-equivariant linear maps as morphisms. As any representation of
G
can be restricted through the inclusion map
i
to a representation of
H
, we have a functor
\text{Res}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}[G,Vect_{k}]\to[H,Vect_{k}]
defined by precomposition with
i
. Now, any representation on
H
can be extended to the (induced) representation of
G
via the induction functor
Ind
and vice-versa, extended to the coinduced representation of
G
, via the coinduction functor
Coind
. These extensions define functors,
\text{Ind, Coind}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}[H,Vect_{k}]\to[G,Vect_{k}]
, which are left and right adjoints to the restriction functor
Res
. Due to the general principle stated above, we have that these functors define left and right Kan extensions along the
i
.

Cofinality

Definition 54
.

A subcategory
I\subseteq J
is said to be
cofinal
if for any functor
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}J\to\mathcal{C}
, the induced map on colimits

\text{colim}_{I}F\to\text{colim}_{J}F

is an isomorphism. Hence, to compute colimits in
J
, it suffices to compute it in the subdiagram
I\subseteq J
.

Example A.2.25
.

The subcategories
2\mathbf{N}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
2\mathbb{N}+1\subseteq\mathbb{N}
are cofinal in
\mathbb{N}
.

A Very Weak Five Lemma

We state the 5-lemma for a general pointed model category and elicit its effectiveness under contractibility assumptions. For more reference, we redirect the reader to
[
101
, Chapter 6]
.

Lemma A.2.26
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be any pointed model category and consider the following commutative diagram in
\mathcal{C}
where the rows are cofiber sequences.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}

If
f
and
g
are weak equivalences, then so is
h
. In addition, assume that
f
and
h
are weak equivalences and
B
is contractible in
\mathcal{C}
, then
g
is a weak equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
and consequently,
B^{\prime}
is contractible in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

The proof is due to
[
101
, Proposition 6.5.3 (b)]
. It suffices to show that
[B^{\prime},X]\simeq*
for every object
X\in\mathcal{C}
. Applying the representable Hom functor
[-,X]
for any object
X\in\mathcal{C}
to the above diagram, we get a commutative diagram of long exact sequences of pointed sets and groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,X]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[B,X]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C,X]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\Sigma A,X]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A^{\prime},X]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[B^{\prime},X]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[C^{\prime},X]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\Sigma A^{\prime},X]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u^{\prime})*}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(v^{\prime})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Sigma f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\prime}}

We conclude that the fiber
g^{*}
is trivial by a simple diagram chase. As
[B,X]\simeq*
, it follows that
[B^{\prime},X]\simeq*
as well.
∎

The following lemma, due to Ken Brown, is extremely useful in model categories.

Lemma A.2.27
.

Suppose
\mathscr{C}
is a model category and
\mathscr{D}
is a category with a subcategory of weak equivalences that satisfies the two out of three axiom. Suppose
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
is a functor that takes trivial cofibrations between cofibrant objects to weak equivalences. Then
F
takes all weak equivalences between cofibrant objects to weak equivalences. Dually, if
F
takes trivial fibrations between fibrant objects to weak equivalences, then
F
takes all weak equivalences between fibrant objects to weak equivalences.

Proof.

This is due to
[
101
, Lemma 1.1.12]
.
∎

Appendix B
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-Algebraic Topology

In this section, we define and illustrate the first properties of strictly and strongly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant following the book "
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-algebraic topology over a field
" (
[
160
]
). In the sequel, we will also define the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-derived category and the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homology sheaves, which we rely on in
Chapter

5
. Following this, we present the theory of Milnor-Witt sheaves and the associated Chow-Witt theory within the scope of this script.

One has from algebraic topology that the set of path-connected components
\pi_{0}
and the homotopy groups
\ pi_{i}
are all discrete. The study of (strictly and strongly)
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant sheaves can be thought of as studying the analogous "discreteness" property of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theoretic sheaves.

Definition 55
.

All the sheaves mentioned below are considered in the Nisnevich topology.

1.

A sheaf of sets
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
is said to be
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant
if for any
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
,the map

\mathcal{S}(X)\to\mathcal{S}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1})

induced by the projection
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times X\to X
, is a bijection.

2.

A sheaf of groups
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
is said to be
strongly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant
if for any
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
, the map

H^{i}_{Nis}(X;\mathcal{G})\to H^{i}_{Nis}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1};\mathcal{G})

induced by the projection
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times X\to X
, is a bijection for
i\in\{0,1\}
.

3.

A sheaf of groups
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
is said to be
strictly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant
if for any
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
, the map

H^{i}_{Nis}(X,\mathcal{M})\to H^{i}_{Nis}(X\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{1},\mathcal{M})

induced by the projection
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times X\to X
, is a bijection for
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark B.0.1
.

One also has the following characterization of these sheaves in terms of their associated algebraic invariants:

1.

A sheaf of set
\mathcal{S}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant if and only if it is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local as a space,

2.

A sheaf of groups
\mathcal{G}
is strongly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant if and only if the classifying space
B(\mathcal{G})=\textbf{K}(\mathcal{G},1)
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local space,

3.

A sheaf of abelian groups
\mathcal{M}
is strictly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant if and only if for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the Eilenberg-MacLane space
\textbf{K}(\mathcal{M},n)
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local.

Theorem B.0.2
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a pointed space. Then the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fundamental sheaf of groups
\pi_{1}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{X})
is strongly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant. For any
n\geq 2
, the higher
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy sheaves of abelian groups
\pi_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
are strictly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant.

Proof.

This proof is due to
[
160
, Theorem 6.1, Corollary 6.2]
.
∎

Example B.0.3
.

For
i\geq 1
, the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy sheaves
\pi_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
are strongly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant, Rost cycle modules,
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant sheaves with transfers of Voevodsky, sheaf
W
of unramified Witt groups.

B.0.1
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-derived category

Let us denote by
\mathcal{A}b(k)
the abelian category of sheaves of abelian groups on
\mathrm{Sm}_{S}
in the Nisnevich topology. Let
C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k))
be the category of chain complexes in
\mathcal{A}b(k)
. As in the case of classical homological algebra, one obtains the derived category of
\mathcal{A}b(k)
, denoted by
D(\mathcal{A}b(k))
, from
C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k))
by inverting the class of quasi-isomorphisms between chain complexes. To establish the model categorical structure, one can use the following (Nisnevich) local model structure on the category of chain complexes
C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k))
in
\mathcal{A}b(k)
:

1.

A morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A_{*}\to B_{*}
in
C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k))
is a
weak equivalence
if it induces a quasi isomorphism over the sections of the smooth Henselian local schemes (stalks in the Nisnevich topology),

2.

A morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A_{*}\to B_{*}
is a fibration if it is an epimorphism,

3.

A morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A_{*}\to B_{*}
is a cofibration if it has the left lifting property with respect to the trivial fibrations.

The derived category
D(\mathcal{A}b(k))
is the associated homotopy category obtained by formally inverting the quasi-isomorphisms in the above model structure. We will now define the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-derived category. It is obtained by the left Bousfield localization, where we invert the projection maps with respect to
\mathbb{Z}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k})
in the above local model structure, where
\mathbb{Z}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k})
is the free abelian sheaf on
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
.

Definition 56
.

We will now define the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-derived category.

•

A chain complex
D_{*}\in C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k))
is called
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local
if and only if for any
C_{*}\in C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k))
, the projection
C_{*}\otimes\mathbb{Z}(\mathbb{A}^{1})\to C_{*}
induces a bijection:

Hom_{D(\mathcal{A}b(k))}(C_{*},D_{*})\to Hom_{D(\mathcal{A}b(k))}(C_{*}\otimes\mathbb{Z}(\mathbb{A}^{1}),D_{*}).

•

A morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{*}\to D_{*}
in
C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k))
is called an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-quasi isomorphism
if and only if for any
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local chain complex
E_{*}
, the morphism:

Hom_{D(\mathcal{A}b(k))}(D_{*},E_{*})\to Hom_{D(\mathcal{A}b(k))}(C_{*},E_{*})

is bijective.

•

Finally, we define the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-derived category
D_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{A}b(k))
as the category obtained by inverting the all the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-quasi isomorphisms.

As in the case of the theory of simplicial presheaves, we have an (abelian)
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-localization functor

L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{Ab}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k))\to C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k))

together with a natural transformation
\theta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Id\to L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{Ab}
such that for any chain complex
C_{*}
,

\theta_{C_{*}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{*}\to L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{Ab}(C_{*})

is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-quasi isomorphism whose target is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-local fibrant chain complex. There is natural functoriality that one derives as a formal consequence of such a framework (See
[
160
, §6.2]
).

\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homology sheaves

Recall that in algebraic topology (see e.g.,
[
145
, Chapter 5]
), we have the notion of a normalized chain complex
N(C_{*})
associated to any chain complex
C_{*}
. Importing this machinery into algebraic geometry, one defined the notion of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homology sheaves (
[
160
, §6.3]
). We will briefly touch upon this now.
Let
\mathcal{X}
be any simplicial sheaf of sets with its normalized chain complex
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{X})
in
C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k))
, which is associated to the free simplicial sheaf of abelian groups
\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{X})
on
\mathcal{X}
. This defines a functor

\mathcal{N}_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Delta^{op}\operatorname{Shv}_{Nis}(\mathrm{Sm}_{k})\to C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k)).

This functor has a right adjoint

K\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{*}(\mathcal{A}b(k))\to\Delta^{op}\operatorname{Shv}_{Nis}(\mathrm{Sm}_{k})

called the
Eilenberg-MacLane space functor
.

Remark B.0.4
.

For an abelian sheaf
\mathcal{A}\in\mathcal{A}b(k)
and an integer
n
, the pointed simplicial sheaf
K(\mathcal{A},n)
is defined by applying
K
to the shifted complex
\mathcal{A}[n]
, of the complex
\mathcal{A}
placed in degree 0. It is characterized by the following property:

\pi_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(K(\mathcal{A},n))\cong\begin{cases}0&if $n<0$\\
\mathcal{A}&if $i=n$\\
0&if $i\neq n$\end{cases}

Moreover, it is a fact that the functor
\mathcal{N}_{*}(-)
sends simplicial weak equivalences to quasi-isomorphisms and
K(-)
maps quasi-isomorphisms to simplicial weak equivalences. As a consequence, these induce a pair of adjoint functors

\mathcal{N}_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}_{s}(k)\to D(\mathcal{A}b(k))

and

K\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}D(\mathcal{A}b(k))\to\mathcal{H}_{s}(k).

Thus
\mathcal{N}_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}_{s}(k)\to D(\mathcal{A}b(k))
maps
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-quasi isomorphisms and induces a functor

\mathcal{N}_{*}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}(k)\to D_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{A}b(k))

defined by

\mathcal{X}\mapsto L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{Ab}(\mathcal{N}_{*}(\mathcal{X})).

Definition 57
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a space and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
be an integer. The
n
-th
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homology sheaf
associated to
\mathcal{X}
, denoted as
H_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{X})
, is defined as the
n
-th homology sheaf of the complex
\mathcal{N}_{*}(\mathcal{X})
.

If
\mathcal{X}
is pointed, one defines the
reduced
n
-th
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homology sheaf
as the

\widetilde{H}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{X})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Ker(H_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{X})\to H_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\operatorname{Spec}k))

and attributed to the vanishing of homology sheaves of
\operatorname{Spec}k
in all non-zero dimensions, we have that

H_{*}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{X})=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\widetilde{H_{*}}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{X})

The following is formal of the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connectivity theorem (
[
160
, Corollary 6.31]
):

Corollary B.0.5
.

The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homology sheaves
H_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{X})
of a space
\mathcal{X}
vanish for
n<0
and are strictly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant sheaves for
n\geq 0
.

Corollary B.0.6
.

The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-localization functor commutes with the suspension in
D(\mathcal{A}b(k))
and so there exists a canonical suspension isomorphism for any pointed space
\mathcal{X}
and any integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
:

\widetilde{H}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{X})\cong\widetilde{H}_{n+1}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\Sigma_{s}\mathcal{X})

where
\Sigma_{s}\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=S^{1}\wedge\mathcal{X}
is the simplicial suspension of
\mathcal{X}
.

We end this section with one powerful application of these sheaves for
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-simply connected spaces that we use in Chapter 5.

Theorem B.0.7
.

Let
k
be any perfect field. Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
be a morphism between
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-simply connected pointed smooth
k
-schemes and let
d=\text{max}\{\dim\ X+1,Y\}
. If
f
induces an isomorphism
H_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{i}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(Y)
for all
2\leq i<d
and an epimorphism
H_{d}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(X)\to H_{d}^{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(Y)
,
f
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence in
Spc_{k}
.

Proof.

See
[
185
, Theorem 1.1, §3]
.
∎

B.0.2
The Milnor-Witt Sheaves

The Milnor-Witt sheaves are examples of strictly
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant sheaves. In this section, we will complement the background for the (
Section

2.2
) closely following
[
77
,
56
]
. We will begin with the background on Grothendieck-Witt ring, Witt ring, and fundamental ideals. We will also then define the Milnor
K
-theory and state Milnor’s conjecture. We will end with some additional knowledge in Milnor-Witt
K
-theory and Chow-Witt groups. In general, as far as the exposition here is concerned, the references will be
[
129
,
160
,
56
,
23
,
77
]
.

Grothendieck-Witt Ring

Definition 58
.

Let
F
be a field. An
inner product space

(E,\phi)
over
F
is a finite
F
-vector space
E
equipped with a bilinear form

\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}E\otimes_{F}E\to F

which is symmetric and non-degenerate
1
1

1

this means
E\to E^{\vee},\ x\mapsto\phi(x,-)
is an isomorphism
. A morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}(E,\phi)\to(F,\psi)
of inner spaces is a
F
-linear morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}E\to F
such that
\phi(f(v),f(w)=\psi(v,w)
. We define the
rank
of
(E,\phi)
to be its dimension of the vector space
E/F
.

The category of inner spaces admits direct sums
\oplus
and tensor products
\otimes
on its elements. Thus, the set of isomorphism classes of inner spaces, denoted by
I_{F}
, is a commutative monoid for
\oplus
and a commutative semi-ring
\oplus,\otimes
. The following definition using inner spaces is due to
[
149
]
.

Definition 59
.

The
Grothendieck-Witt ring
of field
F
is defined as the group completion of the monoid
(I_{F},\oplus)
, with products induced by the tensor product
\otimes
. The rank map
rk
defined above induces a ring homomorphism
rk\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{GW}(F)\to\mathbb{Z}
.

Alternatively, one also defines the
\operatorname{GW}(F)
to be the isomorphism classes of quadratic forms of
F
, exploiting the one-to-one correspondence between the symmetric bilinear forms and the theory of quadratic forms. But this will no longer hold whenever char
F=2
(cf.
[
56
, Remark 2.1.3]
). Nevertheless, the definitions based on the inner spaces are valid irrespective of the characteristic of
F
, and so we will adopt this approach for all the formulations in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory in our thesis. The
\operatorname{GW}(F)
can also be presented as explicit generators and relations (See
[
56
, Theorem 2.1.11]
). The
\operatorname{GW}(F)
can be presented as ring via an explicit element
\epsilon=-\langle-1\rangle
which provides
\operatorname{GW}(F)=\mathbb{Z}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-1)
.

Remark B.0.8
.

For an unit
u\in F^{\times}
, the inner space
F\otimes F\to F
defined by
(x,y)\mapsto u\cdot xy
is of rank 1. We denote its class in
\operatorname{GW}(F)
by the symbol
\langle u\rangle
. This gives a relation
\langle uv^{2}\rangle=\langle u\rangle
. If we have
n
-units,
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}
, we write
\langle u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\rangle=\langle u_{1}\rangle+\dots\langle u_{n}\rangle
.

Definition 60
.

A field
F
is said to be
quadratically closed
if every element of
F
is a square in
F
. Equivalently, every quadratic polynomial has a root in
F
. We denote the quadratic classes of
f
by
Q(F)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=F^{\times}/(F^{\times})^{2}
. For any field
F
, there is a canonical map
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Q(F)\to\operatorname{GW}(F)
defined by the relation in the above remark.

Proposition B.0.9
.

A field
F
is quadratically closed if and only if the map
rk\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{GW}(F)\to\mathbb{Z}
is a ring isomorphism.

Proof.

This is an easy consequence of the isomorphism of the map
\phi
above, see
[
129
, Proposition 3.1]
.
∎

Example B.0.10
.

The following are some of the first computations of Grothendieck-Witt rings.

•

If
F
is an algebraically closed field, then the rank map
rk\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{GW}(k)\to\mathbb{Z}
is an isomorphism. More generally,
rk
is an isomorphism whenever
(-1)
is a square in
k
(since they are quadratically closed).

•

Let
F=\mathbb{R}
, then
\operatorname{GW}(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
. Indeed, we know that the signature determines a quadratic form over a real vector space. Hence, any class
\sigma\in\operatorname{GW}(\mathbb{R})
can be uniquely written as
\sigma=p\cdot\langle 1\rangle+q\cdot\langle-1\rangle
with
rk(\sigma)=p+q
and the signature of
\sigma
is the pair
(p,q)
. Hence, the map
\operatorname{GW}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
defined by
\sigma\mapsto(p,q)
is an isomorphism.

•

Let
F=\mathbb{F}_{q}
be the finite field with
q=p^{n}
and
p
prime. Then we have that

\operatorname{GW}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}&q \text{even}\\
\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}&q \text{odd}\end{cases}

The Grothendieck Witt ring is intimately related to the Milnor-Witt ring by the following fact: for
a\in F^{\times}
, let us denote by
\langle a\rangle
the class of symmetric bilinear forms on
F
defined by
(X,Y)\mapsto aXY
. Then we have a fundamental statement due to
[
160
, Lemma 3.10]
.

Lemma B.0.11
.

The map

\displaystyle\operatorname{GW}(F)\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{0}(F)

\displaystyle\langle a\rangle\mapsto 1+\eta[a]

passes to a well-defined isomorphism.

Remark B.0.12
.

Due to this result, we will denote by
\langle a\rangle
the symbol
1+\eta[a]
of degree 0. For example, the relation 2 in
19
then can written as

[ab]=[a]+\langle a\rangle[b]=[b]+\langle b\rangle[a]

for any
a,b\in F^{\times}
. In particular, we have that
\langle b^{2}\rangle=1
yields
[ab^{2}]=[a]+[b^{2}]
. Moreover, the fourth relation becomes

\displaystyle\eta[-1]+2=\langle-1\rangle+1=\langle-1,1\rangle=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}h\quad\ \text{implies}

\displaystyle\eta h=0

(B.1)

Here,
h
is called the
hyperbolic form
on
F^{2}
.

The following element in
\operatorname{GW}(F)
is of paramount importance to describe the commutativity of
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(F)
.

Lemma B.0.13
.

Let
\epsilon\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=-\langle-1\rangle\in\operatorname{GW}(F)
. For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

n_{\epsilon}=\begin{cases}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle(-1)^{i-1}\rangle&if $n>0$.\\
0&if $n=0$.\\
\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{-n}\langle(-1)^{i-1}\rangle&if $n<0.$\end{cases}

Then the following holds.

1.

For any
a\in F^{\times}
, we have
[a,a]=[-1,a]=[a,-1]
and
[-a,a]=0

2.

For any
\alpha\in K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F)
and
\beta\in K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{m}(F)
, we have
\alpha\beta=\epsilon^{mn}\beta\alpha

3.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a\in F^{\times}
, we have
[a^{n}]=n_{\epsilon}[a]
.

Witt Ring

The
Witt ring
is defined as the quotient ring

\operatorname{W}(F)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{GW}(F)/(h).

The hyperbolic form
h
has a rank 2, the rank map
rk
induces a morphism of rings
\operatorname{W}(F)\to\mathbb{Z}/2
. The negative part of the Milnor-Witt
K
-theory is governed by the Witt ring as described by the following fact (
[
160
, Lemma 3.10]
):

Lemma B.0.14
.

For all
n\leq-1
and
a\in F^{\times}
, the morphism

\gamma_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F)\to\operatorname{W}(F)\quad\text{defined by}\quad\langle a\rangle\eta^{-n}\mapsto\langle a\rangle

is an isomorphism of abelian groups.

As a consequence, we have that for all
m,n\leq-1
, the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{m}(F)\times K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{m+n}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{W}(F)\times\operatorname{W}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{W}(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{m}\times\gamma_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{m+n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}

is commutative.

Fundamental ideal

The kernel of the rank map
\operatorname{Ker}(rk)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{I}
is called the
fundamental ideal
, which is additively generated by the classes of the form
\langle-1,a\rangle-h
in
\operatorname{GW}(F)
. In a similar vein, there is another rank map in the Witt theory,
rk\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{W}(F)\to\mathbb{Z}/2
, and we set
Ker(rk)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{I}(F)
, called (by abuse of naming)
Fundamental ideal
. The
\mathcal{I}(F)
is additively generated by the classes of
\langle-1,a\rangle
in
\operatorname{W}(F)
. For
n\leq 0
, one often sets
\mathcal{I}^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{W}(F)
and considers the quotient groups

\overline{\mathcal{I}}^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{I}^{n}(F)/\mathcal{I}^{n+1}(F)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
(by convention,
\overline{\mathcal{I}}^{n}=0
, if
n\leq-1
). The fundamental ideals are described conveniently using the Pfister forms. For any
u\in F^{\times}
, the Pfister form is defined as
\langle\langle u\rangle\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1-\langle u\rangle
.

Proposition B.0.15
.

A field
F
is quadratically closed if and only if the map
rk\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{W}(F)\to\mathbb{Z}/2
is a ring isomorphism.

Example B.0.16
.

The following are some simple examples of Witt rings and their fundamental ideals.

•

If
F
is algebraically closed field, then
\operatorname{W}(F)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
and so its fundamental ideal
\mathcal{I}(F)
is trivial,

•

If
F=\mathbb{R}
, then any quadratic form is equivalent to a diagonal form
\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\rangle=(a_{1}x_{1}^{2},\dots,a_{n}x_{n}^{2})
and hence, we have that the induced rank map
\operatorname{W}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{Z}
is an isomorphism. Consequently, the kernel consists of even forms, and so we have
\mathcal{I}(\mathbb{R})=2\mathbb{Z}
.

•

Let
F=\mathbb{F}_{q}
be the finite field with
q=p^{n}
, for some prime
p
. Then using the computation of
\operatorname{GW}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we have that,

I(\mathbb{F}_{q})\cong\begin{cases}0&q \text{even}\\
\mathbb{Z}/2&q \text{odd}\end{cases}

Consequently, we have that

\operatorname{W}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/2&q \text{even}\\
\mathbb{Z}/2[t]/(t-1)^{2}&q=1 \text{mod} 4\\
\mathbb{Z}/4&q=3 \text{mod} 4\end{cases}

The higher fundamental ideals all vanish,
I^{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=0
, for
n\geq 2
.

Milnor
K
-theory

To give more context, we will also briefly describe the Milnor
K
-theory (
[
151
]
). The Milnor
K
-theory
K^{\operatorname{M}}(F)
of field
F
is defined as the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded algebra generated by the symbols
\{a\}
in degree +1, for
a\in F^{\times}
modulo the relations:

(M1)

\{a,1-a\}=0
, for all
a\in F\backslash\{0,1\}
(Steinberg relation)

(M2)

\{ab\}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{a\}\cdot\{b\}=\{a\}+\{b\}

The primordial properties that one has are those that
K^{\operatorname{M}}_{0}(F)=\mathbb{Z}
and
K_{1}^{\operatorname{M}}(F)=F^{\times}
. There is a canonical map
K^{\operatorname{M}}_{n}(F)\to K_{n}(F)
from the Milnor
K
-theory to the algebraic
K
-theory due to Quillen is an isomorphism if
n\leq 2
(for
n=2
, this is due
[
144
]
).
The fundamental ideal
\mathcal{I}
defined above forms an important part of the study associated with Milnor’s conjecture (
[
151
, Question 4.3]
), now a theorem due to Kato
[
121
]
in characteristic 2 and Orlov-Vishik-Voevodsky
[
167
]
and
[
159
]
for all other cases. We will finish this section by stating the following beautiful result:

Theorem B.0.17
.

Let F be an arbitrary field. Then for any
n\geq 0
, the map
F^{\times}\to I(F)
,
u\mapsto\langle\langle u\rangle\rangle
induces a ring morphism :

\mu\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{M}}_{*}(F)/2K_{*}^{M}(F)\to\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{I}^{n}(F)/\mathcal{I}^{n+1}(F)

which is an isomorphism.

B.0.3
Residue morphisms in
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}

One of the quintessential tools nested with the Milnor-Witt
K
-theory is the
residue homomorphism,
which allows us to define the differential between the Rost-Schmid complex that will be defined in the sequel, which leads us to the definition of the Chow-Witt groups. Let
F
be a field and
v\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}F\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
be a discrete valuation with residue field
\kappa(v)
and valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{v}
and choose a uniformizing parameter
\pi=\pi_{v}
of
v
. Then one defines the following group homomorphism, called the
non-canonical residue morphism
due to
[
160
, Theorem 3.15]
:

Theorem B.0.18
.

There is a unique homomorphism of graded Abelian groups

\partial^{\pi}_{v}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(F)\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*-1}(\kappa(v))

of degree -1 such that
\partial_{v}^{\pi}
commutes wit the multiplication by
\eta
and

1.

\partial_{v}^{\pi}([\pi,u_{1},\dots,u_{n}])=[\bar{u_{1}},\dots,\bar{u_{n}}]

2.

\partial_{v}^{\pi}([u_{1},\dots,u_{n}])=0

We call such a
\partial_{v}^{\pi}
the
residue homomorphism
.

It turns out that this homomorphism
\partial_{v}^{\pi}
depends on the chosen valuation
\pi
(hence, the naming) as described below. This is the reason for
[
160
]
to introduce the theory of
twisted Milnor Witt
K
-theory
, which is the most suitable morphism to consider in this setup:

Proposition B.0.19
.

Let
\alpha\in K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(F)
and let
u\in\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}
. Then
\partial_{v}^{\pi}(\langle u\rangle\alpha)=\langle\bar{u}\rangle\partial_{v}^{\pi}(\alpha)
.

Proof.

This is
[
160
, Proposition 3.17]
.
∎

Remark B.0.20
.

(
[
77
, Remark 1.9]
)
The homomorphism
\partial_{v}^{\pi}
depends not only on the valuation
v
, but also on the choice of the uniformizing parameter
\pi
.

Proof.

Suppose
\pi^{\prime}=u\pi
, for some
u\in\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}
. Then we have that
\partial_{v}^{\pi}([\pi,-1])=[-1]
, essentially by construction. However, we see that

\begin{split}\partial_{v}^{\pi^{\prime}}([\pi,-1])&=\partial_{v}^{\pi^{\prime}}([u^{-1}\pi^{\prime},-1])\\
&=\partial_{v}^{\pi^{\prime}}([u^{-1},-1]+[\pi^{\prime},-1]+\eta[u^{-1},\pi^{\prime},-1])\\
&=\partial_{v}^{\pi^{\prime}}([u^{-1},-1]+[\pi^{\prime},-1]+\eta[\pi^{\prime},u^{-1},-1])\\
&=[-1]+\eta[u^{-1},-1]\\
&=\langle u^{-1}\rangle[-1].\end{split}

(B.2)

The class
[-1]\neq\langle u^{-1}\rangle[-1]
in general. For example, if
\kappa(v)=\mathbb{R}
, then the homomorphism
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{I}(\mathbb{R})
shows that
[-1]\neq\langle-1\rangle[-1]
.
∎

Twists in Milnor-Witt
K
-theory

We will now explain this theory of twists in the Milnor-Witt ring. The twists are generally considered with respect to an invertible sheaf, a.k.a line bundle (in full generality, one uses the graded line bundles: (see
[
77
, §1.3]
). We will now describe the twisted version of
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}
and, as a result, the twisted residue morphism
\partial_{v}^{\pi}
and convince ourselves that this modification indeed eliminates the choice of the uniformizer.

Let
V
be an
F
-vector space of rank 1 with
V^{\times}
comprising the non-zero elements of
V
. The group
V^{\times}
acts transitively on
F^{\times}
, and this gives the free Abelian group
\mathbb{Z}[V^{\times}]
a structure of a
\mathbb{Z}[F^{\times}]
-algebra. On the other hand, we know that
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(F)
is a
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{0}(F)
-algebra and there is a canonical map

F^{\times}\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{0}(F)\quad\text{defined by}\quad u\mapsto\langle u\rangle.

Thus, all the groups
K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F)
also inherits the structure of
\mathbb{Z}[F^{\times}]
-algebra. Now, we can conveniently set
the
Milnor-Witt
K
-theory twisted by
V
as

K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F,V)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[{F^{\times}}]}\mathbb{Z}[V^{\times}]

With this definition, we can now consider the
twisted residue homomorphism
(also called the
canonical residue map
) with respect to a line bundle
L
. The homomorphism

\partial_{v}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(F,L)\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*-1}(\kappa(v),(\mathfrak{m}_{v}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}_{v})^{*}\otimes L)

is defined by

\partial_{v}(\alpha\otimes l)=\partial_{v}^{\pi}(\alpha)\otimes\overline{\pi}^{*}\otimes l

for
l\in L
. Here,
(\mathfrak{m}_{v}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}_{v})^{*}
represents the relative tangent space, which, by definition, is the dual of the relative cotangent space
\mathfrak{m}_{v}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}_{v}
(seen as
\kappa(v)
-vector spaces).

Lemma B.0.21
.

[
77
, Lemma 1.19]

The homomorphism
\partial_{v}
is well-defined and is independent of the uniformizer
\pi
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we set the line bundle
L=\mathcal{O}_{v}
. The morphism
\partial_{v}^{\pi^{\prime}}
is well-defined as
\partial_{v}^{\pi}
is. Let
\pi^{\prime}
be another uniformizing parameter. Then there exists
u\in\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}
such that
u\pi=\pi^{\prime}
. As in
Remark

B.0.20
, we see that
\partial_{v}^{\pi}=\langle u\rangle\partial_{v}^{\pi^{\prime}}
. Since then we have that
\overline{\pi}^{*}=u^{-1}(\overline{\pi}^{\prime})^{*}
, we have that

p(\alpha)\otimes\overline{\pi}^{*}=\langle u\rangle\partial_{v}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\alpha)\otimes\overline{\pi}^{*}=\langle u\rangle\partial_{v}^{\pi^{\prime}}\otimes u^{-1}(\overline{\pi^{\prime}})^{*}=\partial_{v}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\alpha)\otimes(\overline{\pi^{\prime}})^{*}

∎

Geometric and Canonical Transfers

Similar to many other motivic theories, the Milnor-Witt
K
-theory also has
transfers
, colloquially called the
wrong way maps
in both untwisted (called the "geometric" transfer) and twisted (called the ’canonical’ transfer) settings. The fact that we have it in the latter again emphasizes the fact that the transfer in the non-canonical setting again depends on something that we wouldn’t want it to (choice of a section!).

In the untwisted setting, we have the following (
[
77
, Theorem 1.11]
) split short exact sequence: for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}

0\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F)\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F(t))\xrightarrow{\sum\partial_{p}^{p}}\bigoplus_{p}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n-1}(F(p))\to 0

where
p
runs over set of monic irreducible polynomials of
F[t]
and
F(p)=F[t]/(p)
. The left-hand homomorphism is induced by the field extension
F\subset F(t)
and the right-hand homomorphism is the sum of the residue homomorphisms associated to the
p
-adic valuation and the uniformizing parameter
p
. For any
\alpha\in K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F(t))
, one considers
[t]\alpha\in K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n+1}(F(t))
and use the residue map
\partial_{t}^{t}
. But one can also consider the other important valuation,
p=\infty
, that is,
v_{\infty}(f/g)=deg(g)-deg(f)
with uniformizing parameter
-1/t
. Indeed, this gives rise to the definition of
geometric transfers
, which are certain transfer maps with respect to valuation
\partial_{\infty}
in the untwisted Milnor-Witt
K
-theory. For for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the homomorphism

\partial_{\infty}^{1/t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F(t))\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n-1}(F)

helps us defines the following (
[
160
, §4.2]
): Let
p
be a monic irreducible polynomial in
F[t]
, then the composite

\tau_{F}^{F(p)}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n-1}(F(p))\subset\bigoplus_{p}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n-1}(F(p))\xrightarrow{s}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F(t))\xrightarrow{-\partial_{\infty}^{-1/t}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n-1}(F)

is called the
geometric transfer
, where
s
is any section of
\sum\partial_{p}^{p}
associated to the extension
F\subset F(p)
. The following lemma summarizes this story.

Lemma B.0.22
.

The geometric transfers
\tau_{F}^{F(p)}
are the unique homomorphisms which makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{K^{MW}_{n}}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{K^{MW}_{n}}(F(t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{p}\mathrm{K^{MW}_{n-1}}(F(p))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{K^{MW}_{n-1}}(F)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum\partial_{p}^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\partial_{\infty}^{-1/t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum\tau_{F}^{F(p)}}

commutative. In other words, there are unique homomorphisms
f_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n-1}(F(p))\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}n-1(F)
with
\sum(f_{p}\circ\partial_{p}^{p})+\partial_{\infty}^{-1/t}=0
.

But it turns out that these transfers depend on the chosen section
s
. This is the main reason for developing the theory of (canonical) transfers using the twisted residue morphism. We will now briefly touch upon this. The twists are with respect to the canonical sheaf of differential
\omega_{F/k}
for a finitely generated field extension
F/k
. Note that the
F[t]
-module
\Omega_{F[t]/k}
is free of rank equal to
\text{tr.deg}\ (F/k)+1
(if
k
is perfect). Now, given a monic polynomial
p\in F[t]
, consider the
p
-adic valuation to obtain twisted residue morphism

\partial_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(F(t),\det(\Omega_{F(t)/k}))\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*-1}(F(p),((\mathfrak{m}_{p})/(\mathfrak{m}_{p}^{2})^{*})\otimes_{F[t]}\det(\Omega_{F[t]/k}).

Then it follows that we have a split exact sequence as in the untwisted case (
[
76
, Proposition 1.20]
):

0\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F,\det(\Omega_{F/k}))\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n}(F(t),\det(\Omega_{F(t)/k}))\xrightarrow{d}\bigoplus_{p}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*-1}(F(p),\det(\Omega_{F(p)/k}))\to 0

where
d
is the total residue homomorphism taken over all
p
. Now for any monic irreducible polynomial
p
in
F[t]
, we have a homomorphism of the form

\mathrm{Tr}_{F}^{F(q)}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n-1}(F(p),\det(\Omega_{F(p)/k}))\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{n-1}(F,\det(\Omega_{F/k}))

called the
canonical transfer
. For field extensions
k\subset F\subset L
with
L/F
finite, this homomorphism is given by

\mathrm{Tr}_{F}^{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(L,\det(\Omega_{L/k})\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{*}(F,\det(\Omega_{F/k}))

B.0.4
The Rost-Schmid Complex and the Chow-Witt theory

As a result of twisted residue homomorphism and the canonical transfer, one can now define the
Rost-Schmid complexes
and thereby, the
Chow-Witt groups
.

Definition 61
.

Let
X
be a finite type, separated scheme over any field
k
, and let
L
be any line bundle. For any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, the Rost-Schmid complex
\tilde{C}(X,j,L)
in weight
j
is the complex (in homological dimension) whose term in degree
i
is given by

\tilde{C}_{i}(X,j,L)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(i)}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{j+i}(\kappa(x),\omega_{\kappa(y)/k}\otimes L)

One consequently verifies that for
x\in X_{(i)}
and
y\in X_{(i-1)}
, there exists a well-defined notion of differential

d^{x}_{y}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{j+1}(\kappa(x),\omega_{\kappa(x)/k}\otimes L)\to K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{j+i-1}(\kappa(y),\omega_{\kappa(y)/k}\otimes L)

defined using the residue homomorphism and the canonical transfer described above, and that we have

d_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\tilde{C}_{i}(X,j,L)\to\tilde{C}_{i-1}(X,j,L)

In all, the complex
(\tilde{C}(X,j,L)
is a (graded) Abelian group with a homomorphism
d
of degree -1 such that
d_{i-1}\cdot d_{i}=0
. This is called the
homological Rost-Schmid complex twisted by
L
and we write
H_{i}(X,j,L)
for the homology groups of this complex.

Now, let us consider the special case when
X
is smooth. In this case, we obtain the (graded) Abelian group whose component of degree
i
is of the form

C(X,j,L)^{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{x\in X^{(i)}}K^{\operatorname{MW}}_{j-i}(\kappa(x),(\omega_{\kappa(x)/k})^{-1}\otimes L)

and we have
\tilde{C}_{i}(X,j,(\omega_{\kappa(x)/k}^{-1}))=C(X,j+d_{X})^{d_{X}-i}
, where
d_{X}
is the dimension of
X
. It follows that we obtain a group homomorphism of (cochain) complex
C(X,j)
with differentials
d
, i.e.. a pair
(C(X,j),d)
of degree +1. We call this the
cohomological Rost-Schmid complex
, and we write
H^{i}(X,j,L)
for its cohomology groups.

Remark B.0.23
.

If
Y\subset X
is a closed subset of
X
, then one also considers subcomplexes supported on
Y
, and we denote them by
\tilde{C}_{Y}(X,j,L)
and
C_{Y}(X,j,L)
respectively. Furthermore, if
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\hookrightarrow X
is a closed subscheme of
X
, then we have the following identification

\tilde{C}(Y,j,i^{*}L)=\tilde{C}_{Y}(X,j,L).

(B.3)

Furthermore, these groups are also invariant under taking reduction of schemes, that is,
\tilde{C}(X,j,L)=\tilde{C}(X_{red},j,L)
. And one can also relax that
X
is essentially of finite type.

We are now all equipped to comprehend the definition of the Chow-Witt groups.

Definition 62
.

Let
X
be a separated scheme that is essentially of finite type over a field
k
. For any
i\in\mathbb{N}
and any line bundle
L
over
X
, the group

\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}_{i}(X,L)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H_{i}(X,-i,L)

is called the
homological Chow-Witt groups of
i
-dimensional cycles twisted by
L
. Other names include the homological
i
-th Chow-Witt groups, or simply Chow-Witt groups. In a similar vein, one also has

\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}^{i}(X,L)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H^{i}(X,i,L)

called the
cohomological Chow-Witt groups of
i
-dimensional cycles twisted by
L
or similarly, called as the homological
i
-th Chow-Witt groups, or simply Chow-Witt groups. If
Y\subset X
is a closed subset, then we also have the supported version

\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}^{i}_{Y}(X,L)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H^{i}(C_{Y}(X,i,L))

called the
cohomological Chow-Witt groups of
i
-dimensional cycles supported on
Y
and twisted by
L
or the Chow-Witt groups supported on
Y
.

Proposition B.0.24
.

We will list some of the basic properties in this theory following
[
77
, §2.2, 2.3, 2,4]
:

1.

If
i>\dim(X)
or
i<0
, then
\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}_{i}(X,L)=0
.

2.

If
X
is smooth of dimension
d_{X}
, then we have
\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}_{i}(X,\det(\Omega_{X/k})^{-1}\otimes L)\simeq\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}^{d_{X}-i}(X,L)
.

3.

For a proper morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
of finite type schemes, we have the pushforward (cf.
[
77
, Theorem 2.9]
)

f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\widetilde{C}(X,j,f^{*}L)\to\widetilde{C}(Y,j,L)

and if
f
is finite morphism of smooth
k
-schemes
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
with
d=\dim(Y)-\dim(X)
, we have

f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathrm{C(X,j,\det(\Omega_{X/k})\otimes f^{*}L)\to C(Y,j+d,\det(\Omega_{Y/k})\otimes L)}

4.

If
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
is a flat morphism of essentially smooth
k
-schemes, then we have pullbacks (cf.
[
77
, Theorem 2.12]
):

f^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C(X,j,L)\to C(Y,j,f^{*}L).

For smooth morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
of essentially smooth
k
-schemes of finite type with
d=\dim(Y)-\dim(X)
, we get (
[
77
, Theorem 2.14]
)

f^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\widetilde{C}(C,j,L)\to\widetilde{C}(Y,j-d,\det(\Omega_{Y/X})^{-1})\otimes f^{*}L)

5.

If
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\subset X
is a closed subscheme (with reduced structure on
Y
), then
\widetilde{C}(Y,j,\iota^{*}L)=\widetilde{C}_{Y}(X,j,L)
, for all
L
.

6.

Letting
u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\to X
be the complement open subscheme of
Y
, we have an exact sequence of complexes

0\to\widetilde{C}(Y,j,\iota^{*}L)\to\widetilde{C}(X,j,L)\to\widetilde{C}(U,j,u^{*}(L))\to 0

which induces a long exact sequence of Chow-Witt groups

..\cdots\to\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}_{i}(Y,\iota^{*}(L))\to\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}_{i}(X,L)\to\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}_{i}(U,u^{*}(L))\to\cdots..

In contrast with the classical Chow groups, the right-hand arrow
\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}(X,L)\to\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}(U,u^{*}(L))
is not surjective, in general.

7.

If
X
is a smooth scheme, then the pullback map turns the group
\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}_{i}(X,j,L)
into a ring structure with the ring product acquired via the exterior product, which then extends to a ring homomorphism of smooth schemes (
[
77
, Proposition 3.13.]
).

Appendix C
Miscellaneous

C.1
Scheme Theory and Motivic Topology at Infinity

This section comprises miscellaneous concepts from scheme theory put together. We will present a brief background on the motivic version of the topology at infinity machinery.

Weil restriction functor

Let
h\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S^{\prime}\to S
be a morphism of schemes. Then, for any
S^{\prime}
-scheme
X^{\prime}
, the contravariant functor

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{S/S^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{S})^{op}\to\operatorname{Set}

\displaystyle T\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{S^{\prime}}(T\times_{S}S^{\prime},X^{\prime})

is defined on the category
\mathrm{Sch}_{S}
of S-schemes. If it is representable, the corresponding
S
-scheme, again denoted by
\mathfrak{R}_{S^{\prime}/S}(X^{\prime})
is called the
Weil restriction
of
X^{\prime}
with respect to
h
. Thus, the latter is characterized by a functorial isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(T,\mathfrak{R}_{S^{\prime}/S}(X^{\prime}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{S^{\prime}}(T\times_{S}S^{\prime},X^{\prime})

of functors in
T
where
T
varies over all
S
-schemes.

Sheaf of relative differentials

The sheaf of relative differentials of a scheme is the algebraic analogy of Kähler differentials for rings and modules. For example, for a smooth variety
X
over
\mathbb{C}
, the sheaf of differentials is essentially the same as the dual of the tangent bundle defined in differential geometry. For the short exposition here, we will refer to
[
96
, Chapter 2, §8]
. Let us recall the classical definition. Let
A
be a ring (commutative, unital), let
B
be an
A
-algebra, and let
M
be a
B
-module.

Definition 63
.

The
module of relative differentials
of
B
over
A
is a
B
-module
\Omega_{B/A}
, together with an
A
-derivation
d\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}B\to\Omega_{B/A}
, which satisfies the following universal property: for any
B
-module
M
, and for any
A
-derivation
d^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}B\to M
, there exists a unique
B
-module homomorphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Omega_{B/A}\to M
such that
d^{\prime}=f\circ d
.

Example C.1.1
.

If
B=A[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
, then
\Omega_{B/A}
is the free
B
-module of rank
n
generated by
dx_{1},\dots,dx_{n}
.

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to Y
be a morphism of schemes. We consider the diagonal morphism
\Delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to X\times_{Y}X
with the closed subscheme
\Delta(X)\subset X\times_{Y}X
.

Definition 64
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be the sheaf of ideals of
\Delta(X)
. Then we define the

sheaf of relative differentials
of X over Y to be the sheaf

\Omega_{X/Y}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Delta^{*}(\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2})

in
X
. The sheaf
\Omega_{X/Y}
has a natural structure of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, which is a priori, quasi-coherent. If
Y
is Noetherian and
f
is of finite type, then
\Omega_{X/Y}
is a coherent sheaf on
X
.

Example C.1.2
.

If
X=\mathbb{A}^{n}
, then
\Omega_{X/Y}
is a free
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module of rank
n
, generated by the global sections
\{dx_{1},\dots,dx_{n}\}
, where
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
are affine coordinates for
\mathbb{A}^{n}
.

Definition 65
.

The
relative canonical sheaf

\omega_{X/Y}
is defined as the sheaf associated to
n
th-exterior product
\bigwedge^{n}\Omega_{X/Y}
, where
n=\dim(X)
. The canonical sheaf
\omega_{X/Y}
is an invertible sheaf on
X
.

Example C.1.3
.

For a smooth projective variety
X\to\operatorname{Spec}k
, the canonical sheaf produces a birational invariant called its
genus
,
\rho_{g}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\dim_{k}\Gamma(X,\omega_{X})
. From the adjunction formula, one finds that
\omega_{\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}(-n-1)
. Since
\mathcal{O}(l)
has
no global sections for
l<0
, we find that
\rho_{g}(\mathbb{P}^{n})=0
for any
n\geq 1
. Thus, if
Y
is any rational variety, then
\rho_{g}(Y)=0
.

\mathbb{G}_{a}
-actions and Locally Nilpotent Derivations

Let
k
be a field of characteristic zero. Then for a
k
-algebra
B
, there is a bijection between the set of
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-actions on
B
and the locally nilpotent derivations on
B
(
[
78
, §1.5]
). Let
X=\operatorname{Spec}B
be the corresponding affine variety. Given
\partial\in\operatorname{LND}(B)
, then we have a group homomorphism

\displaystyle\eta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}(\ker\partial,+)\to\operatorname{Aut}_{k}(B)

\displaystyle f\mapsto\exp(t\partial)(f)

where
\exp
is the exponential map

exp(t\partial)(f)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1+({\partial f})t+\frac{(\partial^{2}f)t^{2}}{2!}+\dots+\frac{(\partial^{n}f)t^{n}}{n!}+\dots.

In addition, if
\partial=0
, then
\eta
is injective. Restricting
\eta
to the subgroup
\mathbb{G}_{a}=(k,+)
, we obtain the algebraic representation
\eta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{G}_{a}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}_{k}(B)
. Geometrically, this means that
\partial
induces the faithful algebraic
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action
exp(t\partial)
on
X
(
t\in k
). Conversely, let
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{G}_{a}\times X\to X
be an algebraic
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action over
k
. Then
\rho
induces a derivation
\rho^{\prime}(0)
, where differentiation takes places relative to
t\in\mathbb{G}_{a}
.

The following lemma is useful for testing the smoothness of a scheme via fibers.

Lemma C.1.4
.

Let
S
be any Noetherian scheme of finite type. Then for any affine
S
-scheme
X
, we have that the canonical morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
is an affine morphism which is locally of finite presentation. Moreover, we have that
f
is smooth if all the fibres of
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
are smooth.

Proof.

This follows from
[
190
,
Lemma 01SG
]
and
[
190
,
Lemma 01V8
]
.
∎

C.2
Motivic Topology at Infinity

The
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractibility is a delicate notion as compared to its topological counterpart. To emphasize this further, we know in topology it is always true that if a space
X
is contractible, then every vector bundle over
F\to E\to X
is isomorphic to a trivial bundle
E\cong X\times F
, where
F
is the fiber. But this is in no sense straightforward in algebraic geometry. In particular, there are examples of smooth schemes that carry trivial vector bundles but are not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible. On the flip side, there are examples of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes that carry non-trivial bundles (see the beautiful work by
[
12
]
); however, all such examples known so far are strictly quasi-affine schemes, and this wilderness can be contained by restricting the question to the affine realm. Altogether, these instances only reiterate the fact that there is so far no universal invariant that can uniquely identify the affine spaces among all the smooth affine
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes.

In connection with the motivic open Poincaré Conjecture (cf.
Section

1.2
), one approach is to develop a
motivic topology at infinity
. Following the footsteps of topology, it seems instructive to first develop a notion of "motivic homology at infinity". This was proposed by
[
213
]
under the name of
boundary motive
, which takes place in Voevodsky’s derived category of motives
\operatorname{DM}_{Nis}^{eff,-}(\operatorname{Spec}k,\mathbb{Z})
.

Motivic homology at infinity

Let us quickly recall Voevodsky’s derived category of motives over
k
following
[
201
, Chapter 5, §2.1]
. Fix a perfect field
k
and consider the category of correspondences
\operatorname{SmCor}(k)
whose objects are smooth schemes
X,Y\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
. A morphism
Y\to X
in
\operatorname{SmCor}(k)
is a
finite correspondence
: the free Abelian group generated by integral closed subschemes of the following kind:

\operatorname{Cor}(Y,X)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{Z}\{Z\subset Y\times_{k}X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Z\to Y\ \text{is finite, surjective}\}\subset Y\times X.

The composition law in this category is given by the natural pullback of schemes. The category
\operatorname{Shv}_{Nis}(\operatorname{SmCor}(k))
of
Nisnevich sheaves with transfer
is the category of those contravariant additive functors from
\operatorname{SmCor}(k)
to Abelian groups, whose restriction to
\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
is a sheaf for the Nisnevich topology.
\operatorname{Shv}_{Nis}(\operatorname{SmCor}(k))
is an Abelian category and so contains the derived category
D^{-}(\operatorname{Shv}_{Nis}(\operatorname{SmCor}(k)))
of complexes bounded above. This in turn contains the full subcategory of
effective motivic complexes

\operatorname{DM}_{-}^{eff}(\operatorname{Shv}_{Nis}(\operatorname{SmCor}(k)))
over
k
. The latter is the one whose cohomology sheaves are homotopy invariant (
[
201
, Definition 3.1.10]
).

There is another triangulated category
\operatorname{DM}_{gm}^{eff}(k)
called the
effective geometrical motives
(see
[
201
, Chapter 5, §2.1]
). It has a canonical full triangulated embedding of
\operatorname{DM}_{gm}^{eff}(k)
into
\operatorname{DM}_{-}^{eff}(k)
, mapping the geometrical motive of
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
to
M_{gm}(X)
. Via this embedding, one identifies
M_{gm}(X)=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}M(X)
as an object of
\operatorname{DM}_{gm}^{eff}(k)
. The category of
geometric motives

\operatorname{DM}_{gm}(k)
is the one obtained by inverting the Tate motive
\mathbb{Z}(1)
in
\operatorname{DM}_{gm}^{eff}(k)
(
[
201
, P. 192]
). This latter category is also called the
Voevodsky’s derived category of mixed motives
, denoted
\operatorname{DM}(\operatorname{Spec}k,\mathbb{Z})
.

The following version of the boundary motive suffices for our context. For a precise definition, one can refer to
[
213
, Definition 2.1]
.

Definition 66
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Sm}_{k}
be a smooth scheme over a perfect field
k
. Then the
boundary motive

\partial M_{gm}(X)
can be defined as the one that fits into the following exact triangle

\partial M_{gm}(X)\to M_{gm}(X)\to M_{gm}^{c}(X)\to\partial M_{gm}(X)[1]

where
M_{gm}(X)
denotes the motive of
X
and
M_{gm}^{c}(X)
its motive with compact support in
\operatorname{DM}_{-}^{eff}(\operatorname{Spec}k)
.

In particular, there are chain complexes
C_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X)
and
C_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}^{c}(X)
giving rise to the corresponding categories of motive
M(X)
and compactly supported motive
M^{c}(X)
of
X
as objects in
DM_{Nis}^{eff,-}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. This is obtained in analogy with "singular chain complex at infinity" as in topology (
2
). Then one has a canonical map
\iota_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X)\to C_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}^{c}(X)
induced from the underlying morphism of presheaves with transfers
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}^{c}(X)
. Then one defines the
motive at infinity
, denoted
M^{\infty}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=M_{gm}^{\infty}(X)
, as the motive associated with the complex
C_{*}(\text{coker}(\iota_{X})[-1])
. Given this setup, we have the following result that strikes an analogy with that of topology
Example

1.1.4
:

Lemma C.2.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{X}
is an
m
-dimensional
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible finite type smooth scheme. Then we have

M^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})\simeq\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}(m)[2m-1]

(non-canonically) as objects in
\operatorname{DM}(\operatorname{Spec}k,\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

See
[
11
, Lemma 6.5]
.
∎

Motivic Homotopy at Infinity

Put differently, the motivic homology at infinity of
\mathcal{X}
is that of the motivic homology of the sphere of appropriate dimension. But to fully distinguish between
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes, it is indispensable to have the more powerful theory of homotopy at infinity. The existence of homotopy purity allows one to do so, at least in the stable
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category
\mathrm{SH}(k)
. This was first described by
[
133
]
. In addition to this, by developing the theory of punctured tubular neighborhoods in stable motivic homotopy theory, the authors in
[
53
]
have recently established a candidate for stable motivic homotopy at infinity that is in alignment with that of topology.

Definition 67
.

([ibid, Eq. 1.1.0.a])
Let
S
be a quasi-compact, quasi-separated base scheme. Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
be any separated scheme of finite type. Then the
stable motivic homotopy type at infinity
is defined as the homotopy exact sequence

\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X)\to f_{!}f^{!}(\textbf{1}_{S})\xrightarrow{\alpha_{X}}f_{*}f^{!}(\textbf{1}_{S}).

Here
\textbf{1}_{S}
is the motivic sphere spectrum over
S
,
f_{!}f^{!}\textbf{1}_{S}=\Pi_{S}(X)
is the stable homotopy type of
X
and
f_{*}f^{!}(\textbf{1}_{S})=\Pi_{S}^{c}(X)
is the properly supported stable homotopy type of
X
. The canonical morphism
\alpha_{X}
is obtained from the six-functor formalism for the stable motivic homotopy category
\mathrm{SH}(S)
. The following fundamental properties show that this definition is a canonical choice.

•

If
f
is smooth, then
f_{!}f^{!}(\textbf{1}_{S})=\Sigma^{\infty}X_{+}
retrieves back the motivic suspension spectrum of
X
,

•

If
f
is proper, then
\alpha_{X}
is an isomorphism (as
f_{*}\cong f_{!}
)

•

The morphism
\alpha_{X}
is covariant with respect to proper morphisms and contravariant with respect to étale morphisms.

For a morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to S
as above, recall that a
compactification
is given by a system of open immersions
j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\hookrightarrow\widetilde{X}
into a proper
S
-scheme
\widetilde{f}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\widetilde{X}\to S
. The closed complement, known as the
boundary
, is given by
\partial X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\widetilde{X}\backslash X)_{red}
of
\widetilde{X}
and comes equipped with closed immersion
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\partial X\hookrightarrow X
. Moreover by setting,
\partial f=\widetilde{f}\circ i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\partial X\to S
, we get the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial f}

This system gives rise to the following definition:

Definition 68
.

([ibid, Definition 4.1.1])
Let
(X,Z)
be a closed
S
-pair and let
v
be a (virtual) vector bundle on
X
. Then the
punctured tubular neighbourhood
of
Z
in
X
relative to
S
twisted by
v
,
\operatorname{TN}_{S}^{\times}(X,Z,v)
is the homotopy fiber in
\mathrm{SH}(k)
of the composite

\beta_{X,Z}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Pi_{S}(Z,i^{-1}v)\to\Pi_{S}(X,v)\to\Pi_{S}(X/X-Z,v)

Among several other properties of
\operatorname{TN}_{S}^{\times}
, they prove that it coincides with that of stable homotopy at infinity (
ibid
[Proposition 4.4.2]):

Proposition C.2.2
.

Let
(\widetilde{X},\partial X)
be the closed
S
-pair associated with a compactification of a separated
S
-scheme of finite type. Then there exists a canonical isomorphism

\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X)\simeq\operatorname{TN}_{S}^{\times}(\widetilde{X},\partial X)

Moreover, this isomorphism is in fact independent of the choice of compactification ([
ibid
, Corollary 4.4.4]):

Corollary C.2.3
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a closed
S
-pair such that
X/S
is proper. Then the punctured tubular neighborhood
\operatorname{TN}_{S}^{\times}(X,Z)
is isomorphic to
\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X\backslash Z)
and hence only depends on the open subscheme
X\backslash Z
.

The authors have furthermore computed the stable motivic homotopy at infinity of several algebraic varieties, including the Du Val singularities on normal surfaces (
ibid
[§5.4, Example 1]) and the Danielewski surfaces (
ibid
[§5.4, Example 2]), among others. In principle,
\Pi_{S}^{\infty}
can be seen to measure the formal difference between
\Pi_{S}(X)
and its compact counterpart
\Pi_{S}^{c}(X)
. In unison with the context of our script, let us end with the following example (
ibid
[Example 4.3.7]).

Example C.2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a stably
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible variety of dimension
d
over a field
k
. Then the stable motivic homotopy at infinity of
\mathcal{X}
is seen to be that of the affine
d
-space, i.e.,

\Pi_{k}^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})=\textbf{1}_{k}\oplus\textbf{1}_{k}(d)[2d-1]=\Pi_{k}^{\infty}(\mathbb{A}^{d}_{k}).

Thus, the stable motivic homotopy at infinity cannot distinguish between a stably
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible variety and
\mathbb{A}^{d}_{k}
. Moreover, recall from topology that taking the product with Euclidean spaces (or any contractible space) kills off the homotopy at infinity. This is also true for
\Pi_{S}^{\infty}
as the following illustrates (cf.
ibid
[Proposition 4.3.9]):

Example C.2.5
.

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to S
be a smooth stably
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-contractible
S
-scheme of relative dimension
n
with a trivial relative tangent bundle (e.g., take
Y=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
), then we have the following splitting of extension

\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X\times Y)\simeq\Pi_{S}(X)\oplus\Pi_{S}^{c}(X)(n)[2n-1].

The authors in
[
53
]
furthermore believe that an unstable version of motivic homotopy at infinity should resolve this issue at stake. However, there are currently several difficulties in defining a candidate for the
unstable motivic homotopy at infinity
; for instance, a coherent way to choose a base point at infinity or to formulate the "right definition" of motivic homotopy at infinity itself: since unstably, the homotopy fibre and the homotopy cofibre are generally different, it is unclear what should be the right notion to begin with.

So far, it is only a dream to have such a distinguished motivic invariant in play; nevertheless, this dream too shall become a reality!
